Pending that verification, "The Romance of Celes" should NOT be treated as evidence, in either direction, for or against the Lahontan-Liahona thesis: it is very likely a post-1830 (and quite possibly post-1838) Spalding-family literary curiosity, unrelated to the 1816-deceased Solomon Spalding at the center of the classic authorship theory, and therefore not a viable pre-Book-of-Mormon source text of any kind — Lahontan-related or otherwise.
What it IS useful for is exactly the point developed in Part XIX: a concrete, documented instance of the Spalding-name-conflation risk this whole research tradition has to watch for. Any future claim built on "a Spalding manuscript says X" needs its specific manuscript, specific author, and specific date independently verified before being used as evidence — this item is a clean illustration of why that check cannot be skipped.
# PART XXIII — BROADER SEARCH FOR LAHONTAN ECHOES IN NEWSPAPERS, PLAYS, AND OTHER WORKS: AN INCONCLUSIVE RESULT THIS SESSION
Elliott asked whether other works — newspapers, plays, or otherwise — might reference or echo Lahontan beyond what this dossier has already documented (the Dialogues with Adario's influence chain into Gulliver's Travels, Rousseau, Voltaire, and Diderot; the reception/criticism trail in Part XVI). This session's general web searches did not turn up either of two specific things: (a) confirmed 1790s–1830s American newspaper or periodical reprints, excerpts, or reviews of Lahontan's New Voyages, or (b) any stage or theatrical adaptation of the Dialogues with Adario or the Long River narrative specifically.
What these searches DID reinforce is the existing literary-critical treatment of the Dialogues (the Prose Studies article "The Importance of the Literary: Lahontan's Dialogues and Primitivist Thought," and the secondary treatments of David Hayne's DCB entry already cited in Part XVI) — but nothing beyond what is already documented there.
This should be read honestly as an inconclusive, not a negative, result. General web search does not reach the proprietary and specialized databases where 1790s–1830s American periodical content actually lives — Early American Imprints (Evans/Shaw-Shoemaker), the American Periodical Series, Chronicling America, and NYSL City Readers all remain unsearched with the Part VI orthographic strategy (period spelling variants, long-s handling). Given that Lahontan's book is independently confirmed to have circulated in elite institutional libraries (Jefferson's, the Library Company of Philadelphia), it is entirely plausible that periodical excerpts or reviews exist and simply have not been located yet by the tools available this session. This is now an explicit, separately-flagged Priority 2 task in Part XV, distinct from (and complementary to) the general orthographic search strategy already directed at book-length sources.
# PART XXIV — THE SEA OF THE WEST AND THE "NARROW NECK OF LAND": AN HONEST, PARTIAL PARALLEL [NEW — CARTOGRAPHY DEEP DIVE]
Elliott asked where else the cartographic parallels could be pushed. The most obvious next candidate is the Book of Mormon's "narrow neck of land" (Alma 22:32; Ether 10:20) — one of the text's most structurally load-bearing geographic features, dividing "land northward" from "land southward" and, in Ether 10:20, described as a place "by which the sea divides the land." Given that Lahontan's Long River material fed directly into the "Sea of the West" cartographic tradition (Part II.1), it is natural to ask whether that tradition included anything resembling a narrow neck.
The honest answer, based on this session's research: not cleanly. The Sea of the West, across the roughly eight cartographic archetypes catalogued by researcher R. McGuirk (239 period maps surveyed), was consistently depicted as an inland body of water connected to the Pacific Ocean by at least one STRAIT — that is, a narrow passage of WATER linking two seas, not a narrow neck of LAND separating them. Some of the earliest (1600s) versions went further, showing "branches of the Pacific Ocean protruding deeply into the North American continent," in one case coming within a few hundred miles of the eastern seaboard. Structurally, this is the INVERSE of the Book of Mormon's narrow neck: the Book of Mormon's feature is a thin strip of LAND that a traveler could cross in a day and a half (Alma 22:32) separating two seas; the Sea of the West's defining feature is a thin channel of WATER connecting two seas. This dossier should say so plainly rather than rounding the resemblance up — see the new Red Team Objection 11 and Blind Spot R.
What, if anything, survives this honest correction: the broader cartographic HABIT the Sea of the West exemplifies — resolving an unexplored, unmapped edge of the known world with a single, decisive, thin geographic feature (a strait, a neck, a narrows) rather than leaving it vague — is common to both. A period mapmaker's and a period-influenced author's shared instinct to give unmapped terrain one clean, narrow, nameable dividing feature is a real and interesting parallel at the level of cartographic imagination, even though the specific feature (water vs. land) does not match. This is considerably weaker than the direct river/name arguments elsewhere in this dossier, and should be presented that way: as an interesting resonance in HOW period geography-makers thought about unexplored space, not as a specific borrowed detail.
On chronology: the Sea of the West was decisively disproven by James Cook's 1778 voyage and George Vancouver's detailed 1791–92 surveys, with new depictions ending by roughly 1790 and only isolated stragglers persisting to about 1810. This means the concept was likely already fading from serious cartographic use well before Joseph Smith's birth (1805) and further still before Book of Mormon composition (1828–29) — a real chronological strain on any version of this argument that leans on the Sea of the West specifically (as opposed to the "Sea of the West"-descended, more general convention of bounding unexplored land with hypothesized seas at every edge, which is the version Part II.1/XX actually relies on, and which does not require the Sea of the West concept itself to still be current in 1829).
Task: confirm via Map 6 (Part XIII) whether, once actually drawn side by side, the strait-vs-neck distinction reads as decisive as it does in prose, or whether the general "thin decisive dividing feature" resonance is visually persuasive enough to be worth keeping in the working paper as a soft, clearly-labeled aside.
# PART XXV — LAHONTAN'S REAL GREAT LAKES CARTOGRAPHY AND THE "GREAT LAKES MODEL" OF BOOK OF MORMON GEOGRAPHY: A NEW, MORE PROMISING SYNTHESIS [NEW — CARTOGRAPHY DEEP DIVE]
Where Part XXIV is an honest near-miss, this section is a more promising thread. It rests on a fact already noted in passing in this dossier but not yet exploited: Lahontan was not uniformly a fabulist. His Long River and "Sea of the West" material is fictional or badly garbled, but his Great Lakes cartography was genuinely good. Geographicus's assessment of his 1715 "Carte Generale de Canada" states it is "considered to be the most factual and informative" of his maps, calling its depiction of the Great Lakes "primitive though surprisingly superior to a number of later maps of the region," and notes the map's "excellent illustration of the regional river systems" was praised by the historian Kershaw for its usefulness in understanding the French fur-trade river networks. In other words: the same explorer, the same volume, the same set of maps that gave 18th-century readers a wildly fictional western interior gave them a genuinely reliable eastern one — the Great Lakes and their connecting rivers.
This matters because an entirely separate, existing Book of Mormon geography proposal — the "Great Lakes model," surveyed by FAIR — places Nephite geography directly on top of this same real terrain: the narrow neck of land at the Niagara Peninsula (an approximately 3-mile-wide point between Lake Erie and Lake Ontario at its narrowest), the four seas assigned to Lake Erie/Huron (west), Lake Cayuga in the Finger Lakes (east), southern Lake Erie (south), and Georgian Bay (north), and the River Sidon correlated with the Mississippi. This model already has a serious internal critic (John Clark, cited via FAIR's summary), who argues it requires awkward duplication of place names and "two lands northward" — a real, unresolved textual problem with the model on its own terms, independent of anything to do with Lahontan.
The synthesis: nobody in either the critical or apologetic literature checked this session has connected the Great Lakes model's real-world geography to the fact that Lahontan himself was one of the earliest and most historically credited cartographers of exactly that same terrain. This is a different, arguably stronger kind of parallel than the Sea of the West comparison in Part XXIV, precisely because it does not require any of Lahontan's FICTIONAL material to be true or influential — it only requires that accurate, widely-praised, historically confirmed Great Lakes cartography (of the kind Lahontan helped establish, alongside contemporaneous Jesuit missionary maps) was circulating in the same general cartographic tradition that later authors, mapmakers, and geography-literate readers in the Rigdon/Cowdery/Smith milieu would plausibly have encountered. If Rigdon, Cowdery, or anyone in their circles had any exposure at all to Great Lakes-region maps or geographic description (a far more mundane and plausible claim than exposure to Lahontan's specific, obscure western fabrications), that exposure sits on cartographic ground Lahontan helped map.
This should be stated carefully: it is a claim about SHARED GEOGRAPHIC GROUND, not about direct borrowing, and it does not by itself explain why the Great Lakes model's proponents chose that terrain — that choice may simply reflect their own independent reading of Book of Mormon geography against a modern map, with no historical-cartography input at all. That specific question (did the Great Lakes model's originator have any exposure to historical Great Lakes cartography, Lahontan's or otherwise) is untraced and flagged as a new Priority 2 task. What can be said now is only that the terrain the Great Lakes model needs — an accurately mapped, narrow, lake-and-river-dominated landscape — is the same terrain Lahontan is independently, non-controversially credited with helping put on the map.
Task: build Map 7 (Part XIII) — a direct overlay of Lahontan's 1715 Great Lakes map against the Great Lakes model's geography, to see how precisely the historical and the proposed-Nephite features actually align once drawn together, and to check whether Lahontan's map specifically labels or depicts the Niagara Peninsula narrow point in a way worth quoting directly.
# PART XXVI — JEDIDIAH MORSE'S SCHOOLBOOK: THE HIGHEST-VALUE UNCHECKED ACCESS VECTOR [NEW — CARTOGRAPHY DEEP DIVE]
The single most valuable open thread to come out of this session's cartographic exploration is not a new parallel but a new, more promising ACCESS CHAIN. Every argument in this dossier about Lahontan's circulation has so far relied on elite institutional libraries (Jefferson's, the Library Company of Philadelphia, the hoped-for Dartmouth catalog) — real, but a step removed from Joseph Smith, Rigdon, or Cowdery personally, and requiring an inferential "would have had access" argument each time (exactly the kind of necessary-requirement link flagged in Part XIX).
Jedidiah Morse's American Universal Geography (also published as The American Geography), first issued in 1789 with numerous revised editions running into the 1820s, was THE standard early-American geography textbook — used widely in American schools, including in exactly the New England and upstate New York milieu Joseph Smith, Oliver Cowdery, and their families grew up in. A schoolbook is a categorically different, and much stronger, kind of access claim than an elite private or institutional library holding: it does not require special status, wealth, or scholarly connections, only ordinary schooling.
This session was not able to confirm whether Morse's Geography discusses Lahontan by name or presents the "Sea of the West" hypothesis to its young readers — general web search did not surface a direct citation, and the full text (multiple editions are on archive.org and the Evans Early American Imprint Collection) was not read directly this session. This is now the single highest-value unchecked task in the entire dossier: if a widely-used, personally-plausible schoolbook Joseph Smith or Oliver Cowdery could very plausibly have actually read as children taught either (a) Lahontan's account directly, or (b) the "Sea of the West"/sea-bounded-continent cosmology generally, that would meaningfully strengthen the access-chain leg of this whole thesis in a way no library-holdings argument can, because it removes an entire inferential step. Conversely, if Morse's Geography is checked and contains neither, that is also valuable information — it would suggest the "Sea of the West" cosmology, even if once schoolbook material, had likely faded from ordinary American education by the 1820s (consistent with the Cook/Vancouver-driven decline noted in Part XXIV), pushing this dossier back toward its existing library-access and ambient-diffusion arguments rather than a clean schoolbook citation.
Task: read Morse's American Universal Geography directly (starting with the most relevant editions — likely 1793, 1805, and any 1810s–1820s revision closest to Joseph Smith's and Oliver Cowdery's actual school years) and search specifically for "Lahontan," "Sea of the West," "Western Sea," and general descriptions of the unexplored trans-Mississippi or trans-Missouri interior. This is now the top Priority 1 archival task in Part XV.# "Sea North" and "Sea South": Cardinal-Direction Sea Naming in the Book of Mormon and Early North American Cartography
## TL;DR
- The four-directional-sea formula appears in its full form only once in the Book of Mormon (Helaman 3:8: "from the sea south to the sea north, from the sea west to the sea east"); "sea north" and "sea south" occur essentially nowhere else as named bodies, while "sea west/east" recur roughly a dozen times — an asymmetry that Hebrew idiom (yam = "sea/west," rarely "south," never "north") explains for west and marginally for south but leaves "sea north" without any Semitic basis.
- The best-fitting source model is not Lahontan's text directly but the early-19th-century Anglo-American hemispheric geography that read the New World as North and South America bounded by four oceans — a framing matched almost verbatim in Ethan Smith's *View of the Hebrews* (1st ed. 1823; 2nd ed. 1825) and adopted in Orson Pratt's own 1879 footnotes to Helaman 3:8; period cartographic conventions (Pacific = "South Sea," Atlantic/Caribbean = "North Sea"/Mer du Nord, the fictional "Sea of the West") supply a live conventional backdrop but map onto the text only partially or invertedly.
- Two prominent negative findings weaken a naive Lahontan/cartographic thesis for this specific feature: (1) Lahontan's own 1703 prose does not name any directional sea — his "Sea of the West" is a downstream cartographic invention, not his text; and (2) Jedidiah Morse's *American Universal Geography* — the flagship American schoolbook of Joseph Smith's youth — uses "Pacific Ocean" and "Atlantic," not "South Sea"/"North Sea"/"Sea of the West," so directional naming was already receding from formal American geography by 1800–1830.
## Key Findings
**1. Textual distribution is the load-bearing datum.** Helaman 3:8 is the sole verse deploying all four directional seas as a coordinated set. Its structural twin by the same narrator, Helaman 11:20, drops the north and south seas entirely ("from the sea west to the sea east"), and every concrete, plot-level reference to a sea in the war chapters is to the sea east or sea west only (e.g., Alma 50:8, 34; 52:11–13; 53:8, 22; 63:5). "Sea north" and "sea south" never anchor a described journey, battle, or city. LDS archaeologist John E. Clark concedes the point bluntly in "A Key for Evaluating Nephite Geographies," *FARMS Review* Vol. 1, Issue 1 (1989), p. 65 (a review of F. Richard Hauck's *Deciphering the Geography of the Book of Mormon*): "I am convinced that the reference to a north sea and a south sea is devoid of any concrete geographical content… Any geography that tries to accommodate a north and south sea, I think, is doomed to fail."
**2. Word order.** The text uses both the noun-adjective order ("sea east," "sea west," "sea south," "sea north" — unusual in English) and the ordinary adjective-noun order ("east sea," "west sea"). Apologists read the noun-adjective construct as a Hebraism (construct-state calque). I found no evidence in the available Skousen Critical Text materials of a manuscript variant in these specific phrases; the noun-adjective order is consistent both with the Book of Mormon's broader pseudo-biblical register and with a Hebraizing construction, so it does not by itself adjudicate authorship. (Skousen's *Analysis of Textual Variants* entries for Alma 22, Alma 50, and Helaman 3 should be checked on page images to confirm no variant exists.)
**3. Hebrew/ANE comparanda confirm the asymmetry.** Standard lexicons (Strong's H3220) define *yam* as "sea… locally, the west, or (rarely) the south." Hebrew's cardinal system is *qedem/mizrach* (east/front), *yam/ma'arav* (west/"sea"/rear), *negev/teman* (south/right), *tsaphon* (north/left). There is therefore a mainstream, non-Lahontan explanation for "sea west" (and, very marginally, "sea south"), but **no Hebrew idiom whatsoever yields "sea north."** This is the genuinely unresolved residue.
**4. There is no authentic ancient "four seas" precedent.** Contrary to the apologetic defense (Clark, Hamblin, Gardner) that Helaman 3:8 echoes an ancient cosmology of four bounding seas, the primary evidence shows ancient Near Eastern and classical cosmologies used a *single* encircling ocean, not four directional seas: the Babylonian "Map of the World" (BM 92687) depicts one ring-shaped *marratu* ("bitter river"); Homer, Hesiod, and Strabo describe a single Oceanus; and the Hebrew Bible has no world-ocean-surrounding-land motif at all. The Mesopotamian "King of the Four Corners/Quarters" (*šar kibrāt erbetti*) title concerns four quarters of *land* and four rivers — not four named seas. This is a strong negative finding for apologetics and, by elimination, points toward a modern (hemispheric) rather than ancient origin for the four-seas image.
**5. Period cartographic directional-sea conventions were real but map onto the text only partially.** Three of four directional sea-names were live conventions in the 17th–18th centuries: (a) the Pacific as "South Sea" (*Mar del Sur*, from Balboa 1513), which persisted "well into the nineteenth century" (Melville, Poe, and Stevenson all used "South Seas" rather than "Pacific" in titles); (b) the Atlantic/Caribbean as "North Sea" (*Mar del Norte*) — as Wikipedia's North Sea disambiguation summarizes, "from the 16th century, Europeans visiting the region distinguished the 'South Sea' (the Pacific Ocean south of the isthmus of Panama) from the 'North Sea' (the Caribbean Sea north of the same isthmus)," a usage dominant on 16th–17th-century maps but archaic by 1800; and (c) the fictional "Sea of the West"/*Mer de l'Ouest*. That cartographic myth originated with Guillaume Delisle, Royal Geographer to the King of France, who drew it on manuscript maps between 1695 and 1700 (drawing on the 1669 Jesuit Relations and Juan de Fuca's report); the first printed map to show it was Jean-Baptiste Nolin's *Le Globe Terrestre Represente En Deux Plans-Hemispheres* (Paris, 1700). Don McGuirk's cartobibliography *The Last Great Cartographic Myth: Mer de l'Ouest* "lists 242 maps (many in multiple states)"; the myth appeared on "over 200 printed maps between 1700 and 1810" (Map Myths Blog), fed partly by Lahontan's river/salt-lake reports and disproven by Cook (1778) and Vancouver (1791–92).
**6. The hemispheric four-ocean reading is the closest pre-1830 parallel.** Ethan Smith's *View of the Hebrews* (1st ed. Poultney, VT: Smith & Shute, 1823; 2nd ed. 1825) frames the New World exactly as Helaman 3:8 does: Israel "may wander still from sea to sea; from the northern frozen ocean, to the southern ocean at Cape Horn; and from the Pacific to the Atlantic." Orson Pratt's official 1879 footnotes to Helaman 3:8 read the four seas as the Arctic ("north of North America"), the "Atlantic, south of Cape Horn," the Pacific, and the Atlantic. This is the natural 19th-century Anglo-American reading, and it fits the text's language far better than any Mesoamerican or Hebrew reconstruction. (Both the exact Ethan Smith wording and Pratt's footnote text — the latter documented as removed in the 1920 LDS edition — should be confirmed on primary page images before publication.)
## Details
**The inverse-structure problem, restated.** The Sea-of-the-West maps depict a *water strait/inland sea dividing land* — the structural inverse of the Book of Mormon's *narrow neck of land dividing seas*. This inversion holds against Helaman 3:8's four seas as well: the cartographic tradition bounds the continent with a western sea intruding *into* the land, not a symmetrical box of four external seas. Ether 10:20's "the place where the sea divides the land" is an interesting partial exception: the phrase's image of a sea cleaving the continent actually resonates more with the *Mer de l'Ouest* cartographic idea (a sea dividing the land) than the narrow neck does — but the narrow neck itself remains the inverse of a dividing strait. Any parallel here must be labeled **partial at best, inverse for the neck.**
**The "direct North Sea/South Sea" hypothesis and why it partly backfires.** It is tempting to argue that "sea north" and "sea south" in Helaman 3:8 directly reflect the period English "North Sea"/"South Sea" conventions. But the mapping is dislocated: period "South Sea" was the *Pacific* (a western/southwestern ocean, so named because Balboa crossed Panama north-to-south), and "North Sea" was the *Atlantic/Caribbean* (an eastern/northeastern body). A clean lexical borrowing would therefore place the "South Sea" in the west and the "North Sea" in the east — orthogonal to how Helaman 3:8 pairs its axes (south/north as one axis, west/east as the other). The direct-convention hypothesis thus **weakens on inspection**; the cleaner and better-attested source is the hemispheric four-ocean reading (Frozen/Arctic Ocean north, ocean at Cape Horn south, Pacific west, Atlantic east) exemplified by Ethan Smith and Orson Pratt.
**Lahontan's text vs. Lahontan's maps.** Lahontan's *New Voyages to North-America* (1703 English edition), Letter XVI, describes a westward "salt Lake" of "three hundred Leagues in Circumference… its Mouth stretching a great way to the Southward," using compass descriptors ("westward," "to the Southward") but **not** any directional sea-name. The "Sea of the West" is the later cartographers' extrapolation from his map, not his prose. Consequently, the directional-sea feature of Helaman 3:8 cannot be tied to Lahontan's *text*; his relevance is confined to the cartographic afterlife of his river/salt-lake reports.
**Morse's usage (the flagship American schoolbook).** Jedidiah Morse's *American Universal Geography* (1793) — the most influential geography in the early republic — consistently uses "Pacific Ocean" and "Atlantic ocean," e.g., "The Andes, in South-America, form the height of land between the Atlantic and Pacific Oceans," and bounds the United States "south east, by the Atlantic ocean." For the polar sea he uses "Frozen Ocean" (e.g., Tartary "is bounded by the Frozen Ocean, on the North; by the Pacific Ocean, on the East"). I found no confirmed instance of "South Sea," "North Sea," "Western Ocean," or "Sea of the West" in Morse's text. This is a significant negative datum: in the formal geographic register of Joseph Smith's own milieu, directional sea-naming was already being displaced by the modern ocean-basin names, even though "South Sea" survived in voyage and literary usage (e.g., Daniel Coxe's *Carolana*, 1722/1741, promising "an easy communication betwixt the River Meschacebe [Mississippi], and the South Sea [Pacific Ocean], which separates America from China").
**Critical-scholarship coverage.** Earl Wunderli (*An Imperfect Book*, 2013) and the Vogel–Metcalfe critiques engage Book of Mormon geography and directional problems, generally in service of a hemispheric/distance critique of Sorenson's limited Mesoamerican model rather than a focused source analysis of the four-seas language. The most pointed secular-style treatment of the four seas is Robert M. Bowman Jr.'s (Institute for Religious Research), which — though evangelical rather than from the named critical scholars — assembles the primary ANE evidence and concludes that "the simplest explanation is that the Book of Mormon was composed as a modern fiction on the assumption that its lands constituted both North and South America." No treatment I located from Vogel, Palmer, Persuitte, or Grunder isolates "sea north"/"sea south" as a distinct source-critical problem; this remains an under-worked niche where the present project can contribute.
## Recommendations
1. **Frame "sea north"/"sea south" as independent corroborating evidence for 19th-century composition, not as a necessary requirement of the Lahontan thesis.** Under the dossier's conjunction-probability discipline, the four-seas datum supports early-19th-century Anglo-American authorship broadly (via the hemispheric four-ocean reading and the Ethan Smith parallel); it does *not* specifically require or single out Lahontan, whose text names no directional sea. Tying Helaman 3:8's four seas specifically to Lahontan would over-reach.
2. **Lead with the Ethan Smith (1823/1825) and Orson Pratt (1879) parallels, not the cartographic conventions.** These are the strongest, cleanest, best-sourced comparanda and avoid the dislocation problem. Label the Ethan Smith parallel **near-exact (structural)**; label the "South Sea = Pacific," "North Sea = Atlantic/Caribbean," and "Sea of the West" conventions **partial** (live period usage but dislocated axes); label the narrow-neck/dividing-strait relationship **inverse**.
3. **Foreground the Hebrew asymmetry as the analytical fulcrum.** Concede the mainstream *yam* = west explanation (and note the marginal *yam* = "rarely… south"), then show that no Semitic idiom generates "sea north," so the north/south seas cannot be defended as a Hebraism and must be explained on 19th-century grounds.
4. **Prosecute the "no ancient four-seas precedent" point aggressively.** The Babylonian single-ring *marratu*, Greek single Oceanus, and absence of the motif in the Hebrew Bible directly refute the Clark/Hamblin/Gardner "ancient mythic four seas" defense. Cite Horowitz (*Mesopotamian Cosmic Geography*) and Rochberg via the primary map (BM 92687).
5. **Verify against page images before publication.** Because HathiTrust/Google Books full text is inaccessible and archive.org/Michigan pages block automated fetching, confirm on page images: (a) that Lahontan's Letter XVI and memoirs contain no "South Sea"/"Western Sea"/"Frozen Sea"/"Western Ocean"; (b) Morse's full phrase-search for "South Sea"/"Sea of the West" across the 1793 and later editions; (c) the exact Skousen *Analysis of Textual Variants* entries (Alma 22; 50; Helaman 3) for any variant in the sea-direction phrases; and (d) the verbatim Ethan Smith wording and Orson Pratt's 1879 footnote text. **Benchmark that would change the assessment:** if page-image review turns up Lahontan or Morse actually using "sea north/sea south" or a four-directional-sea boundary description in prose, upgrade the cartographic thesis from "partial/corroborating" to "direct."
## Caveats
- The subagent's "not found" results for Lahontan and Morse were captured from full-text search extracts, not exhaustive page-image reading, because the host sites blocked automated retrieval; treat the negative findings as strong but not yet definitive pending image verification.
- The exact Ethan Smith "northern frozen ocean… to the southern ocean at Cape Horn" wording and Orson Pratt's 1879 Helaman 3:8 footnote (documented as removed in the 1920 LDS edition) were not independently confirmed from an authoritative primary scan within the research budget; they are reproduced here from secondary quotation and flagged for page-image verification.
- Much of the four-seas discussion online originates from LDS apologetic and geography-model sites (Sorenson, Hauck, Clark, Gardner, FAIR, Interpreter, and various Heartland/Great Lakes blogs) and from one evangelical counter-apologetic (IRR/Bowman); these are used here only to document claims and assemble primary evidence, not as authoritative endorsement.
- The claim that "sea north/sea south" appear "only twice" (some sources add Alma 22:32) versus "only in Helaman 3:8" varies by counting method; the safe, defensible statement is that the *fully explicit four-directional-sea formula* is unique to Helaman 3:8 and that neither a "sea north" nor a "sea south" ever functions as a located feature in the narrative.
- "South Sea" (Pacific) and "North Sea" (Atlantic/Caribbean) were genuinely live period conventions, but their directional axes do not align with Helaman 3:8's usage; do not round this partial resemblance up to an exact parallel.
# Rigdon's Pittsburgh years and the making of a textual world, 1822–1826
Sidney Rigdon inhabited a richer intellectual environment during his four Pittsburgh years than any previous scholarship has fully reconstructed. Between his arrival on **January 28, 1822** and his departure for Ohio in late 1825 or early 1826, Rigdon had demonstrable access to a city with at least **2,000 cataloged library volumes**, multiple active booksellers and printers, a fledgling university, and a social circle that included two of the most learned theological minds on the American frontier — Alexander Campbell and Walter Scott. The books most commonly cited as environmental sources for the Book of Mormon — Heckewelder's *Indian Nations*, Carver's *Travels*, Robertson's *History of America*, and Josephus — were all almost certainly available in Pittsburgh during these years, and Rigdon sat four miles from the McKees Rocks Mound, the largest burial mound in western Pennsylvania, in a city that functioned as the literal gateway to the Ohio Valley's archaeological heartland.
What follows is a structured chapter outline mapping verifiable facts about Rigdon's Pittsburgh environment to arguments about his plausible reading, organized with explicit evidence tiers: **[DOCUMENTED]** for claims supported by primary sources, **[INFERRED]** for claims supported by strong circumstantial evidence, and **[REQUIRES ARCHIVAL WORK]** for claims that cannot be resolved without further research.
---
## I. Rigdon's Pittsburgh chronology: what is actually established
**[DOCUMENTED]** Rigdon and his wife Phebe received letters of dismission from the Baptist Church at Warren, Ohio, to the Baptist Church at Pittsburgh in January 1822. He departed Warren on January 5 and arrived in Pittsburgh on January 28. The invitation came from the First Baptist Church of Pittsburgh in November 1821, likely facilitated by Alexander Campbell's influence within the Redstone Baptist Association. His father-in-law Jeremiah Brooks urged him to accept, noting "it was not very often a young minister received such an offer."
**[DOCUMENTED]** The First Baptist Church had been organized in 1812 with twelve members under the auspices of the Redstone Baptist Association. It met in homes for its first eight years before erecting a wooden chapel at Third Avenue and Grant Street in 1820. The church was formally **chartered in 1822 with Rigdon listed as the first charter member**, alongside James Morford, Benjamin Pyatt, William H. Hart, and twenty-seven others. Rigdon was its fourth minister, succeeding John Davis. Under his preaching, "his congregation became one of the largest in the city."
**[DOCUMENTED]** On **July 11, 1823**, a schism split the congregation, with each faction disfellowshipping the other. Rev. John Winter led the opposition. The Redstone Baptist Association met in Pittsburgh on September 5–7, 1823, and appointed a committee to investigate "the merits of the case respecting the Pittsburgh church." Rigdon's party withdrew from consultation. On **October 11, 1823**, Rigdon was "excluded from the Redstone Association Baptist Denomination." The ministers who tried him "denied him the liberty of speaking in self-defense." The doctrinal charges centered on three positions: rejection of infant damnation, baptismal regeneration (baptism for remission of sins), and Campbellite restorationism — New Testament priority over Old Testament, rejection of creeds, weekly communion, and rejection of clerical hierarchy. A majority of the congregation followed Rigdon; the minority regained control of the meetinghouse through the Association's intervention but could not buy it back for five years.
**[DOCUMENTED]** From approximately 1824 to 1825–26, Rigdon entered the tanning business with his brother-in-law **Richard Brooks** (a currier by trade), operating a tannery on **Penn Avenue near the intersection with Hand Street** (now 9th Street), along the Allegheny River. Rigdon's son John reported that after two years "they sold the tannery." During this period, Rigdon preached Campbell's restorationism on Sundays in the Pittsburgh courthouse to a portion of his former congregation.
**[DOCUMENTED]** Rigdon left Pittsburgh in winter 1825–26, moving first to Bainbridge, Geauga County, Ohio. In June 1826, the Baptist minister in Mentor died, and Rigdon preached the funeral sermon. Judge Orris Clapp offered to build him a home if he would assume the Mentor pastorate, which he accepted.
**Key gap:** The early First Baptist Church of Pittsburgh records were **destroyed in the Great Pittsburgh Fire of 1845**. The Redstone Baptist Association minutes (1804–1826), however, survive at the **Western Reserve Historical Society Archives** in Cleveland and have been transcribed by Dale Broadhurst on sidneyrigdon.com. These minutes constitute the best surviving institutional documentation of Rigdon's ecclesiastical career in Pittsburgh.
---
## II. The Pittsburgh book world that Rigdon entered
### Libraries: the Pittsburgh Permanent Library Company
**[DOCUMENTED]** The Pittsburgh Permanent Library Company was organized in **November 1813**, shortly after the death of printer Zadok Cramer. Cramer's personal circulating library of approximately **2,000 volumes** became the nucleus of the company's holdings. The library was housed in **a room in the Court House**, open once a week on Saturday evenings. Its president was **Rev. Francis Herron** (a prominent Presbyterian minister), its treasurer was **John Spear** (Cramer's surviving business partner), and its twelve directors were described as "all prominent citizens of the town." The library's founding and early operations are documented in Riddle's *Pittsburgh Directory for 1815* and in George T. Fleming's 1923 article in the *Pittsburgh Gazette Times*.
**[REQUIRES ARCHIVAL WORK]** No standalone printed catalog of the Pittsburgh Permanent Library Company from the 1822–1826 period has been located. D.M. Goodfellow's article "Centenary of a Pittsburgh Library" (*Western Pennsylvania Historical Magazine* 31, 1948, pp. 21–25) likely contains additional information about holdings but was not accessible for this research. The nature of the collection — derived from Cramer's stock — would have included literature, religious texts, school books, travel narratives, legal texts, and reference works in multiple languages, given Cramer's wide-ranging inventory. **This is the single most important archival gap for this chapter.** If a catalog survives, it would resolve many questions about specific titles available to Rigdon.
### The university library and student societies
**[DOCUMENTED]** The Western University of Pennsylvania (now University of Pittsburgh) received its charter in 1819, upgrading from the Pittsburgh Academy founded in 1787. By 1822, it had a library — records show students were "responsible for paying for any damage they did to books." The Irving and Philomathean Literary Societies maintained separate membership libraries. The first graduating class of three students finished in 1823.
**[REQUIRES ARCHIVAL WORK]** No surviving library catalog or book list from the 1820s WUP library has been located. The original library was destroyed in the 1845 fire. Whether any inventory survived elsewhere is unknown.
### What the booksellers offered
**[DOCUMENTED]** Cramer & Spear, the successor firm to Zadok Cramer's bookstore (originally at the **"Sign of the Franklin Head" on Market Street**), was the city's most established bookselling operation. After Cramer's death in 1814, his widow Elizabeth continued the business; after her death in 1818, daughter Susan Cramer replaced her, and the firm operated as Cramer & Spear until 1835. Between 1822 and 1826, the firm published the annual **Cramer's Pittsburgh Almanack**, the **12th and final edition of *The Navigator*** (1824), Lindley Murray's *Introduction to the English Reader* (4th Pittsburgh edition, 1823), and other school texts, Bibles, and religious works. Cramer had originally sold books in **English, German, French, Greek, Latin, and Spanish** — dictionaries, almanacs, Bibles, schoolbooks, music instruction books, legal texts, and stationery.
**[DOCUMENTED]** **Johnston & Stockton** was formed in **1824** by Samuel Reed Johnston and R.C. Stockton. They operated a printing office, bookbindery, and bookstore at No. 43 Market Street and established a paper mill in Fallston, Beaver County in 1825. They became one of Pittsburgh's most prominent bookselling firms.
**[DOCUMENTED]** Other printers active during this period included **Eichbaum & Johnston** (publishers of the *Pittsburgh Gazette*, 1820–1822, after which David MacLean purchased the paper); **Silas Engles** (a printer who produced works including Gallitzin's *Defence of Catholic Principles*); **Henry Holdship** (who published *The Western Farmer's Almanac*); and **Patterson & Lambdin** (discussed in detail below).
**[REQUIRES ARCHIVAL WORK]** No standalone bookseller catalogs from 1822–1826 Pittsburgh survive in any identified archive. The best surviving proxies are **advertisements in the Pittsburgh Gazette** (available on microfilm at Carnegie Library of Pittsburgh's Pennsylvania Department and through ProQuest Historical Newspapers) and the **Cramer's Almanacks** themselves, which contain advertisements for available stock. The **Jones Directory of 1826** (*Pittsburgh in the year eighteen hundred and twenty-six*) is digitized on Internet Archive (archive.org/details/Pgh1826) as 162 page images and would provide the definitive roster of booksellers at the end of this period, but has not been fully examined.
### Pittsburgh's other reading infrastructure
**[DOCUMENTED]** The **Pittsburgh Apprentices' Library** was founded around 1825, but very limited information survives about its collection or catalog. No Pittsburgh Athenaeum existed during this period; the **Franklin Reading Room**, the earliest documented subscription reading room by name, opened in 1831 with 1,500 titles — just outside the relevant period.
**[INFERRED]** Church libraries almost certainly existed in some form. Religious texts were the largest category of books sold in early Pittsburgh. The First Baptist Church, with its 1820 chapel, likely maintained at least a small collection of theological works. However, no specific documentation of any Pittsburgh church library from this period has been located, and the First Baptist records were destroyed in 1845.
---
## III. Patterson & Lambdin: what the printing firm actually was
**[DOCUMENTED]** Robert Patterson operated a printing and publishing business in Pittsburgh. **J. Harrison Lambdin** (born September 1, 1798) joined the firm in 1812 at age fourteen and became a partner on January 1, 1818, after which it was styled "Patterson & Lambdin." The firm was located on **Fourth Street, west of Wood Street**. Lambdin "superintended the printing-office" and died circa 1825 — the exact date has never been established. **Silas Engles** served as foreman printer and "general superintendent of the printing business"; Robert P. DuBois was another employee (1818–1820).
**[DOCUMENTED]** The firm's known publications include: **Patterson's Magazine Almanac** (annual, including 1820 edition); **The Honest Man's Extra Almanac** (1813); **Pittsburgh Town and Country Magazine Almanac**; German almanacs; Robert Patterson Sr.'s *A Treatise of Practical Arithmetic* (1818–1819, co-published with M. Carey and Son, Philadelphia); and *The Wonders of Redeeming Love* (religious publication). The firm co-published with major eastern publishers including M. Carey and Son (Philadelphia), Collins and Co. (New York), and Fielding Lucas Jr. (Baltimore). Almanacs were the firm's primary output.
**[DOCUMENTED]** No surviving account books, customer records, or business correspondence from Patterson & Lambdin have been located in any known archive. This absence is a permanent impediment to resolving questions about who visited the shop and what materials were on its shelves.
### The Spalding manuscript: reconstructing the chain of evidence
**[DOCUMENTED]** Solomon Spalding (1761–1816), a Dartmouth-educated former Congregationalist minister, wrote a manuscript historical romance circa 1809–1812 in Conneaut, Ohio, purporting to be a record of ancient inhabitants of America. He moved to Pittsburgh around 1812–1814 and reportedly brought the manuscript to Patterson & Lambdin for publication.
Patterson's own 1842 statement confirmed that "a gentleman, originally from the east, had put into his [assistant's] hands a manuscript of a singular work, chiefly in the style of our English translation of the Bible." Patterson "only read a few pages" and agreed publication might proceed "if the author furnished the funds." No funds came, and "after some weeks" the manuscript was supposedly returned. Spalding died in Amity, Pennsylvania on October 20, 1816, about forty miles from Pittsburgh.
**[DOCUMENTED]** The actual Spalding manuscript was rediscovered in **1884** by L.L. Rice in Honolulu, at the suggestion of Oberlin College President James H. Fairchild. It bore the label "Manuscript Story — Conneaut Creek" and is now held at **Oberlin College Library**. The manuscript describes Romans blown off course on a voyage to Britain who land in eastern North America circa the 4th century CE. It features names like Moonrod, Mammoon, Lamesa, and Hamelick — not Nephi, Lehi, Moroni, or Zarahemla. It contains no "it came to pass," no biblical-style language, and no religious content. **It bears virtually no resemblance to the Book of Mormon.** Fairchild concluded in 1885 that "the theory of the origin of the Book of Mormon in the traditional manuscript of Solomon Spaulding will probably have to be relinquished."
Proponents of the theory posit a hypothetical **second manuscript** in "scripture style" that has never been found. This lost-manuscript argument is considered the theory's greatest weakness by most scholars.
### What the witnesses actually said — and didn't say
The evidence connecting Rigdon personally to the manuscript rests on a thin and contested chain of testimony. **Rebecca Eichbaum** (1879, age 87) claimed that Rigdon and Lambdin were friends who "often came to the Post Office together" — but her period of postal service (primarily 1811–1816) predates the best evidence for Rigdon's Pittsburgh arrival. **Lambdin's widow**, contacted by Robert Patterson Jr. in 1879, "denied any knowledge of Rigdon, who 'certainly could not have been friends with Mr. Lambdin.'" Robert P. DuBois (employee, 1818–1820) had "no recollection of either Spalding or Rigdon." Samuel Cooper (friend of Lambdin) was "equally sure Rigdon never worked there." Isaac Craig's 1882 investigation found that "all impressed me with the belief that it was Silas Engles with whom Rigdon was intimate," not Lambdin.
**[DOCUMENTED]** Rigdon consistently denied any knowledge of Spalding, writing in 1839: "If I were to say that I ever heard of the Rev. Solomon Spaulding and his hopeful wife until Dr. P. Hurlbut wrote his lie about me I should be a liar like unto themselves." He acknowledged only "a very slight acquaintance" with publisher Patterson. His son John quoted him as swearing "before high heaven" that the Book of Mormon was presented to him for the first time by Parley P. Pratt in Mentor, Ohio in 1830.
**The scholarly consensus** since at least the 1970s, articulated most thoroughly by Lester Bush ("The Spalding Theory Then and Now," *Dialogue* 10:4, 1977) and endorsed by Fawn Brodie, Richard Van Wagoner, and Dan Vogel, holds the Spalding-Rigdon theory to be unpersuasive. Six of seven people claiming firsthand knowledge about Rigdon and Patterson's printing office "failed to recall or explicitly denied any contact between the two." Van Wagoner concluded that "the weight of scholarly studies since Fawn Brodie's seminal 1945 *No Man Knows My History*... has all but eliminated the Spalding theory."
**For the chapter's argument:** The Spalding manuscript question, while historically important, is a distraction from the more productive inquiry — namely, what ideas and texts Rigdon **demonstrably** could have accessed through Pittsburgh's ordinary intellectual infrastructure. The chapter should not rest its case on the Spalding theory but rather on the documented richness of the environment itself.
---
## IV. The intellectual circle: Campbell, Scott, and the frontier of ideas
### Alexander Campbell as intellectual catalyst
**[DOCUMENTED]** In the summer of 1821, before arriving in Pittsburgh, Rigdon and his brother-in-law Adamson Bentley traveled to Buffaloe (later Bethany), Virginia to meet Campbell. They talked "from after tea in the evening until the next morning." Rigdon was won over to Campbell's reform cause. Campbell was described as "a biblical scholar with a reputation for erudition," and his library at Bethany was legendary — the single largest personal theological library on the American frontier. Campbell read Greek, Latin, and French. His published debates (with Walker in 1820, with McCalla in 1823) circulated widely.
**[DOCUMENTED]** In **May 1823**, Rigdon served as **secretary** at Campbell's debate with Presbyterian William L. McCalla on baptism — a major public event demonstrating their close collaboration. Campbell began publishing *The Christian Baptist* in August 1823, the major periodical of the reform movement. Walter Scott contributed articles. This publication circulated in Pittsburgh and would have been a regular part of Rigdon's reading.
**[INFERRED]** Through Campbell, Rigdon gained access to a bibliographic network far exceeding what Pittsburgh's local booksellers offered. Campbell's correspondence and publications referenced a wide range of theological, philosophical, and historical works. Any book Campbell recommended or cited would have been within Rigdon's conceptual horizon, even if Rigdon did not read it firsthand.
### Walter Scott: the Edinburgh-educated neighbor
**[DOCUMENTED]** Walter Scott (1796–1861), born in Scotland, had studied for **six years at the University of Edinburgh** before arriving in Pittsburgh circa 1818–1819. He took over a small Haldanean congregation after the drowning of George Forrester in 1820 and also ran a school. Scott was deeply read in John Locke, John Glas, Robert Sandeman, and the Haldane brothers. He read Latin and Greek fluently.
**[DOCUMENTED]** Rigdon and Scott were **simultaneous Pittsburgh ministers from 1822 to at least 1826**. Hans Rollmann (*BYU Studies*, 1981) explicitly corrected the assumption that they did not meet until 1828. In 1823 or 1824, Scott's congregation merged with Rigdon's. Scott then withdrew to collaborate with Campbell on *The Christian Baptist*. By 1825, Scott took control of the Pittsburgh church when Rigdon departed. Scott married Sarah Whitsette in Pittsburgh in 1823. Sandemanian theology — including foot washing — passed from Scott to Rigdon and later into Mormon temple practice.
**[INFERRED]** Scott, with his Edinburgh education, almost certainly maintained a significant personal library. His intellectual interests — Lockean epistemology, primitive Christianity, biblical languages — would have exposed Rigdon to a range of philosophical and theological texts. No inventory of Scott's library has been located, but this represents a significant potential archival discovery.
### The wider circle
**[DOCUMENTED]** Other figures in Rigdon's Pittsburgh orbit included: **Adamson Bentley** (brother-in-law, Warren-based but in regular contact; documented Freemason in the Warren, Ohio lodge); **Richard Brooks** and **William Brooks** (brothers-in-law and tanning partners); **Silas Engles** (the printer with whom Rigdon was reportedly "intimate"); **Rev. John Winter** (antagonist in the schism, who later claimed to have seen "a large manuscript" in Rigdon's study); and **Robert Richardson** (as a boy, he studied under Thomas Campbell in Pittsburgh and was present when Alexander Campbell visited in 1821–22; later became Campbell's biographer).
---
## V. Books Rigdon could have accessed: a tiered assessment
### Tier 1 — virtually certain availability
**Josephus's *Antiquities of the Jews*** (Whiston translation, 1732, many editions): "From the 18th century Josephus' works were almost as widespread in Britain as the Bible." Whiston's translation was "often the book — after the Bible — that Christians most frequently owned." Multiple American editions existed from the 1790s forward. **As a Baptist minister, Rigdon almost certainly owned or had immediate access to Josephus.** The work's detailed accounts of the destruction of Jerusalem, Jewish warfare, and ancient Israelite history are directly relevant to Book of Mormon themes.
**Jonathan Carver's *Travels Through the Interior Parts of North America*** (1778, 30+ editions): One of the most popular travel books about North America in the 18th and early 19th centuries. Seven American editions were printed between 1784 and 1813. George Washington owned a copy. With **over thirty editions in multiple languages**, this book was almost certainly available through any Pittsburgh bookseller or library.
**William Robertson's *History of America*** (1777, 12+ editions by 1806): A standard reference work ranked alongside Hume and Gibbon. Its treatment of Pre-Columbian civilizations and Native American anthropology made it a foundational text in any discussion of American origins. Almost certainly present in the Pittsburgh Permanent Library and available through booksellers.
### Tier 2 — high probability of availability
**John Heckewelder's *Account of the History, Manners, and Customs of the Indian Nations*** (1819): **This is the most consequential single title for the chapter's argument.** Published by the American Philosophical Society in Philadelphia as part of the *Transactions of the Historical & Literary Committee* (Vol. 1). Heckewelder was a **Moravian missionary who spent decades in western Pennsylvania and Ohio** — his mission stations included Friedensstadt on the Beaver River in western Pennsylvania, and Gnadenhütten in Ohio. He reported military intelligence to American authorities in Pittsburgh during the Revolution. His book appeared just three years before Rigdon's arrival, and its subject matter — the Lenni Lenape, their traditions of a noble people displaced by warlike invaders — directly parallels the Nephite/Lamanite framework. A 1959 article in *Western Pennsylvania History* by Philip H. Lantz documented Heckewelder's broad influence on western Pennsylvanians specifically. Heckewelder was elected to the American Antiquarian Society in 1822 and died on January 21, 1823 — events that would have raised his profile during Rigdon's Pittsburgh pastorate.
**[INFERRED]** No direct evidence places a copy in a specific Pittsburgh collection, but the combination of Philadelphia publication, APS distribution networks, intense regional relevance, and recent publication date makes Heckewelder's presence in Pittsburgh during 1822–1826 **highly probable**. Confirming this requires locating the Pittsburgh Permanent Library catalog or searching Pittsburgh Gazette advertisements.
**Caleb Atwater's *Description of the Antiquities Discovered in the State of Ohio*** (1820): Published in Volume 1 of the *Transactions of the American Antiquarian Society*. The first substantial scientific treatment of Ohio mound antiquities, proposing "Hindoo" origins for the mound builders. Of acute regional interest — Pittsburgh was the gateway to the Ohio Valley mound region, and the McKees Rocks Mound sat **four miles from downtown Pittsburgh**. The American Antiquarian Society's distribution networks would have reached western Pennsylvania. Reviewed in the *Western Review*, showing Ohio Valley circulation.
### Tier 3 — moderate probability
**Ethan Smith's *View of the Hebrews*** (1823, 2nd ed. 1825): Published in Poultney, Vermont by a small publisher. B.H. Roberts identified eighteen points of similarity with the Book of Mormon. However, its small-town Vermont origin and limited distribution network make rapid penetration to Pittsburgh uncertain. **The first edition appeared in 1823 — Rigdon had at most three years to encounter it before leaving Pittsburgh.** The second edition (1825) gave even less time. Oliver Cowdery (later Joseph Smith's scribe) lived in Poultney and may have attended Ethan Smith's church, providing an alternative transmission path that does not require Rigdon's Pittsburgh access.
**Gilbert Hunt's *The Late War Between the United States and Great Britain*** (1816): Went through at least sixteen editions or imprints between 1816 and 1819. The third edition (1819) was marketed as a school textbook. Computer analysis has found statistically significant n-gram overlaps with the Book of Mormon, though scholars debate whether this reflects direct influence or shared use of biblical language conventions. Primarily distributed through New York school systems, making Pittsburgh availability uncertain but possible.
**James Adair's *History of the American Indians*** (1775): Only one edition existed (London, 1775), never reprinted in America until 1930. Described as "one of the most widely-cited, but least-available, contemporary texts." However, Adair's central argument — that Native Americans descended from the Lost Tribes of Israel — was the **single most influential framework** for understanding Native American origins in this period, and it circulated widely through secondary sources. Ethan Smith's *View of the Hebrews* cites Adair extensively. Rigdon could have absorbed Adair's thesis without reading the book itself.
### Tier 4 — low probability of direct access
**Baron de Lahontan's *New Voyages to North America*** (1703): Only two English editions (1703 and 1735), both London imprints. No American edition existed before the 20th century. An antiquarian collector's item by the 1820s. **Unlikely to have been in Pittsburgh.**
**Robert Beverley's *History and Present State of Virginia*** (1705): Limited reprinting, primarily focused on Virginia. **Unlikely in western Pennsylvania collections.**
**Samuel Purchas's *Pilgrimage* or *Pilgrimes*** (1613/1625): Massive, rare, expensive multi-volume works. Only major research libraries held copies. **Essentially no chance of Pittsburgh availability.**
---
## VI. Pittsburgh as the epicenter of the Mound Builder question
The chapter's strongest argument may not rest on any single book but on the **ambient intellectual environment** surrounding Native American antiquities. Pittsburgh in the 1820s was not merely adjacent to the Mound Builder question — it was at the geographic heart of it.
**[DOCUMENTED]** The **McKees Rocks Mound**, built by the Adena people circa 500 BCE–1000 CE, stood just four miles west of downtown Pittsburgh at the confluence of Chartiers Creek and the Ohio River. It was the largest burial mound in western Pennsylvania — sixteen feet tall, eighty-five feet wide, 266 feet in circumference. It would have been visible and known to any Pittsburgher of the 1820s.
**[DOCUMENTED]** The **Grave Creek Mound** in present-day Moundsville, West Virginia was approximately sixty miles south of Pittsburgh along the Ohio River — one of the largest conical mounds in North America at sixty-nine feet tall. The entire Ohio River corridor downstream from Pittsburgh was dotted with mounds at Marietta, Circleville, Newark, and Chillicothe — all within the cultural orbit of Pittsburgh as the gateway to the Ohio Valley.
**[DOCUMENTED]** The dominant theories about the mound builders in the early 1820s included Hebrew/Israelite origins (Adair, 1775; Ethan Smith, 1823), Hindu origins (Atwater, 1820; Clifford, 1819–20), Welsh origins (various, from 1812), Phoenician origins, and Egyptian origins. The common denominator was the "lost race" narrative — that a sophisticated civilization had been displaced by the ancestors of contemporary Native Americans. This framework maps directly onto the Book of Mormon's Nephite/Lamanite structure, in which a literate, city-building civilization is destroyed by a more "savage" people.
**[DOCUMENTED]** Pittsburgh newspapers, particularly the **Pittsburgh Gazette** (available on microfilm at Carnegie Library of Pittsburgh) and the **Pittsburgh Mercury** (1820–1826, cataloged at the Library of Congress), reported on these debates. DeWitt Clinton's popular lectures on mound builders, Atwater's 1820 publication, and ongoing discoveries along the Ohio Valley would have been covered. **Systematic searching of these newspaper files for 1822–1826 is a critical next step** — the ProQuest Historical Newspapers database (Pittsburgh Post-Gazette archives from 1786–2003) and microfilm holdings at the University of Pittsburgh provide access.
**[INFERRED]** A Baptist minister living four miles from a major archaeological mound, at the head of a river system lined with such monuments, in a period of intense public debate about who built them and where they came from, would have encountered these ideas through multiple channels — newspapers, conversation, sermons, and published accounts — regardless of which specific books he read.
---
## VII. What Rigdon read and how he thought
### Documented reading habits
**[DOCUMENTED]** John W. Rigdon's reminiscences, published by Karl Keller in *Dialogue* 1:4 (Winter 1966), portray his father as a compulsive reader from childhood: "He borrowed all the histories he could get and began to read them. In this way he became a great historian, the best I ever saw." Sidney "seemed to have the history of the world on his tongue's end" and "was as familiar with the Bible as a child was with his spelling book." As a boy, he read by firelight: "He used to get [hickory bark] and at night throw it on the old fire place and then lay with his face headed towards the fire and read history till near morning."
**[DOCUMENTED]** The *Times and Seasons* (1843) biography states that after losing the pastorate, Rigdon "abandoned preaching and all other employment, for the purpose of studying the bible." This claim of intensive biblical study during 1824–1826 comes from Rigdon's own account and was later used by E.D. Howe to argue that Rigdon had three years to study and rework the Spalding manuscript — but it equally supports the argument that Rigdon was deepening his theological and historical knowledge through wide reading.
### The theological sources behind his doctrinal disputes
**[DOCUMENTED]** Rigdon's 1823–24 doctrinal positions derived primarily from three streams. First, **Alexander Campbell's teachings**: New Testament priority over Old Testament, rejection of creeds, baptismal regeneration, weekly communion, and rejection of clerical hierarchy. Campbell's published debates (Walker, 1820; McCalla, 1823) and *The Christian Baptist* (from August 1823) were Rigdon's primary theological reference points. Second, **Walter Scott's formulations**: the five-step plan of salvation (faith, repentance, baptism, remission of sins, gift of the Holy Spirit) and Sandemanian theology, including foot washing. Third, **Rigdon's own biblical reading**, which led him to emphasize spiritual gifts (tongues, prophecy, healing) — a point on which he later diverged from Campbell, who rejected charismatic practices.
**[INFERRED]** Through the Campbell-Scott circle, Rigdon was exposed to a sophisticated hermeneutical tradition rooted in Scottish Enlightenment thought (via Scott's Edinburgh education), Lockean epistemology, and Haldanean restorationism. This is not a marginal frontier preacher absorbing ideas passively — it is a man embedded in an active, text-driven, argumentative intellectual community that debated the nature of religious authority, the structure of primitive Christianity, and the proper interpretation of biblical prophecy.
### Masonic connections
**[DOCUMENTED]** Rigdon's brother-in-law Adamson Bentley was a documented member of the Warren, Ohio Masonic Lodge. In 1842, both Joseph Smith and Rigdon were raised to the third degree of Master Mason "on sight" by Grand Master Abraham Jonas of Illinois. **No direct evidence establishes Rigdon as a Mason during the Pittsburgh years.** However, through Bentley's membership and the pervasiveness of Masonry in early republic civic culture, Rigdon would have been familiar with Masonic ideas and rituals — including secret combinations, oaths, and covenants that scholars have compared to elements of the Book of Mormon's Gadianton Robbers.
---
## VIII. Archival roadmap: where the answers are
The following repositories hold materials that could resolve the remaining questions:
**Heinz History Center / Detre Library & Archives** (1212 Smallman Street, Pittsburgh): The Historical Society of Western Pennsylvania's archives document 250 years of western Pennsylvania life. Open Wednesday–Saturday, free to all visitors. Holdings include manuscripts, newspapers, photographs, maps, and memorabilia from the 1820s. **Priority search: Pittsburgh Permanent Library Company records, bookseller advertisements, First Baptist Church materials that may have survived outside the church itself.**
**Darlington Memorial Library / University of Pittsburgh Archives** (7500 Thomas Blvd, Pittsburgh): Assembled by William M. Darlington (1815–1889), containing 11,000+ volumes of early western Pennsylvania and Ohio Valley materials. Includes frontier-era documents, early Pittsburgh imprints, and Native American history materials. Digitized at darlington.library.pitt.edu and digital.library.pitt.edu. **Priority search: surviving Cramer & Spear imprints, Pittsburgh Permanent Library records, WUP library catalogs.**
**Carnegie Library of Pittsburgh, Pennsylvania Department**: Holds microfilm copies of the Pittsburgh Gazette (1805–1825) and other newspapers. ProQuest Historical Newspapers provides digital access to Pittsburgh Post-Gazette archives from 1786. **Priority search: bookseller advertisements, coverage of mound builder debates, religious controversy reporting, 1822–1826.**
**Western Reserve Historical Society Archives** (Cleveland, Ohio): Holds the **Redstone Baptist Association Minutes, 1804–1826** — the primary institutional documentation of Rigdon's ecclesiastical career in Pittsburgh. Already transcribed by Dale Broadhurst on sidneyrigdon.com.
**Oberlin College Library** (Oberlin, Ohio): Holds the actual Spalding manuscript ("Manuscript Story — Conneaut Creek"), rediscovered in 1884.
**University of Utah, Marriott Library, Special Collections** (Salt Lake City): Holds the **Dale R. Broadhurst Papers** (Accn 913, 1800–2000), including transcripts, news clippings, historical journals, and research materials relating to the Book of Mormon and Spalding manuscript theory.
**BYU L. Tom Perry Special Collections** (Provo, Utah): Holds Rigdon-related materials from the post-Nauvoo period, including letters from Sidney Rigdon in the Stephen Post papers.
**[REQUIRES ARCHIVAL WORK]** Several critical items remain unlocated:
- The Pittsburgh Permanent Library Company catalog (if one was ever printed)
- Any surviving papers of Robert Patterson, Harrison Lambdin, or Silas Engles
- Walter Scott's personal library inventory
- First Baptist Church of Pittsburgh records beyond what survived the 1845 fire
- The Redstone Baptist Association's full correspondence files (as distinct from the meeting minutes)
- Subscription records of the Pittsburgh Permanent Library Company (which would reveal who was reading what)
---
## IX. Unresolved questions and directions for further research
**1. The library catalog question.** If a catalog of the Pittsburgh Permanent Library Company's 2,000 volumes survives — whether as a printed broadside, a manuscript list, or an entry in a now-forgotten Pittsburgh directory — it would transform this chapter from inference to documentation. The Detre Library at the Heinz History Center and the Darlington Collection at Pitt are the most likely repositories. Goodfellow's 1948 *Western Pennsylvania Historical Magazine* article is the most promising secondary source.
**2. Newspaper advertisements.** Systematic searching of the Pittsburgh Gazette files for 1822–1826 — now accessible through ProQuest — could yield bookseller advertisements listing specific titles for sale, including the key books identified above. This is labor-intensive but potentially definitive work.
**3. The Heckewelder question.** No direct evidence has been found placing Heckewelder's 1819 book in a specific Pittsburgh collection, despite overwhelming circumstantial probability. A review of American Philosophical Society distribution records (held at the APS Library in Philadelphia) might resolve this. The APS sent its *Transactions* to subscribing institutions and individuals; if the Pittsburgh Permanent Library Company or WUP was a subscriber, this would be documented.
**4. Campbell's library and reading recommendations.** The full text of *The Christian Baptist* (1823–1830) needs page-by-page searching for references to books about Native American origins, mound builders, or antiquities. Campbell's later *Millennial Harbinger* engaged with these topics, but no evidence has emerged that the earlier periodical did. Campbell's personal library at Bethany, if an inventory survives, would reveal the full range of texts in Rigdon's intellectual orbit.
**5. Walter Scott's reading.** Scott's Edinburgh education and subsequent career suggest he owned a significant library. No inventory has been located. His descendants' papers, if they survive, could reveal what books were in the Pittsburgh household that Rigdon visited regularly.
**6. The *Christian Baptist* subscription list.** If subscribers are documented, this would map the Pittsburgh readership of the reform movement's primary periodical and identify who in Rigdon's circle was engaged with these ideas.
**7. Rigdon's pre-1822 Pittsburgh presence.** John W. Rigdon reported that Sidney had briefly preached in Pittsburgh before going to Warren. An 1816 notice in the *Pittsburgh Commonwealth* shows mail at the post office for both Rigdon and Solomon Spalding — placing them in the same city simultaneously. Whether Rigdon was in Pittsburgh in 1816, before the documented 1822 arrival, remains contested. Resolving this requires further examination of the *Commonwealth* files and postal records.
---
## Conclusion: the case the evidence supports
The chapter's argument should not depend on the Spalding manuscript or on proving Rigdon read any single book. The stronger argument is structural: Pittsburgh in 1822–1826 was a place where **a voracious reader with theological training, embedded in a text-driven intellectual community, living at the gateway to the Ohio Valley's archaeological heartland, could not have avoided** encountering the ideas that later appeared in the Book of Mormon. The Hebrew-origin theory for Native Americans was not an obscure academic hypothesis — it was the dominant popular explanation, promoted by best-selling books (Carver, Robertson), by the American Philosophical Society (Heckewelder), and by the American Antiquarian Society (Atwater). Josephus was as common as the Bible. The mound builder debate was literally visible from Pittsburgh's western suburbs.
What distinguishes Rigdon from other frontier ministers who absorbed these ideas passively is the quality of his intellectual circle. Through Campbell and Scott, he had access to a **hermeneutical tradition** — a way of reading scripture, interpreting prophecy, and constructing historical narratives — that most of his contemporaries lacked. Campbell's restorationism provided the theological engine; the mound builder literature provided the narrative substrate; and Rigdon's own "tongue's end" command of history provided the raw material for synthesis.
The critical scholarly works for this chapter remain Van Wagoner's *Sidney Rigdon: A Portrait of Religious Excess* (1994), Rollmann's "Early Baptist Career" (*BYU Studies*, 1981), Bush's "The Spalding Theory Then and Now" (*Dialogue*, 1977), McKiernan's "Conversion of Sidney Rigdon" (*Dialogue*, 1970), McClellan's "Sidney Rigdon's 1820 Ministry" (*Dialogue*, 2003), and the primary source compilations on sidneyrigdon.com. The archival gaps — particularly the Pittsburgh Permanent Library catalog, newspaper advertisements, and APS distribution records — represent the most productive avenues for future research that could convert inference into documentation.
# The Brass Director: Complete Research Dossier
## Tracing the Literary Origins of the Liahona
*A comprehensive compendium of all research material developed for the book project, compiled April 2026.*
---
## About This Document
This is the **complete research dossier** for *The Brass Director: Tracing the Literary Origins of the Liahona* — an independent scholarly project arguing that the Book of Mormon's Liahona was assembled from identifiable pre-1830 literary sources rather than divine revelation. Its companion document, the *Research Compendium*, provides a condensed synthesis and project-management view; this document assembles the full, detailed research artifacts that support the argument.
The material below represents roughly 42,000 words of original research developed across eight extended research sessions between February 26 and April 7, 2026. Each section is a self-contained research report, originally produced as a standalone artifact within a single research session. Where content overlaps across reports, this reflects the iterative nature of the investigation — each session built on what came before, often revisiting earlier findings with new context.
**Framework:** Critical, secular, academic. LDS apologetic sources (FARMS, FairMormon, Book of Mormon Central, BYU Religious Studies) are categorically excluded. The relevant scholarly interlocutors are Dan Vogel, Grant Palmer, Fawn Brodie, David Persuitte, and Rick Grunder.
**Working authorship theory:** Solomon Spalding and possibly Sidney Rigdon, drawing on sources available at Dartmouth College (where Spalding was a student 1782–1785) and in the western Pennsylvania-Ohio cultural environment (where Rigdon lived during the 1820s). The Lahontan thesis is framed to be workable under multiple authorship theories.
**Key finding across all sessions:** The Liahona is a composite literary object assembled from multiple pre-1830 source streams — Baron de Lahontan's *New Voyages to North America* (1703) for the phonetics of the name, Josephus *Antiquities* III for the theological architecture, the biblical manna narrative for the mode of acquisition, the manitou-compass colonial tradition for the spirit-animated instrument concept, Gilbert Hunt's *The Late War* (1816) for specific phrasing, and the learned English antiquarian register for the phrase "curious workmanship." No published scholar — apologetic or critical — has previously examined French colonial literature as a Book of Mormon source tradition.

# PART I: LAHONTAN AS PRIMARY LITERARY SOURCE
## Phase 1: Initial Source-Critical Review
**The complete works of Louis-Armand de Lom d'Arce, Baron de Lahontan (1703), contain three distinct passages describing Native Americans attributing spiritual or divine agency to European navigational and scientific instruments — including one that specifically names "a Compass" among "Machines…moved by Spirits."** This finding, combined with a striking phonetic correspondence between "La Hontan"/"Lahontan" and the Book of Mormon names "Liahona" and "Lehonti" (originally spelled "LAHONTI" in the manuscript), and numerous thematic parallels involving stone cities, copper metallurgy, a great salt lake, fortifications, and vanished civilizations, establishes Lahontan's *New Voyages to North America* as a potentially significant and previously unexamined source for Book of Mormon source criticism. No published scholarship — apologetic, critical, or secular — has ever drawn this connection. This review documents every relevant passage, provides exact page citations, catalogs all available digitized editions, and assesses the plausibility of textual transmission.
---
### I. Three passages link instruments, spirits, and the supernatural
Lahontan's works contain not one but **three separate passages** in which Native Americans attribute spiritual or divine power to European scientific instruments. These passages appear in different sections of his works and use distinct language, but together they form a coherent motif that parallels the Book of Mormon's Liahona — a "round ball of curious workmanship" made of "fine brass" with "two spindles," described as both a "compass" and a "director," which operated "according to the faith and diligence" of its users (1 Nephi 16:10, 16:28; Alma 37:38).
**Passage 1 — The Graphometer as "somewhat Divine"**
In the *Mémoires* section (1703 English edition, Vol. 2, p. 15; Thwaites 1905 edition, Vol. 2, p. 429), Lahontan writes:
> "They took my Graphometer for somewhat Divine, being unable to guess how we could know the distance of Places without measuring them by Cords or Rods, **without there were some Supernatural Assistance**. Longimetry pleased them far more than Altimetry…"
The graphomètre was a semicircular protractor with two sighting devices (alidades with pinnules) and, critically, **a built-in compass (boussole) at its center**, per Philippe Danfrie's 1597 original design and confirmed by the 17th-century Le Robert dictionary definition: *"un demi-cercle divisé en 180 degrez, avec une alhidade & des pinnules & une boussole au milieu."* This means the instrument Lahontan describes as "somewhat Divine" and requiring "Supernatural Assistance" was literally a **direction-finding device with pointers and a compass** — a conceptual match for the Liahona's description as a brass instrument with "spindles" that pointed direction through spiritual means.
**Passage 2 — Solar Quadrant and Watch as "Genius or Spirit"**
In the chapter "The way of Worship, used by the Savages" (*Mémoires*, 1703 English edition, Vol. 2, pp. 29–30; Thwaites, Vol. 2, pp. 446–447):
> "Before we launch out into the particulars of their Worship, 'twill be proper to remark that the Savages give the name of **Genius or Spirit** to all that surpasses their Understanding, and proceeds from a cause that they cannot trace. Some of these Spirits they take to be Good, and some Bad; of the former sort are the Spirit of Dreams, the Michibichi mentioned in my list of Animals, **a Solar Quadrant, an Alarm Watch, and an infinity of other things** that seem to them to be inconceivable."
The passage continues: "If a Fusee burst either through the fault of the Metal, or by being over Loaded, and Maims a Man, they'll tell you there was **an Evil Spirit lodged within it**." Lahontan here explains the Algonquian concept of *Manitou* — that mechanical objects whose operation the Native Americans cannot explain are categorized as housing spirits, either good or bad.
**Passage 3 — "A Compass…moved by Spirits"**
In the Appendix section containing letters from Lahontan's European travels, specifically a letter dated **July 4, 1695** (1703 English edition, Vol. 2, within pp. 185–286; Ouellet critical edition, p. 906):
> "**The Savages believe that a Watch, a Compass, and a thoufand other Machines are moved by Spirits**; for your ignorant and clownifh People form extravagant Ideas of every thing that surpasses their Imagination. The Laplanders and the Tartarian Kalmouks ador'd Strangers for playing Legerdemain Tricks. The Fire-eater at Paris pass'd a long while for a Magician. The Portuguese burnt a Horfe that did wonderful things…In Afia the Chymists are look'd upon as Poyfoners. In Africa the Mathematicians bear the name of Wizards…"
This third passage is the most significant for Liahona source criticism because it **specifically names "a Compass"** and uses the phrase **"moved by Spirits"** — directly attributing spiritual animation to a navigational instrument. The Liahona is repeatedly called a "compass" in the Book of Mormon text (Alma 37:38; 1 Nephi 18:12) and operates through faith/spiritual power rather than natural mechanisms. Lahontan's passage was identified by historian Benjamin Breen during research at UT Austin's Harry Ransom Center.
The combined effect of these three passages creates a **conceptual template**: European navigational and scientific instruments, perceived by Native Americans as operating through **spirit power** and **supernatural assistance**, with explicit reference to compasses, direction-finding, and divinity. This maps remarkably onto the Liahona's narrative function.
---
### II. "LAHONTI" in the original manuscript is nearly identical to "LAHONTAN"
The phonetic parallels between Lahontan's name and Book of Mormon nomenclature are more striking than previously recognized. The key discovery is that the **original manuscript** of the Book of Mormon spelled the name now published as "Lehonti" as **"LAHONTI"** — a form nearly identical to "LAHONTAN" with only the terminal *-n* differing.
**Documented variant spellings of "Lahontan" across editions:**
The Dictionary of Canadian Biography records: "he sometimes signed himself **Darce**, but more frequently **Lahontan**; the name also appears as **Lahontang, La Hontan** and **La Hontaa**." Additional variants documented across editions include: La Hontan (French title pages, consistently), Lahontan (English editions), Lahontemps (Hannover church death register, 1716), and LaHontan (some modern scholarship). French editions uniformly use the two-word form "La Hontan" while English editions prefer the single word "Lahontan."
**Phonetic correspondences:**
- **"La Hontan" → "Liahona"**: The consonant skeleton **L-H-N** is identical. The vowel pattern **a-o(n)-a** closely matches **ia-o-a**. If the terminal French nasal *-an* softens or drops — as it often does in English ears hearing French — "La Honta" → "Liahona" is remarkably close. The documented variant **"La Hontaa"** narrows the gap further.
