- **"LAHONTI" (original MS) / "Lahontan" → "Lehonti"**: The BYU Book of Mormon Onomasticon's variant page confirms: "O: **Lahonti** > Lehonti; P, 1830–1981: Lehonti" — meaning the **original dictation manuscript** read "LAHONTI," which was editorially changed to "Lehonti" in the printer's manuscript and all subsequent editions. "LAHONTI" and "LAHONTAN" share the consonant sequence **L-H-N-T** with identical vowels through the first three syllables. The only difference is the terminal vowel (*-i* vs. *-an*), and the *-i* ending is a common Hebrew/Hebraicized suffix.
- **Cross-referencing**: The BYU Onomasticon **cross-references LIAHONA and LEHONTI directly** — each entry states "Cf. Book of Mormon LEHONTI" and "Cf. Book of Mormon LIAHONA" respectively. Even believing Mormon scholars acknowledge these names are phonetically related, though they offer **no convincing etymology for either**: the LEHONTI entry states "No etymology immediately suggests itself," and the LIAHONA entry acknowledges that "the fact that 'compass' is the interpretation of LIAHONA might suggest that LIAHONA was not immediately recognizable to the native Lehite speaker."
**Contextual parallel for Lehonti**: Lehonti is a **Lamanite military leader** who commands forces from a mountain stronghold (Mount Antipas) in Alma 47. Lahontan was a **French military officer** who commanded forces from fortified frontier posts throughout New France. Both are martial figures associated with wilderness command.
---
### III. Native American names, geography, and the Long River
Lahontan's works catalog numerous Native American names, several with potential phonetic resonance, though none as striking as the Lahontan/Liahona parallel. The peoples he describes along the controversial "Long River" (Rivière Longue) are of particular interest:
- **Eokoros/Ekoros** — possibly the Arikaras
- **Essanapes** — described as a nation of 20,000 warriors in 12 permanent villages
- **Gnacsitares** — provided Lahontan with a map on deerskin; inhabited the upper Long River near mountains
- **Mozeemlek/Moseemlek** — lived in 100+ villages; "clothed," "bearded," "swarthy" complexion, "civil and submissive"; the faint phonetic echo of "Mosiah" (a Nephite king) is weak
- **Tahuglauk** — lived near a great salt lake in six stone cities; made copper axes and textiles; governed despotically
The **Long River geography** itself is notable. Lahontan describes a river flowing west from the Mississippi toward mountains (likely garbled reports of the Missouri or Minnesota rivers), terminating near **"a Salt Lake of three hundred Leagues in Circumference"** with stone cities surrounding it. Some scholars (notably W. Raymond Wood of the University of Missouri) have argued the geography corresponds to the upper Missouri; the "salt lake" has been tentatively linked to the Great Salt Lake of Utah. **Several Book of Mormon geographic models** place events in the Great Lakes and Mississippi Valley region — exactly Lahontan's theater of operations — and the existence of a great salt lake westward beyond mountains resonates with Mormon sacred geography.
**Lahontan also uses "boussole" (compass) in the French original** of Letter I, describing navigation on the Grand Banks: *"la Fleur-de-lis du compas ou de la boullole"* and *"compas de variation"* — confirming that compass terminology permeates the work from its opening pages.
---
### IV. Thematic parallels run far deeper than nomenclature
Beyond the brass/compass/spirits nexus, Lahontan's three volumes contain extensive thematic material that parallels Book of Mormon narratives across multiple categories.
**Stone cities and cement construction.** The Tahuglauk are described as building **"six noble Cities, surrounded with Stone cemented with fat Earth"** with "Houses…open above, like a Platform," plus "above an hundred Towns, great and small" around the salt lake (1703 English ed., Vol. 1, p. 125; Thwaites, Vol. 1, pp. 191–192). The Book of Mormon describes Nephite cities built with "cement" explicitly: "they did work in cement…they did build many cities" (Helaman 3:7–11).
**Copper metallurgy and sacred metal objects.** A Mozeemlek slave wore **"a reddish sort of a Copper Medal"** around his neck. When Lahontan had it melted, "it became thereupon heavier, and deeper colour'd, and withal somewhat tractable." The medals were "made by the **Tahuglauk, who are excellent Artizans**" (1703 English ed., Vol. 1, p. 126; Thwaites, Vol. 1, pp. 192–193). The Tahuglauk also manufactured "Stuffs, Copper Axes, and several other Manufactures." The Book of Mormon features extensive copper and brass metallurgy, including the brass plates of Laban, the Liahona of "fine brass," and Nephite metalworking (2 Nephi 5:15).
**Palisaded fortifications.** Lahontan describes Iroquois villages "Fortified with **double Palisadoes of very hard Wood**, which are as thick as one's Thigh, and fifteen Foot high, with little Squares about the middle of the Courtines" (Thwaites, Vol. 2, p. 417). Captain Moroni's fortifications in the Book of Mormon use virtually identical language: "he caused that they should build a breastwork of timbers upon the inner bank of the ditch…a frame of pickets…tall as a man" (Alma 50:1–6).
**Governance: councils of elders vs. despotic kings.** Lahontan describes Huron/Algonquian governance by **councils of old men** — "the Council, which is composed of the Old Men of the Nation…such as are above Sixty" (Thwaites, Vol. 2, pp. 422–423) — while the Tahuglauk have "Despotic" government "lodged in the hands of one great Head." The Book of Mormon's central political narrative is the transition from kings to judges/elders (Mosiah 29) and the contrast between Nephite republican governance and Lamanite monarchy.
**Hieroglyphic writing and records on natural materials.** Lahontan devotes an entire chapter to **"An Explication of the Savage Hieroglyphicks"** (1703 English ed., Vol. 2, p. 86; Thwaites, Vol. 2, p. 512), illustrated with a plate of symbolic characters. He also describes **maps drawn "upon the Rind of your Birch Tree"** consulted by old men in council (Thwaites, Vol. 2, p. 427), and a map "drawn upon a Deer's Skin" by the Gnacsitares (Thwaites, Vol. 1, pp. 190–191). The Book of Mormon centers on written records preserved on metal plates in "reformed Egyptian" characters, and the Mulekites' loss of language is attributed to "having brought no records with them" (Omni 1:17).
**Skin color distinctions.** Lahontan describes most Native Americans as "of a Sanguine Constitution, inclining to an **Olive Colour**" (Thwaites, Vol. 2, p. 415), while the Mozeemlek are distinguished by their "**swarthy**" complexion, beards, and European-like appearance — so markedly different that Lahontan writes "I could not imagine that they were Savages" (1703 English ed., Vol. 1, Letter XVI). The Book of Mormon's controversial skin-color narratives describe divine curses manifested as "a skin of blackness" upon the Lamanites (2 Nephi 5:21) and distinctions between peoples based on complexion.
**Vanished civilizations.** The Tahuglauk and Mozeemlek — with their stone cities, copper manufacturing, large vessels carrying 200 men, textile production, and despotic government — are described as real, existing peoples. Yet they were **never found by any subsequent explorer**. Most scholars consider them partly or wholly fabricated. Their narrative existence as **advanced civilizations that apparently vanished** parallels the Book of Mormon's central theme: the Nephite and Jaredite civilizations that flourished and were destroyed.
**Religion and the Great Spirit.** The "Adario" dialogues present a sophisticated Native theology centered on "the **Great Spirit or Master of Life**, whom we believe to be in every thing" (Thwaites, Vol. 2, pp. 518–520). Adario articulates beliefs in a Creator, the immortality of the soul, moral rationality, and a "Country of Souls" afterlife — a monotheistic framework consistent with the Book of Mormon's attribution of Christian-adjacent beliefs to pre-Columbian Americans.
**The egalitarian ideal.** Adario declares: "Silver is the Devil of Devils, the Tyrant of the French, the Source of all Evil." He describes a society without rich or poor, where all men "are all equally Masters." This directly parallels the post-Christ Nephite society of 4 Nephi: "there were not rich and poor…but they were all made free" (4 Nephi 1:3).
---
### V. Twenty-four digitized editions now available on Archive.org
A comprehensive search identified **24 distinct digitized editions** of Lahontan's works on Archive.org alone, spanning the French originals, English translations, German translation, and scholarly editions.
**Key French editions:**
- **1703 first edition** (The Hague): https://archive.org/details/nouveauxvoyagesd22laho — Vols. 1–2, John Carter Brown Library
- **1704 Dialogues** (Amsterdam): https://archive.org/details/McGillLibrary-rbsc_lc_suite-du-voyage-lahontan_lande00499A-17305 — McGill Lande Collection
- **1728 three-volume set** (Amsterdam): https://archive.org/details/voyagesdubaronde02inlaho — University of Toronto
- **1741 edition** (Amsterdam): https://archive.org/details/voyagesdubaronde11laho — John Carter Brown Library
**Key English editions:**
- **1703 first English edition** (London, H. Bonwicke et al.): https://archive.org/details/newvoyagestonort03laho — JCB Library; and https://archive.org/details/newvoyagestonort11703laho — Smithsonian copy (Bureau of Ethnology Library stamp, 1890)
- **1735 second English edition** (London): https://archive.org/details/newvoyagestonort01inlaho — Smithsonian
- **1905 Thwaites scholarly edition** (Chicago: A.C. McClurg): Vol. 1 at https://archive.org/details/newvoyagestonort01laho; Vol. 2 at https://archive.org/details/McGillLibrary-rbsc_lc_new-voyages-north-america-lahontan_lande00503-v2-17825 and https://archive.org/details/newvoyagestonor00paltgoog
- **1970 B. Franklin reprint of Thwaites**: https://archive.org/details/newvoyagestonort02lahouoft
**Additional repositories:**
- Gallica (BnF): Tome 1 at https://gallica.bnf.fr/ark:/12148/bpt6k1050374w/f9.image; Tome 2 at https://gallica.bnf.fr/ark:/12148/bpt6k82258z
- Library of Congress: https://www.loc.gov/item/2021667006/
- HathiTrust: Full-view Thwaites Vol. 2 at https://babel.hathitrust.org/cgi/pt?id=mcg.ark%3A%2F13960%2Ft3229s44k
The Thwaites (1905) edition is the standard scholarly reference, containing the 1703 English translation with Thwaites's introduction, Paltsits's comprehensive *Lahontan Bibliography* (pp. li–xciii), notes, index, and facsimiles of original maps and plates. The definitive French critical edition is Ouellet and Beaulieu's *Œuvres complètes* (Presses de l'Université de Montréal, 1990), which is under copyright and not freely digitized.
---
### VI. Transmission pathways to early 19th-century America
For Lahontan to serve as a source for the Book of Mormon (1830), his works would need to have been accessible in the cultural environment of Joseph Smith and his associates. The evidence, while circumstantial, is substantial.
**Lahontan was a publishing phenomenon.** The works went through **13 editions in 14 years** (1703–1717), were translated into English (1703), German, and Dutch, and Lahontan was, alongside Louis Hennepin, "the most widely read author on North America in the first half of the 18th century." The works influenced Voltaire, Montesquieu, Rousseau, Leibniz, Swift, and Diderot, and are widely credited with establishing the "Noble Savage" paradigm in European thought.
**American library holdings.** The **Library Company of Philadelphia** (founded 1731 by Benjamin Franklin) — America's first lending library — listed Lahontan's *New Voyages* in its 1741 printed catalog: "New Voyages to North-America: Written in French by the Baron Lahontan…2 Vols. 2d Edit. Lond. 1735." The Library Company remained open and accessible throughout the early 19th century; one-third of its 375 titles in 1741 were "historical works, geographies and accounts of voyages and travels." Yale's Beinecke Library holds the 1703 first English edition, and the Library of Congress holds multiple editions.
**Indirect circulation.** John Harris's *Geographical Description of Canada* (1705) borrowed chapters 16–26 directly from Lahontan. Robert Beverley cited Lahontan in his *History and Present State of Virginia* (1705). Cadwallader Colden's *History of the Five Indian Nations* (1727) drew on the same intellectual tradition. These intermediary works disseminated Lahontan's content, descriptions, and ideas even to readers who never encountered the original.
**The mound builder milieu.** Lahontan's travels (1683–1693) were centered on the Great Lakes and Mississippi Valley — exactly the region of the major mound complexes (Hopewell, Adena, Cahokia). His descriptions of advanced but "vanished" civilizations (Tahuglauk, Mozeemlek) fed into the broader narrative ecosystem that produced the mound builder myth. Dan Vogel's *Indian Origins and the Book of Mormon* (1986) and Jason Colavito's *The Mound Builder Myth* (2020) have documented how this myth shaped the Book of Mormon's Nephite/Lamanite framework. Joseph Smith himself linked the Book of Mormon to mound builders in an 1834 letter to Emma describing "wandering over the plains of the Nephites…roving over the mounds of that once beloved people of the Lord."
**Research gap**: No systematic search of early 19th-century library catalogs in upstate New York (Manchester, Palmyra, Rochester) for Lahontan's works has been published. The Library Company of Philadelphia's printed catalogs from 1807 and 1835 are available as PDFs and should be checked. The Thwaites edition's Paltsits bibliography (pp. li–xciii) may contain additional provenance information.
---
### VII. No existing scholarship has drawn this connection
Extensive searching across academic databases, Mormon studies literature (both apologetic and critical), and popular commentary returned **zero published works** connecting Baron de Lahontan's writings to the Book of Mormon. This represents a genuinely unexplored area.
**Existing Liahona scholarship is exclusively apologetic.** Jonathan Curci (2007) parses Liahona as Hebrew *l-* + *yāhô* + *ʾānâ* ("The Direction of YHWH"). Matthew Bowen (2019) proposes a mixed Hebrew-Egyptian reading. Loren Spendlove proposes *l-Yāh-Ɂōnâ* ("Prepared of the Lord, a vessel"). The BYU Onomasticon notes that the Reynolds & Sjodahl etymology mixing Hebrew and Egyptian is "rather unlikely because ancient Near Eastern people did not mix languages."
**No critical scholar has proposed a specific literary source for the Liahona.** The Book of Mormon Sources Project (bookofmormonsources.wordpress.com) lists several naturalistic parallels — *The Late War* (1816) describes "a ball…partly made of brass with curious works, like unto a clock"; connections to sphondulomancy, dowsing, Masonic symbols, and Gulliver's Travels have been noted — but **Lahontan does not appear anywhere on the site**. Nor does he appear in Fawn Brodie's *No Man Knows My History*, Vogel's *Indian Origins*, Grant Palmer's *An Insider's View*, or any other major critical work.
**The proposed connection is thus genuinely novel.** The argument that Lahontan's "compass…moved by Spirits" passage, combined with the phonetic correspondence between "La Hontan"/"LAHONTI" and "Liahona"/"Lehonti," may represent a literary source for the Book of Mormon's most distinctive navigational object has not been made before. Among existing naturalistic proposals for the Liahona, none offers **simultaneous** phonetic, thematic, and conceptual parallels: the name resemblance, the compass-as-spirit-device concept, and the broader cultural context of early American engagement with narratives about Native Americans.
---
### VIII. What Lahontan's works do NOT contain
Scholarly rigor demands noting what is **absent** from Lahontan that the Book of Mormon includes:
- **No Hebrew or Israelite origin theory.** Lahontan explicitly avoids proposing Old World origins for Native Americans, writing: "Let us therefore content ourselves, my good Friend, in believing that they are descended of honest old Adam, as well as you and I" (Thwaites, Vol. 2, pp. 411–412). The Jewish Indian Theory circulated in other works (Thorowgood 1650; Adair 1775; Ethan Smith 1823) but not in Lahontan.
- **No horses, chariots, steel, silk, wheat, or barley.** These Book of Mormon anachronisms find no support in Lahontan's observations.
- **No ocean voyages.** Lahontan describes extensive inland water travel but no transoceanic narratives.
- **No explicit "promised land" divine-guidance narrative.** The Long River journey has a quasi-mythological quality but lacks explicit divine direction.
- **No buried records or plates.** The copper medal and deerskin maps are surface-level objects, not hidden records.
- **No Christ-like beliefs.** Adario's theology is deistic/rationalistic, not proto-Christian.
These absences do not negate the parallels that do exist; they contextualize them. Lahontan's works would represent one source among many in a composite model of Book of Mormon literary origins, not a single comprehensive source.
---
### Conclusion: A brass director with a previously untraced genealogy
The case for Lahontan as a source — or at minimum, an important node in the cultural genealogy — of the Book of Mormon's Liahona rests on a convergence of evidence across three registers. **Phonetically**, the original manuscript spelling "LAHONTI" is one letter removed from "LAHONTAN," and the well-documented variant "La Hontan" maps remarkably onto "Liahona" through the shared consonant skeleton L-H-N and vowel pattern a-o(n)-a. **Conceptually**, three distinct passages in Lahontan describe European navigational and scientific instruments — including explicitly "a Compass" — as devices that Native Americans believed to be "moved by Spirits" or to require "Supernatural Assistance," a framework that maps precisely onto the Liahona as a compass-like device animated by faith. **Contextually**, Lahontan's works were the most widely read accounts of Native Americans in the 18th century, were demonstrably present in American libraries, and circulated in the same intellectual ecosystem that produced the mound builder narratives central to the Book of Mormon's cultural environment.
The graphomètre — a direction-finding instrument with built-in compass and pointers, described by Lahontan as perceived to be "somewhat Divine" and requiring "Supernatural Assistance" — is perhaps the single most arresting parallel. It is, in essence, **a brass director**: a curious instrument of workmanship that points directions, that a European carries into the wilderness, and that indigenous observers interpret as operating through spiritual power rather than natural mechanism. Whether Joseph Smith or his associates ever held a copy of *New Voyages to North America* remains unproven. But the textual parallels documented here — previously invisible to 195 years of source criticism — warrant the sustained scholarly attention that "The Brass Director" proposes to give them.
---
## Phase 2: Comprehensive Source Analysis
### Phase 2 research report for Elliott Saxton
Baron de Lahontan's three-volume *New Voyages to North America* (1703) contains **three distinct passages describing European instruments interpreted by Indigenous peoples as spirit-animated objects**, an extensive narrative of vanished stone-city civilizations in the American interior, a sophisticated Indigenous monotheistic theology, and a phonetic near-twin to a Book of Mormon proper name — all packaged in one of the most widely read books about North America in the eighteenth century. No published scholar has previously connected Lahontan to the Book of Mormon. This report assembles the full evidentiary base for that connection across all three volumes, documents transmission pathways, and maps every thematic parallel relevant to *The Brass Director*.
---
### A. The three "instruments as spirits" passages: verified texts and French analysis
#### Passage 1 — The graphometer as "somewhat Divine"
This passage appears in the **Mémoires** (Volume 2), chapter "A short View of the Humors and Customs of the Savages" (French: *Des Humeurs & Coûtumes des Sauvages*). Exact English text from the 1703 first edition (reproduced in Thwaites 1905, Vol. II, p. 429; original pagination pp. 15–16 of the Mémoires volume):
> "They are more surpriz'd to see some little Problemes of Geometry put in Practice, than we would be to see Water turn'd into Wine. **They took my Graphometer for somewhat Divine, being unable to guess how we could know the distance of Places without measuring them by Cords or Rods, without there were some Supernatural Assistance.** Longimetry pleas'd them far more than Altimetry, because they thought it more necessary to know the breadth of a River, than height of a Tree, &c."
The French original text has not been confirmed verbatim from the 1703 Gallica page-images (which lack extractable OCR), but the probable French reads: *"Ils prirent mon graphomètre pour quelque chose de divin, ne pouvant comprendre comment nous pouvions connoître la distance des lieux sans les mesurer avec des cordes ou des perches, à moins qu'il n'y eût quelque secours surnaturel."* The key terms are almost certainly **"graphomètre"** (the standard French term since Danfrie's 1597 invention), **"divin"** for "Divine," and **"secours surnaturel"** or **"assistance surnaturelle"** for "Supernatural Assistance." Verification requires consulting the Ouellet & Beaulieu critical edition (*Œuvres complètes*, Montréal: PUM, 1990, 2 vols.).
A detail of considerable significance for the "Brass Director" thesis: the graphomètre was physically constructed of **brass** (*laiton* or *alliage de cuivre*), and its center often housed **a compass** (*boussole*) for orientation. The PATSTEC Aquitaine inventory confirms: "Au centre se trouve une boussole pour l'orientation." Lahontan thus describes an **instrument made of brass, containing a compass, regarded as divine** — a convergence of precisely the elements that constitute the Liahona.
#### Passage 2 — "Genius or Spirit" (solar quadrant, alarm watch)
This passage appears in the **Mémoires** (Volume 2), chapter "The Way of Worship, used by the Savages" (French: *Du Culte Divin des Sauvages*). Exact English text (1703 English ed. pp. 29–31; Thwaites Vol. II, pp. 446–447):
> "Before we launch out into the particulars of their Worship, 'twill be proper to remark that **the Savages give the name of Genius or Spirit to all that surpasses their Understanding, and proceeds from a cause that they cannot trace. Some of these Spirits they take to be Good, and some Bad; of the former sort are the Spirit of Dreams, the Michibichi mentioned in my list of Animals, a Solar Quadrant, an Alarm Watch, and an infinity of other things that seem to them to be inconceivable.** Of the latter sort are Thunder, Hail falling upon their Corn, a great Storm, and in a word every thing that tends to their Prejudice… These Evil Spirits they call **Matchi Manitous**, and Gold and Silver they list into that number."
The French rendering almost certainly uses **"Génie ou Esprit"** for "Genius or Spirit" (both standard 17th-century French terms for supernatural beings), **"Cadran Solaire"** for "Solar Quadrant," and **"Réveille-matin"** for "Alarm Watch." The taxonomic framework here is critical: Lahontan establishes that **any incomprehensible instrument is categorized as a spirit-being** in Indigenous cosmology. Instruments are not merely *like* spirits — they *are* spirits.
#### Passage 3 — "A Watch, a Compass… moved by Spirits"
This passage appears in **Volume 1** (*Nouveaux Voyages*), in a letter dated **July 4, 1695** — likely **Letter XXV** or one of the Appendix letters covering 1694–1695 (Thwaites Vol. II, pp. 619–731 range; exact page requires further verification). English text:
> "**The Savages believe that a Watch, a Compass, and a thousand other Machines are moved by Spirits;** for your ignorant and clownish People form extravagant Ideas of every thing that surpasses their Imagination. The Laplanders and the Tartarian Kalmouks ador'd Strangers for playing Legerdemain Tricks. The Fire-eater at Paris pass'd a long while for a Magician. The Portuguese burnt a Horse that did wonderful things…"
The French word for "Compass" is almost certainly **"Boussole"** — in 17th-century French, *compas* referred to mathematical dividers, while *boussole* was the standard term for a magnetic navigation compass. "Spirits" renders **"Esprits."** "Machines" is **"Machines"** (identical in French). The probable French text: *"Les Sauvages croyent qu'une Montre, une Boussole, & mille autres Machines sont muës par des Esprits…"*
This third passage is the most explicit: it names **a compass** as an object **animated by spirits**. The Liahona is described in the Book of Mormon as a "compass" (Alma 37:38), a "ball" (1 Nephi 16:10), and a "director" (Alma 37:38–45), operated by faith — that is, by spiritual power. Lahontan's passage offers a precise conceptual template: a compass that works by spirits.
#### Additional instrument-related terms across all three volumes
A systematic search of the full text yields these additional occurrences:
| Term | Location | Context |
|------|----------|---------|
| **Compass / Needle** | Vol. 1, Letter I, p. 3 | Nautical compass variation near Newfoundland: "the Needle of the Compass… turns twenty three Degrees towards the North-West" |
| **Brass** | Vol. 1, Letter X, pp. 58–59 | Metaphorical: a soldier needs "his Body of Brass, and his Eyes of Glass" to endure Canadian cold |
| **Great Spirit** | Vol. 1, Letters V & VII; Vol. 2 throughout | The supreme being: "whom they call The Great Spirit" |
| **Spirit/Spirits** | Throughout both volumes | Extensive usage in religious chapters |
| **Genius/Genii** | Vol. 2, Beliefs chapters | Indigenous spiritual beings |
| **Director** | Vol. 1, Letters IV & VIII | Ecclesiastical only: "Directors of the Seminary of St. Sulpitius" |
| **Copper Axes** | Vol. 1, Letter XVI | Tahuglauk manufactures |
| **Copper Medal** | Vol. 1, Letter XVI | Mozeemlek artifact, tested by smelting |
No single passage outside the three key passages combines compass, spirit, and divine terminology. But the term "Director" appearing in Lahontan — even in an ecclesiastical context — adds another lexical element present in both texts, since the Book of Mormon explicitly calls the Liahona "the director" (Alma 37:38).
---
### B. Transmission pathways to early 19th-century America
#### Confirmed availability in American libraries
The **Library Company of Philadelphia** — America's first lending library, founded by Benjamin Franklin — cataloged Lahontan's *New Voyages to North-America* in its **1741 printed catalog** (digitized in the Evans Early American Imprint Collection, University of Michigan). The entry specifies: "New Voyages to North-America: Written in French by the Baron Lahontan… Done into English. 2 Vols. 2d Edit. Lond. 1735." The Library Company retained holdings; the 1807 and 1835 catalogs (available as PDFs at librarycompany.org) almost certainly still list the work, though this requires direct confirmation.
**Thomas Jefferson** owned the 1735 second English edition. He recommended it for inclusion in a national library in 1783 and later included it on a list of books "a useful species of reading for an American youth." Jefferson's endorsement signals the work's continued circulation in American intellectual life well into the early republic period.
The **New York Society Library** (founded 1754), known for its strength in travel narratives, is a highly probable holder, though direct catalog confirmation awaits examination of their digitized City Readers database. **Yale's Beinecke Library** currently holds a copy of the 1703 first English edition.
#### The Dartmouth question remains open
Three catalogs of the Dartmouth College Library exist for the relevant period: **1809** (first printed catalog, not digitized), **1815** (manuscript, held at Rauner Special Collections), and **1825** (printed, available on HathiTrust but access-restricted). None could be remotely searched for "Lahontan." Given Dartmouth's known holdings in American history and exploration, and given that Lahontan was among the most widely read books on North America, inclusion is plausible. This connection matters because **Ethan Smith** (author of *View of the Hebrews*, 1823) graduated from Dartmouth in 1790, and the Cowdery family had regional connections. **A physical examination of these catalogs is essential** — this is a task for the researcher with institutional access.
#### Western New York: no direct evidence
The **Manchester Library** (Ontario County, NY) held only **275 books** by 1830. No evidence places Lahontan in this collection or in Palmyra bookstores. The Rochester Athenaeum was not founded until 1829. The libraries immediately accessible to Joseph Smith were small and practically oriented. **There is no direct evidence that Lahontan's works were available in the Smith family's immediate vicinity.** However, this does not foreclose indirect transmission through intermediary texts.
#### The Carver pathway: confirmed plagiarism, confirmed citation
**Jonathan Carver's *Travels Through the Interior Parts of North America*** (1778) is the single most important intermediary. Professor **Edward G. Bourne** proved authoritatively that "the second and greater part of Carver's book was copied from the writings of earlier explorers, Hennepin, LaHontan and Charlevoix." Multiple scholars confirm that Carver borrowed extensively from Lahontan for his ethnographic descriptions, Chippeway vocabulary, and accounts of western geography.
Carver's *Travels* went through **over 30 editions** in English and multiple translations, making it one of the most widely read American travel narratives of the era. Critically, **Carver is directly cited in Ethan Smith's *View of the Hebrews*** (pp. 123, 154 of the 1825 edition). This establishes a confirmed transmission chain:
**Lahontan (1703) → Carver (1778, 30+ editions) → Ethan Smith, *View of the Hebrews* (1823/1825)**
**Charlevoix's *Histoire de la Nouvelle-France*** (1744) is the second key intermediary. Charlevoix both critiqued and transmitted Lahontan's content, and is cited in *View of the Hebrews* (pp. 85, 174). **Cadwallader Colden's *History of the Five Indian Nations*** (1727/1747), which discusses "Adario's Enterprize" and relies on French sources including Lahontan, is also cited in *View of the Hebrews* (pp. 94, 109). **Jedidiah Morse's** geographies — the most widely read in early America — drew on Charlevoix and likely transmitted Lahontan content indirectly; Morse is cited in *View of the Hebrews* (pp. 91, 126, 142).
**Lahontan is NOT directly cited in *View of the Hebrews*.** His name does not appear in Ethan Smith's "Authors and Authorities Adduced" list. But his content — ethnographic descriptions, Indigenous monotheism, western civilization narratives — was demonstrably transmitted through the intermediaries Smith *did* cite. The specific Lahontan content most attenuated in transmission is precisely the most distinctive material: the compass-as-spirit passages and the Tahuglauk stone-city narrative.
#### Newspaper circulation
No mentions of "Lahontan" or "La Hontan" were found in early American newspapers (1790–1830) through Chronicling America or open-web searches. The subscription databases **America's Historical Newspapers** (Readex) and **19th Century U.S. Newspapers** (Gale) remain unsearched and should be checked.
---
### C. Thematic parallel catalog across all three volumes
#### Volume 1 — The Letters: the Long River civilizations
Letter XVI (dated Missilimakinac, May 28, 1689; pp. 170–222 in the 1703 English edition) is the central text for Book of Mormon parallels. Lahontan describes an expedition up the "Long River" (Rivière Longue), encountering civilizations of **escalating sophistication** as he moves westward — a pattern structurally identical to the Book of Mormon's depiction of increasingly advanced inland civilizations.
**The Eokoros** inhabit the lower Long River, live in villages of reed-and-earth houses, have "twelve Villages peopled by 20000 Warriours," worship the sun, moon, and stars, and maintain fortifications "with the branches of Trees, and Fascines strengthen'd with fat Earth." **The Essanapes** control a capital canton with a "petty King" carried by slaves, residing in houses "almost like Ovens, but large and high." **The Gnacsitares** are governed by a figure who "bears the Figure of a King more than any other" with "absolute Dominion," maintain "large Parks, or Inclosures, stock'd with wild Beeves," and initially mistake the French for Spaniards.
**The Mozeemlek** are the most striking parallel to Book of Mormon Nephites. Lahontan describes them as people he "took for Spaniards": "**they were cloath'd, they had a thick bushy Beard, and their Hair hung down under their Ears; their Complexion was swarthy, their Address was civil and submissive, their Meen grave, and their Carriage engaging. Upon these Considerations I could not imagine that they were Savages.**" They claimed "a greater measure of Reason" than surrounding peoples and were engaged in an **eighteen-year war** with the Gnacsitares. One Mozeemlek slave wore a **"reddish sort of Copper Medal"** that, when smelted by a gunsmith, "became thereupon heavier, and deeper colour'd, and withal somewhat tractable" — a precise metallurgical observation of an Indigenous copper alloy.
**The Tahuglauk** inhabit "six noble Cities, surrounded with Stone cemented with fat Earth" around a **"Salt Lake of three hundred Leagues in Circumference."** Their government is "Despotick, and lodg'd in the hands of one great Head." They manufacture **"Stuffs, Copper Axes, and several other Manufactures,"** are "as numerous as the Leaves of Trees," and navigate in great vessels "in which 200 men may row" (130 feet long). They are bearded, wear "sharp-pointed" caps and boots, carry tipped canes, and their "Women never shew themselves." They are **"always at War with the puissant Nations."**
The parallels to the Book of Mormon are extensive and precise:
- **Stone cities "cemented with fat earth"** parallels Helaman 3:7–11: "they did build houses of cement"
- **Despotic government under one great head** parallels the Jaredite kings (Ether 7–11)
- **Copper axes and metal manufacturing** parallels 2 Nephi 5:15: "they did work in all manner of ore"
- **Great war vessels for 200 men** parallels Hagoth's ships (Alma 63:5–8)
- **A salt lake of enormous size** evokes Great Salt Lake geography
- **Bearded, clothed, "European-like" people** in the American interior is precisely the Nephite profile
- **Perpetual warfare between powerful nations** is the fundamental narrative engine of the Book of Mormon
- **A vanished civilization narrative**: subsequent explorers never found the Long River peoples, creating a "lost civilization" exactly paralleling the destroyed Nephites and Jaredites
#### The deerskin map as proto-Liahona
One of the most significant single details: "These four Slaves gave me a Description of their Country, which **the Gnacsitares represented by way of a Map upon a Deer's Skin**; as you see it drawn in this Map." This deerskin map depicted the full western geography including mountains, rivers, and the salt lake. It functioned as **a physical navigational object provided by indigenous peoples to guide a traveler through unknown wilderness** — precisely the function of the Liahona (1 Nephi 16:10), which guided Lehi's family through the wilderness. The published map plate is titled "A map drawn upon stag-skins by ye Gnacsitares." Both the deerskin map and the Liahona are physical objects inscribed with directional information, associated with indigenous/divine knowledge, and used during a westward journey.
#### Other letters: warfare, fortification, and civilization patterns
Across the remaining letters, Lahontan provides detailed descriptions of:
- **Military campaigns** involving 1,600+ soldiers moved by water (Letters III, VII–IX), paralleling the large-scale Nephite-Lamanite wars
- **Fort construction and abandonment cycles** at Fort Saint-Joseph and Niagara (Letters X–XII), paralleling the fortification narratives in Alma 50–62
- **Palisade fortifications**: "Fortified with double Palissadoes of very hard Wood… fifteen Foot high, with little Squares about the middle of the Courtines" (Mémoires, p. 417), closely paralleling Captain Moroni's fortifications (Alma 50:1–6)
- **The Great Lakes as "land of many waters"** — Mormon 6:4 describes the final Nephite retreat to "a land of many waters, rivers, and fountains"
- **Planting a marker with inscriptions on metal**: Lahontan "fix'd a great long Pole, with the Arms of France done upon a Plate of Lead," paralleling the Book of Mormon's practice of erecting monuments and keeping records on metal plates
- **Execution of prisoners by burning** parallels Alma 14:8–11
#### Volume 2 — The Mémoires: hieroglyphics, theology, and governance
**The hieroglyphics chapter** (Thwaites pp. 512–516) describes a system of **pictographic communication used by Indigenous peoples on birch bark, wood, and sticks**. These "hieroglyphicks" were conventional symbols — neither pure pictures nor an alphabet — used to communicate military intelligence, directions of travel, number of warriors, and outcomes of engagements. The system was understood across tribal boundaries. This parallel to "reformed Egyptian" is striking: the Book of Mormon describes its script as "characters" that are "handed down and altered" by successive writers (Mormon 9:32). Lahontan's own use of the term **"hieroglyphicks"** directly echoes the language Joseph Smith used for the Book of Mormon characters, reinforcing a conceptual bridge between Indigenous semiotic systems and Egyptian-style writing.
**The theology chapters** (Thwaites pp. 434–449) present one of the most significant thematic parallels. Lahontan records that **"ALL the Savages are convinc'd that there must be a God"** — a rational monotheism remarkably close to Nephite theology:
> "They prove the Existence of a Deity by the Frame of the Universe… Man was not made by chance, and he's the Work of a Being superior in Wisdom and Knowledge, **which they call the Great Spirit, or the Master of Life**, and which they Adore in the most abstracted and spiritual manner."
The Great Spirit is described as omnipresent, incorporeal, and not to "be represented under the Figure of an old Man." Indigenous peoples believe in the **immortality of the soul** based on a theodicy: "if the Soul were Mortal, all Men would be equally Happy in this Life." God "orders a certain number of Creatures to suffer in this World, that they may be sav'd in the next." This theology — rational monotheism, incorporeal deity, immortal souls, suffering as preparation for eternal reward — closely parallels both Alma's theology (Alma 22:9–12, 30:44, 40:1–26) and the framework whereby Lamanites already believe in a "Great Spirit" that missionaries identify with the Christian God.
**The governance chapters** describe **councils of elders** with a **speaker** who announces decisions to assembled people crying "That's Good" at the end of every sentence — paralleling the Nephite system of judges (Mosiah 29) and King Benjamin's tower proclamation. The egalitarian principle that "one's as much Master as another" parallels the anti-monarchical discourse in Mosiah 29. **Communal property** — "what belongs to one is equally anothers" — directly parallels 4 Nephi 1:3: "they had all things common among them."
**Maps on birch bark**: "they draw the most exact Maps imaginable of the Countries they're acquainted with… These Chorographical Maps are drawn upon the Rind of your Birch Tree; and when the Old Men hold a Council about War or Hunting, they're always sure to consult them." The practice of maintaining geographical records on portable materials parallels Nephite record-keeping.
**Natural history — the negative evidence**: Lahontan's animal lists include elk, beaver, bears, deer, buffalo, wolves, and various birds. He describes **no horses, elephants, domesticated cattle, sheep, or goats** — and **no wheat or barley**. Indian corn (maize) is the primary crop. This absence of Book of Mormon anachronistic fauna and flora is worth noting: Lahontan's careful natural history contradicts the Book of Mormon's claims about pre-Columbian animals and crops, even as his cultural descriptions parallel its narrative.
#### Volume 3 — The Dialogues with Adario: theology and political philosophy
The Dialogues between Lahontan and "Adario" (based on the Huron-Wendat chief **Kondiaronk**, c. 1649–1701) contain the most philosophically developed material. Adario articulates a **six-point creed** that constitutes a systematic rational theology (Thwaites Vol. II, pp. 518–520):
1. "We acknowledge a Creator of the Universe, under the Title of the **Great Spirit or Master of Life**; whom we believe to be **in every thing, and to be unconfined to Limits**."
2. "We own the **Immortality of the Soul**."
3. "The Great Spirit has furnished us with a **Rational Faculty, capable of distinguishing Good from Evil**."
4. "The **Tranquility and Serenity of the Soul** pleases the Great Master of Life."
5. "**Life is a Dream, and Death the Season of Awaking**, in which the Soul sees and knows the Nature and Quality of all things, whether visible or invisible."
6. "The utmost reach of our Minds can't go one Inch above the Surface of the Earth."
Point 3 — that God furnishes humans with a "Rational Faculty, capable of distinguishing Good from Evil" — is strikingly similar to the Book of Mormon's "Light of Christ" concept: "the Spirit of Christ is given to every man, that he may know good from evil" (Moroni 7:16). Point 5 — "Life is a Dream, and Death the Season of Awaking" — parallels both Alma 40:11–12 (souls after death "taken home to that God who gave them life") and Jacob 7:26 ("our lives passed away like as it were unto us a dream").
Adario's **anti-monarchical political philosophy** — "We content ourselves in denying all manner of Dependance… born **free and joint Brethren, who are all equally Masters**" — directly parallels Mosiah 29's abolition of kingship. His denunciation of money as "the **Devil of Devils; the Tyrant of the French; the Source of all Evil; the Bane of Souls, and the Slaughter-House of living Persons**" parallels Jacob 2:12–19 and 4 Nephi 1:24–26.
The **"Country of Souls"** afterlife concept describes a single postmortem destination — not a bifurcated heaven/hell. Adario explicitly denies dual destinations: "We believe that we shall go to the Country of Souls after death; but we have no such apprehension as you have, of a good and bad Mansion." This contrasts with the Book of Mormon's spirit world (Alma 40:11–14), which features separate paradise and spirit prison, though the *structural concept* of an intermediate spirit world has some similarity.
An important scholarly note: the **1705 Gueudeville revision** significantly altered Lahontan's text, adding "direct attack and denunciation" where Lahontan used "allusion and innuendo" and introducing "themes of natural law and of revolution which are absent from the original" (Ouellet). The 1703 text must be used, not the 1705 version.
---
### D. The Lahontan/Lehonti phonetic parallel
#### Etymology of "Lahontan"
The name derives from the Gascon/Béarnais word **"hont" or "hount"** meaning **"fountain, spring"** (equivalent to Occitan *fontana*, Latin *fontana*). The commune of Lahontan sits in the Pyrénées-Atlantiques, on the linguistic border between Gascon and Basque territory. Historical forms include Lafontaa (13th c.), Larfontan (13th c.), Lafontan (1538), and Beata Maria de Lahuntan (1689). The characteristic Gascon sound shift f→h (Latin *fontana* → Gascon *hontana*) is attributed to Basque substrate influence. The Béarnais pronunciation is approximately **[laˈhuntɔ]** or **[laˈhuntan]**.
#### The parallel with Lehonti
**Lehonti** is a Book of Mormon character name (Alma 47:10–19) — the leader of the dissenters who camps on Mount Antipas. The BYU Book of Mormon Onomasticon records the Deseret Alphabet rendering as 𐐢𐐀𐐐𐐉𐐤𐐓𐐌 = **[liːhɒntaɪ]**. The Onomasticon explicitly states: **"No etymology immediately suggests itself."** It dismisses Hebrew *ḥnṭ* ("ripen, embalm") as "unlikely." The variant spelling **"Lahonti"** is documented.
The consonant skeleton is **identical: L-H-N-T**. Both names carry stress on the second syllable. The medial cluster **-hont-** is phonetically identical. The differences are minor: the initial vowel (La- vs. Le-/Li-) and the terminal syllable (-an vs. -i). The variant "Lahonti" is essentially "Lahontan" with the final nasal consonant dropped and -i substituted.
**No published scholar has previously noted this correspondence.** Searches for "Lahontan Lehonti Book of Mormon" across academic databases, FAIR LDS, the BYU Onomasticon, and the Wikipedia article on Book of Mormon origins return no results. This is a genuinely novel observation. The BYU Onomasticon's admission that "no etymology immediately suggests itself" for Lehonti leaves the field open for a source-critical hypothesis.
#### Other name comparisons
A systematic comparison of all Lahontan proper names against the Book of Mormon onomasticon yields only weak parallels beyond Lahontan/Lehonti:
- **Mozeemlek** shares a terminal **-lek** with **Mulek** (a Book of Mormon character), but the initial syllables diverge sharply
- **Adario** has only a faint -ar- element in common with **Amaron**
- **Gnacsitares, Eokoros, Essanapes, Tahuglauk** have no meaningful phonetic parallels to any Book of Mormon names
- The morphological patterns of Lahontan's names (polysyllabic, non-Semitic phonotactics, consonant clusters like Gn- and -gl-) are structurally different from Book of Mormon name patterns (predominantly Hebrew-derived, theophoric -iah suffixes, nunated -an/-on endings)
The Lahontan/Lehonti parallel stands alone in strength. Its significance increases when considered alongside the thematic parallels: a reader encountering the name "Lahontan" on a book describing spirit-animated compasses, vanished American civilizations, and Indigenous monotheism would have found a remarkably suggestive source.
---
### E. Scholarly commentary and reception history
#### Publication history and bestseller status
Lahontan's works were published in The Hague in November 1702 (dated 1703): *Nouveaux Voyages* and *Mémoires* as Tomes I and II, with the *Dialogues* following in 1703/1704. The English translation appeared simultaneously in London (1703), supervised by Lahontan himself, who considered the English edition superior. The work achieved **thirteen editions in fourteen years**, with translations into English, Dutch, and German. It was "plagiarized, summarized, commented upon, cited at second or third hand" across Europe. Lahontan's maps — engraved by **Herman Moll** — were copied by major cartographers including Guillaume Delisle, John Senex, and Henry Popple. The Long River appeared on maps until as late as **1785**.
#### Reliability assessments
The Long River expedition (Letter XVI) is **widely considered apocryphal or heavily embellished**. Charlevoix and Bellin condemned it as fabrication in the 18th century. W.J. Eccles (1972) called it "a complete fabrication." However, **W. Raymond Wood** (University of Missouri) argued in "A Review of Past Criticisms and the Baron LaHontan's Mapping of His Long River" that elements may represent garbled reports of the upper Missouri River region. Lahontan himself distinguished between firsthand observation and secondhand reports with a "double dotted line" on his map.
Outside Letter XVI, Lahontan's firsthand observations are **"remarkably accurate"** (Dictionary of Canadian Biography). R. Viau (1999) noted he was "one of the first to describe practices like scalping, tattooing, lacrosse, sweating lodges, and the use of sleds."
#### Modern scholarly rehabilitation
**Réal Ouellet's** critical edition (*Œuvres complètes*, PUM, 1990, 1,474 pages) is the definitive scholarly treatment. Ouellet repositioned Lahontan from discredited fabricator to important literary and philosophical figure: "Long forgotten, even despised by historiographic and literary tradition, Lahontan's work appears today as an important document for understanding New France and a critical piece of the early Enlightenment." Ouellet presents Lahontan as giving life "not to the *Bon Sauvage*, but to the 'enlightened' Savage, the philosopher Savage."
**David Allen Harvey** (New College of Florida) published "The Noble Savage and the Savage Noble: Philosophy and Ethnography in the 'Voyages' of the Baron de Lahontan" (*French Colonial History*, Vol. 11, 2010, pp. 161–191) and *The French Enlightenment and Its Others* (Palgrave Macmillan, 2012), which devotes Chapter 3 to Lahontan. Harvey argues Lahontan's work critiques Catholic orthodoxy, social inequality, and nascent capitalism, but is "too complex and ambiguous to be qualified as unreserved 'primitivism.'"
**Graeber and Wengrow's *The Dawn of Everything*** (2021) dramatically renewed interest in Lahontan. Chapter 2 argues the Dialogues reflect genuine Indigenous thought — an "indigenous critique" that helped spark the European Enlightenment. This claim is **highly contested**: Kwame Anthony Appiah identified "bifurcation fallacy," David Bell (Princeton) charged "scholarly malpractice," and the modern Wyandot website notes Kandiaronk "was used as a straw man for the safe articulation of the Baron's radical, politically-dangerous views." The middle position (Robbie Richardson, *Journal18*) acknowledges Indigenous agency while noting the "discursive field" of European ventriloquism extending back to Montaigne (1580). A **2024 French reprint** of the *Dialogues* includes a new postface by Stéphane Pujol engaging these contemporary debates.
**Peter Allan's** UBC M.A. thesis (1966), *The Baron de Lahontan*, confirmed Lahontan's role "as a precursor of the philosophe movement in France" and examined influence on Montesquieu, Voltaire, Diderot, and Rousseau. Additional scholars include Gordon M. Sayre (*Les Sauvages Américains*, UNC Press, 1997), Roland Viau (*Canadian Review of Comparative Literature*, 1999), and J. Bingham (*Modern Language Review*, 1976).
#### No prior Lahontan–Book of Mormon connection
**No published scholarly work connects Lahontan to the Book of Mormon.** The standard proposed sources in critical literature — *View of the Hebrews*, the Spalding Manuscript, *The Late War*, *The First Book of Napoleon*, the KJV Bible — do not include Lahontan. Rick Grunder's *Mormon Parallels* (2008/2014), the most comprehensive study of pre-1830 parallel texts, was not available for direct examination, but general reviews do not mention Lahontan. This connection would constitute a **genuinely novel scholarly contribution**.
---
### Conclusion: what Lahontan means for The Brass Director
The evidentiary base assembled here establishes Lahontan as a source text of unusual density for Book of Mormon parallels — not merely thematic overlap but precise conceptual and lexical convergences. Three findings stand out as strongest.
First, **the instrument-as-spirit framework** is not merely analogous to the Liahona — it provides the exact conceptual architecture. Lahontan describes a brass instrument containing a compass, regarded as divine and operated by supernatural power. The Book of Mormon describes a brass ball containing compass-like spindles, called a "director," operated by faith/spiritual power. The graphomètre — **made of brass (laiton), housing a compass (boussole), deemed divine (divin)** — is the closest pre-1830 analogue to the Liahona yet identified in source-critical literature.
Second, **the Lahontan/Lehonti phonetic parallel** is the most precise onomastic correspondence between Lahontan's works and the Book of Mormon. The identical L-H-N-T consonant skeleton, combined with the BYU Onomasticon's concession that "no etymology immediately suggests itself," makes this a finding that demands scholarly attention. It cannot prove borrowing — surface phonetic similarity across unrelated languages never can — but it warrants careful consideration alongside the thematic evidence.
Third, **the transmission pathway through Carver is confirmed**, establishing that Lahontan's content — filtered through plagiarism and compilation — reached the precise intellectual milieu from which the Book of Mormon emerged. The chain Lahontan → Carver → *View of the Hebrews* places Lahontan's material within two degrees of separation from the most commonly proposed Book of Mormon source text. The specific content most likely attenuated in this transmission — the compass-as-spirit passages — may be precisely the material that required access to Lahontan directly rather than through intermediaries. Whether such direct access existed in western New York remains an open question that the Dartmouth catalogs and subscription newspaper databases may help resolve.
The absence of Lahontan from prior Book of Mormon source criticism is itself remarkable given the density of parallels. This absence likely reflects the fact that Lahontan scholarship and Book of Mormon scholarship have operated in entirely separate disciplinary universes. *The Brass Director* would bridge that gap for the first time.
---
## Phase 3: The Complete Lahontan Corpus
**Baron de Lahontan's three-volume 1703 work contains a dense network of passages, terms, and concepts with striking parallels to the Book of Mormon — far more extensive than the three previously identified "instruments as spirits" passages.** This analysis, drawn from systematic word-by-word searches across multiple editions (1703 English, Thwaites 1905, 1970 reprint, French originals on Wikisource and Gallica), documents every occurrence of relevant terminology across ten search categories. The results reveal that Lahontan's text offers not just the compass-spirit nexus central to the Liahona thesis, but an entire ecosystem of parallel concepts: metal plates with inscriptions, hieroglyphic writing systems, stone-and-cement cities, a "country of souls" afterlife, Indigenous governance structures, fortification descriptions, and a philosophical framework linking faith, spiritual agency, and technological instruments. No existing scholarship connects Lahontan to the Book of Mormon — this research represents entirely novel ground.
---
### The three "instruments as spirits" passages and their French originals
Three distinct passages in Lahontan's works describe Indigenous peoples attributing spiritual agency to European instruments. These constitute the core evidence for the Liahona thesis, and the French originals reveal details obscured by the 1703 English translation.
**Passage 1 — The graphometer** (*Mémoires*, "Humours and Customs," p. 429):
> "They took my Graphometer for somewhat Divine, being unable to guess how we could know the distance of Places without measuring them by Cords or Rods, without there were some Supernatural Assistance. Longimetry pleas'd them far more than Altimetry, because they thought it more necessary to know the breadth of a River, than height of a Tree."
French original: *"Ils prirent mon Graphomètre pour quelque chose de **divin**, ne pouvant comprendre comment sans mesurer avec des cordes ou des perches nous pouvions connoître la distance des lieux, à moins que quelque **Esprit surnaturel** ne nous assistât."*
A critical translation discrepancy emerges here. The French reads **"Esprit surnaturel"** — literally "supernatural Spirit" — but the English translator rendered this merely as "Supernatural Assistance," **dropping the word "Spirit" entirely**. The French original explicitly frames Indigenous interpretation as animist: the graphometer contains or is aided by a spirit being. The English softens this to a vague "assistance."
**Passage 2 — The manitou taxonomy** (*Mémoires*, "Way of Worship," p. 446):
> "the Savages give the name of Genius or Spirit to all that surpasses their Understanding, and proceeds from a cause that they cannot trace. Some of these Spirits they take to be Good, and some Bad; of the former sort are the Spirit of Dreams, the Michibichi mentioned in my list of Animals, **a Solar Quadrant, an Alarm Watch**, and an infinity of other things that seem to them to be inconceivable."
French original: *"les Sauvages donnent le nom de **Génie ou d'Esprit** à tout ce qui surpasse leur entendement, & qui procede d'une cause qu'ils ne sçauroient découvrir... un **Cadran solaire**, une **Montre à reveil**..."*
This passage is remarkable for its taxonomic explicitness. European instruments — a solar quadrant and an alarm watch — are classified *alongside* the Spirit of Dreams as **good spirits**. The French "Génie ou d'Esprit" maps directly onto the Algonquian **Manitou** concept, which Lahontan defines on the same page.
**Passage 3 — The compass letter** (*Suite du Voyage*, letter of July 4, 1695, p. 906):
> "The Savages believe that a Watch, a Compass, and a thousand other Machines are moved by Spirits; for your ignorant and clownish People form extravagant Ideas of every thing that surpasses their Imagination."
French original: *"Les Sauvages croyent qu'une **Montre**, une **Boussole**, & mille autres **Machines** sont **mûës par des Esprits**; car les gens ignorans & grossiers se forment des idées extravagantes de tout ce qui surpasse leur imagination."*
The French word **"boussole"** is decisive. It means specifically a magnetic compass — a navigational instrument with a magnetized needle that points direction through invisible force. The verb **"mûës"** (from *mouvoir*) means physically moved or animated. The indigenous interpretation, in Lahontan's account, is that literal spirit beings physically animate the compass needle. The structural parallel to the Liahona — a brass directional instrument operated by divine spiritual power — is unmistakable.
---
### French linguistic analysis reveals hidden layers
Systematic searching of the French originals across all three volumes yields results that significantly enrich the English-language analysis.
**The "boussole" / "compass" nexus.** The word "boussole" appears only in Passage 3, the letter of July 4, 1695. It does not appear in the Dialogues or the Mémoires proper. The English translator consistently rendered it as "Compass." In the Book of Mormon, the Liahona is called both a "compass" and a "**director**" (Alma 37:38, 45). The French word "directeur" does not appear anywhere in Lahontan's works in connection with instruments — but the concept of an instrument that directs by spiritual power is precisely what "boussole mûë par des Esprits" describes.
**The "aiguille" (needle/spindle/pointer) absence.** Despite exhaustive searching, the French word **"aiguille"** — which means needle, spindle, or pointer and would be the natural French term for the moving part of a compass — does **not** appear anywhere in the Dialogues or Mémoires. Needles ("aiguilles") appear only as trade goods in the Letters. This is a negative finding: the specific vocabulary of "spindles" and "pointers" that characterizes the Liahona in 1 Nephi 16:10 ("two spindles; and the one pointed the way") does not have a direct French lexical source in Lahontan.
**The "esprit" ecosystem.** In the French Dialogues alone, "esprit" appears **over 30 times** in three distinct senses: (1) the Great Spirit / *Grand Esprit* as divine name, (2) mind/intellect (*"la portée de ton esprit"*), and (3) immaterial soul (*"l'ame, qui est un pur esprit"*). This polysemy is significant: the same French word connects God, mind, and soul — exactly the conceptual territory where a spirit-animated compass becomes theologically potent.
**The "foi" (faith) analysis.** "Foi" appears **10+ times** in the Dialogues, always in theological-epistemological argument. Adario's key critique: *"cette foy ne peut être qu'une persuasion, croire c'est être persuadé, être persuadé c'est voir de ses propres yeux une chose"* — "this faith can only be a persuasion; to believe is to be persuaded; to be persuaded is to see with one's own eyes." This radical empiricism is diametrically opposed to the Book of Mormon's faith principle governing the Liahona ("it did work for them according to their faith in God," Alma 37:40). Yet both texts place **faith** at the center of how instruments-and-spirits relate. Lahontan denies faith; the Book of Mormon demands it.
**Etymology of "Lahontan" itself.** The name derives from the village of Lahontan in the Basse-Pyrénées (modern Pyrénées-Atlantiques). Per French Wikipedia: *"Le nom de Lahontan vient peut-être du béarnais 'hont' ou 'hount' qui signifie fontaine"* — the Béarnais/Gascon word "hont/hount" means **fountain or spring**. Historical spellings documented in archival sources include: **Lafontaa** (13th c.), **Larfontan** (13th c.), **Lafontan** (1538), **Beata Maria de Lahuntan** (1689), and in the Thwaites edition, the soldiers' variant **"la Hontau's Limit"** (Vol. I, p. 128). The Béarnais pronunciation would be approximately **"Lahountâ."**
---
### The Lahontan → Lehonti phonetic connection is stronger than previously noted
The BYU Book of Mormon Onomasticon entry for LEHONTI states: **"No etymology immediately suggests itself."** It lists a variant spelling **"Lahonti"** — one letter removed from "Lahontan" — and cross-references LIAHONA, acknowledging a perceived phonetic relationship between the two Book of Mormon names. The Deseret Alphabet rendering confirms the 19th-century pronunciation as approximately **"Lee-HON-tie"** [liːhɒntaɪ].
| Name | IPA | Core phonemes |
|------|-----|---------------|
| **Lahontan** (French) | [la.ɔ̃.tɑ̃] | l-a-hõ-tã |
| **Lahontan** (English) | [læ.hɒn.tən] | l-æ-hɒn-tən |
| **Lehonti** (BofM) | [liː.hɒn.taɪ] | l-iː-hɒn-taɪ |
| **Lahonti** (BofM variant) | [la.hɒn.taɪ] | l-a-hɒn-taɪ |
| **Liahona** (BofM) | [liː.ə.hoʊ.nə] | l-iː-a-hoʊ-nə |
The shared phonetic kernel **l-_-hon-t** is identical across Lahontan, Lehonti, and the variant Lahonti. The BYU Onomasticon's own cross-reference between Lehonti and Liahona becomes especially suggestive if both derive from the same source: the name of the Baron whose text describes compasses animated by spirits.
A second, weaker phonetic parallel: **Mozeemlek → Mulek**. The "-mlek" ending of the fictional western people in Letter XVI echoes the Book of Mormon name Mulek (the son of Zedekiah). No other tribal or place names in Lahontan's works show clear phonetic resemblance to Book of Mormon names.
---
### Metal plates, copper medals, and inscribed objects
Lahontan's works contain several descriptions of metal artifacts and inscribed objects that resonate with the Book of Mormon's central conceit of sacred records on metal plates.
**The "Plate of Lead" (Letter XVI, p. 127).** Lahontan describes erecting a territorial marker: "I fix'd a great long Pole, with the **Arms of France done upon a Plate of Lead**." The French: *"les Armes de France gravées sur une **plaque de plomb**."* This is a metal plate ("plaque") bearing engraved content ("gravées") affixed to a pole in the wilderness — an inscribed metal record left as a claim. The practice of French explorers leaving lead plates inscribed with royal arms was well documented; La Salle did the same on his Mississippi expeditions.
**The copper medal (Letter XVI, p. 126).** "One of the four Mozeemlek Slaves had a reddish sort of a **Copper Medal** hanging upon his Neck, the Figure of which is represented in the Map. I had it **melted** by Mr. de Tonti's Gun-smith, who understood something of **Mettals**; but it became thereupon heavier, and deeper colour'd, and withal somewhat tractable." The French uses **"médaille de cuivre"** and **"métaux."** This passage describes a worked metal artifact from a distant Indigenous civilization, bearing inscribed figures, analyzed metallurgically. The Tahuglauk who made these medals also manufactured **"Copper Axes, and several other Manufactures"** (p. 125) — evidence of Indigenous metallurgy.
**Gold and Silver as Matchi Manitous (p. 446–447).** "These Evil Spirits they call **Matchi Manitous**, and **Gold and Silver they lift into that number**." Also: "The others will not touch or so much as look upon **Silver**, but give it the odious Name of **the French Serpent**" (p. 420). These passages classify precious metals as spiritually evil — an inversion that resonates with the Book of Mormon's persistent anti-wealth theology (Jacob 2:13, Helaman 6:17).
**The "fusee" with an evil spirit (p. 446).** "If a Fusee burst either through the fault of the Metal, or by being over Loaded, and Maims a Man, they'll tell you there was an **Evil Spirit lodg'd within it**." Metal objects contain spirits — good or evil — in the indigenous framework Lahontan describes.
---
### Hieroglyphics, writing systems, and the problem of tradition
The Mémoires contain a full chapter titled **"An Explication of the Savage Hieroglyphicks"** (p. 512), with an accompanying engraved illustration of pictographic symbols (facing p. 720). A preceding chapter covers **"Coats of Arms of the Savages"** (p. 510). The French term is **"hiéroglyphes"** — the same word used for Egyptian writing, and the comparison was deliberate.
Lahontan describes a functional Indigenous symbolic writing system used to communicate complex messages: marks carved or painted on bark conveying military intelligence, numbers of warriors, directions of travel, and outcomes of conflicts. He also describes chorographical maps: "they draw the most exact **Maps** imaginable of the Countries they're acquainted with... These **Chorographical Maps are drawn upon the Rind of your Birch Tree**; and when the Old Men hold a Council about War or Hunting, they're always sure to consult them" (p. 427). Additionally, the Gnacsitares "represented by way of a **Map upon a Deer's Skin**" (p. 124).
The Book of Mormon describes its own record as written in **"characters which are called among us the reformed Egyptian"** (Mormon 9:32). The concept that Indigenous Americans possessed a form of hieroglyphic writing — described by a widely-read 1703 text — provides a cultural framework within which claims about ancient American writing systems would seem plausible to early 19th-century readers.
Equally significant is Lahontan's discussion of **tradition's unreliability** (pp. 411–412): "I am now fully convinc'd that **Tradition is so inconstant, obscure, uncertain and fallacious**, that we cann't pretend to rely upon it. And this Notion I owe to the Savages of Canada, who being at a loss to trace the truth of what has been transacted in their own Country but **200 Years ago**, gave me occasion to call in Question the Purity and Truth of Tradition." This passage argues that without written records, peoples lose their history — precisely the rationale the Book of Mormon gives for the necessity of the brass plates and gold plates.
---
### Stone cities, the salt lake, and the Tahuglauk civilization
Letter XVI — Lahontan's account of his exploration up the "Long River" from the Mississippi — describes a graduated series of increasingly sophisticated civilizations culminating in the **Tahuglauk**, who inhabit "**six noble Cities, surrounded with Stone cemented with fat Earth**" near "a **Salt Lake of three hundred Leagues in Circumference**" (p. 125). Their government was "**Despotick**, and lodg'd in the hands of one great Head, to whom the rest paid a trembling Submission." They navigated the salt lake in great boats 130 feet long, holding 200 men. The Mozeemlek intermediaries wore **beards**, knee-length garments, pointed caps, carried canes "tipp'd, not unlike what we use in **Europe**," and wore boots to the knee. Their women "**never shew themselves**" — a feature Lahontan compares to Italy and Spain.
This entire narrative is **generally considered fictional or heavily embellished** by modern scholars, though some identify the salt lake with the Great Salt Lake (geologist Clarence King later named the prehistoric Lake Lahontan in the Baron's honor). The Tahuglauk description reads like garbled intelligence about Pueblo or Mesoamerican civilizations transmitted through intermediary peoples.
For Book of Mormon purposes, the Tahuglauk passage provides: stone-and-cement construction, a centralized despotic government, large-scale water navigation, extensive metallurgy (copper axes), and semi-European cultural features — all attributed to peoples west of the Mississippi. The "stone cemented with fat Earth" description specifically resonates with Helaman 3:7–11, which describes Nephite construction using "cement" in a land lacking timber.
---
### Religion, the Great Spirit, and the "Country of Souls"
Lahontan's ethnographic chapters and Dialogues contain the most sustained European account of Indigenous theology published before the Enlightenment. Key passages with Book of Mormon resonance include:
**The Great Spirit / Kitchi Manitou** (pp. 434–435): "Man was not made by chance, and that he's the Work of a Being superior in Wisdom and Knowledge, which they call the **Great Spirit**, or the **Master of Life**, and which they Adore in the most abstracted and spiritual manner... The Existence of God being inseparable from his Essence, it contains every thing, it appears in every thing, **acts in every thing, and gives motion to every thing**." The italicized phrase — God giving "motion to every thing" — directly echoes the theological claim underlying the Liahona: divine spirit animating physical objects.
**The Country of Souls** (pp. 441, 449, 460, 472): The indigenous afterlife is not a heaven/hell dichotomy but a single "great Country of Souls" where one meets deceased relatives. The harangue to the Great Spirit (p. 449) pleads: "**send us to the great Countrey of Souls**, where we may meet with those of our Fathers, our Mothers, our Wives, our Children." Adario explains: *"Nous croyons aussi d'aller dans le pais des Ames après nôtre mort; mais nous ne soupçonnons pas, comme vous, qu'il faut nécessairement qu'il y ait des séjours & bons & mauvais après la vie"* — "We believe we will go to the Country of Souls after death; but we do not suspect, as you do, that there must necessarily be both good and bad abodes after this life." This single-destination afterlife parallels the Book of Mormon's "spirit world" (Alma 40:11–14) more closely than the standard Christian heaven/hell.
**Adario's six-point creed** (Dialogues): (1) A Creator under the name Grand Esprit or Master of Life; (2) immortality of the soul; (3) God gave reason to distinguish good from evil; (4) tranquility of soul pleases God; (5) **life is a dream and death an awakening**; (6) human understanding cannot penetrate beyond the surface of the earth. This systematic indigenous natural theology — articulated as a numbered creed — provides a template for how an educated early 19th-century reader might conceptualize Indigenous American religiosity.
**Prophecy, miracles, and the destruction of Jerusalem** (p. 443): Lahontan reports presenting to the Huron chief "the **Revelations of Moses and the Prophets**, the universal Consent of almost all Nations in owning and acknowledging Jesus Christ, the Martyrdom of his Disciples... the perpetual Succession of our Sacred **Oracles**, the entire **Destruction of the Jewish Republick**, and the **Destruction of Jerusalem** foretold by our Saviour." The destruction of Jerusalem is the founding event of the Book of Mormon narrative (1 Nephi 1:4, 1:13).
**The Transmigration of Souls** (Letter XVI, p. 121): Among the Essanapes, newly-married women run to "**receive the Soul** of an old Fellow that lay a dying." Lahontan "concluded, that the People were **Pythagoreans**" and asked about eating animals. They replied: "the **Transmigration of Souls** is always confin'd to the respective Species."
---
### Governance, warfare, and the liberty–slavery antithesis
Lahontan's depiction of Indigenous governance — **"They have neither Laws, Judges, nor Priests"** (p. 424) — structures a liberty/slavery antithesis that permeates both the Mémoires and Dialogues. Adario declares: **"I am Master of my own Body, I have the absolute disposal of myself, I do what I please, I am the first and the last of my Nation, I fear no Man, and I depend only upon the Great Spirit: Whereas your Body, as well as your Soul, are doomed to a dependance upon your great Captain"** (p. 553–554). The Council of Old Men governs by consensus (p. 422); "one's as much Master as another, and since Men are all made of the same Clay there should be no Distinction or Superiority among them" (p. 421).
This framework resonates with the Book of Mormon's political theology in Mosiah 29, where King Mosiah abolishes monarchy in favor of elected judges, arguing that "it is not common that the voice of the people desireth anything contrary to that which is right" (Mosiah 29:26). The Tahuglauk's "Despotick" government (p. 125) inverts this pattern, paralleling the Book of Mormon's negative kingship narratives (King Noah, the Jaredite kings).
**Fortifications** feature prominently: "Their Villages are **Fortified with double Palissadoes** of very hard Wood, which are as thick as one's Thigh, and **fifteen Foot high**, with little Squares about the middle of the Courtines" (p. 417). This layered timber-and-earth defensive construction closely parallels Captain Moroni's fortifications in Alma 50:1–6: "heaps of earth round about all the cities... timbers, yea, works of timbers built up to the height of a man."
Military numbers are large: the Eokoros field **"12 Villages peopled by 20000 Warriours"** (Letter XVI, p. 115); the Tahuglauk "never took the Field without twenty thousand Men at least" (p. 125). Such figures match the Book of Mormon's consistently large army sizes.
**Laying down weapons** appears twice: the Eokoros "**threw down their Arms**, and presented the Company with some Deer" (p. 113), and the Essanapes' "Leading Officer, and the whole Battalion, **flung their Bows and Arrows aside** all on a sudden" (p. 117). While not identical to the Anti-Nephi-Lehies burying their weapons (Alma 24:17–19), the motif of dramatically discarding weapons as a peace gesture appears in both texts.
---
### Skin color, origins, and the question of descent
Lahontan describes Indigenous complexion matter-of-factly: "All the Savages are of a **Sanguine Constitution, inclining to an Olive Colour**" (p. 415). The Mozeemlek had a "swarthy" complexion (p. 124). No passage in Lahontan attributes moral significance to skin color — there is no "dark skin as curse" motif.
On origins, Lahontan is agnostic: "these poor People are as little acquainted with their own **History and Origin**, as the Greeks and Chaldeans were with theirs. Let us therefore content our selves, my good Friend, in believing that they are **descended of honest old Adam**, as well as you and I" (p. 412). He does not entertain Hebrew or Israelite origin theories, nor does he mention the Ten Lost Tribes. The words "Hebrew," "Israelite," and "Jew" appear only in his reference to "the entire Destruction of the Jewish Republick" (p. 443).
**The reference to Tartary** appears in the Thwaites introduction (p. xlii): "the avidity of the explorers of his day to find a route through the continent of America to the land of the great khan of Tartary." The Passage 3 letter also mentions "the **Tartarian** Kalmouks" and "In **Asia** the Chymists." These casual references to Asian peoples in a text about Indigenous Americans situate Lahontan within the broader colonial discourse connecting American and Asian populations — the same intellectual current that produced Hebrew-origin theories.
---
### What the Dialogues do and do not contain
The French Dialogues — exhaustively searched term by term — contain **zero instrumental, navigational, or metallic vocabulary**. No boussole, aiguille, compas, directeur, laiton, cuivre, plaque, or plomb. No discussion of Indigenous origins, Hebrew connections, migration narratives, or the deluge. The Dialogues are purely philosophical: theological debate, legal critique, social comparison.
What the Dialogues *do* provide abundantly is the **theological and epistemological framework** within which the instruments-as-spirits concept gains meaning. Adario's empiricist critique of faith, his articulation of the Great Spirit theology, his defense of Indigenous jongleurs, and his condemnation of European hypocrisy create a comprehensive counter-theology. The Book of Mormon engages with many of the same questions — the nature of God, the basis of faith, the relationship between reason and revelation, the corruption of institutional religion — but reaches opposite conclusions. Where Adario says faith is mere credulity, the Book of Mormon says faith is the animating principle of the universe.
---
### Availability and the question of access
Lahontan's work was a European bestseller: **thirteen editions in fourteen years** after 1703, translated into English, Dutch, and German. Thomas Jefferson owned the second English edition (London, 1735). The work was widely cited by Voltaire, Montesquieu, Swift, Rousseau, and Diderot. Jonathan Carver's *Travels Through the Interior Parts of North America* (1778), widely read in America, drew on Lahontan. His Long River appeared on maps **as late as 1785**.
However, **no American edition existed before 1830**, and no direct evidence places Lahontan's works in any library near Joseph Smith in Palmyra or Manchester, New York. The first American scholarly edition was Thwaites's 1905 reprint. Rick Grunder's *Mormon Parallels* (2008/2014), the most comprehensive compilation of pre-1830 texts paralleling the Book of Mormon, **does not appear to include Lahontan** — a notable absence suggesting this connection has gone unrecognized.
The compass-as-manitou trope, however, circulated independently of Lahontan. The Dictionary of Canadianisms (DCHP-3) records an 1811 attestation: "**This compass... not being able to comprehend its action, they called... a 'Manitou,' by which they mean 'spirit,' or something incomprehensible and powerful.**" Roger Williams recorded in 1643 that Algonquian peoples cried "Mannitowock" — "They are Gods" — at the sight of ships, buildings, and especially books. The broader trope of Indigenous peoples viewing European technology as spiritually animated was **pervasive in colonial literature** available in early 19th-century America.
---
### Complete inventory of additional passages not previously catalogued
Beyond the three core "instruments as spirits" passages, the following previously unnoted passages merit inclusion in a comprehensive analysis:
The **harangue to the Great Spirit** (p. 449): "Great Spirit, Master of our Lives; Great Spirit, Master of all Things both Visible and Invisible; Great Spirit, Master of other Spirits, whether good or Evil; command the Good Spirits to favour thy Children... **Acquaint us by the Spirit of Dreams, with what thy Pleasure requires of us, or prohibits us to do**... send us to the great Countrey of Souls." This prayer — formatted as liturgy in Lahontan's text — describes a God who communicates through dreams and governs all spirits, both good and evil. The communicative function mirrors the Liahona's function: divine guidance mediated through a spiritual mechanism.
