The **convex glass and catoptricks** passage (p. 430): "All the Savages that saw this Piece of Catoptricks, thought it no less **Miraculous** than the awaker of a Clock, or a Magical Lanthern, or the Spring of a Machine." This extends the instruments-as-spirits framework to optical devices and mechanical springs — any technology with hidden internal mechanisms.
The **Adario on money as the "Devil of Devils"** (Dialogues, p. 570–571): "I affirm that what you call Silver is the **Devil of Devils**; the Tyrant of the French; the Source of all Evil; the Bane of Souls, and the Slaughter-House of living Persons. To pretend to live in the Money Country, and at the same time to save one's Soul, is as great an inconsistency as for a Man to go to the bottom of a Lake to preserve his Life." The classification of silver/money as diabolical extends the Matchi Manitou concept into systematic social critique.
The **fire in the other world** passage (p. 464): When told by Jesuits that "a **Fire** is Kindled in the other World to **Torment** 'em for ever," the Savages respond: "if their Threats be well grounded, the **Mountains** of the other World must consist of the **Ashes of souls**."
The **Scriptures by heart** passage (p. 438): "they have such happy Memories, that I knew ten of my own Acquaintance that had all the **Holy Scriptures** by Heart." Indigenous people memorizing entire sacred texts parallels the Book of Mormon's emphasis on oral transmission alongside written records.
---
### Structural parallels and the inversion principle
The deepest connection between Lahontan and the Book of Mormon may be structural rather than verbal. Lahontan presents the "compass moved by spirits" idea as Indigenous superstition — evidence of naïveté. The Book of Mormon presents a compass-like device that **genuinely is** spirit-operated, requiring faith to function. What Lahontan frames as primitive credulity, the Book of Mormon reframes as authentic spiritual principle. This constitutes a **theological inversion**: the colonial dismissal becomes the scriptural vindication.
The same inversion operates across multiple themes. Lahontan's Adario argues that Indigenous peoples' simple morality surpasses corrupted European Christianity — a critique the Book of Mormon echoes through figures like Samuel the Lamanite (Helaman 13–16). Lahontan describes Indigenous governance without kings, laws, or priests as natural liberty — a framework the Book of Mormon both validates (Mosiah 29's abolition of kingship) and complicates (the institution of judges and prophets). Lahontan's dismissal of tradition as unreliable (p. 412) mirrors the Book of Mormon's anxiety about lost records — but the Book of Mormon's solution (metal plates, divine preservation) addresses exactly the problem Lahontan identifies.
### Conclusion
This exhaustive analysis reveals that Lahontan's complete works contain far more material relevant to Book of Mormon studies than the three "instruments as spirits" passages alone. The text provides: a compass explicitly described as spirit-animated (French: *boussole mûë par des Esprits*); metal plates with inscriptions; a hieroglyphic writing system; stone-and-cement cities near a great salt lake; an afterlife called the "Country of Souls"; a Great Spirit theology with numbered articles of faith; systematic debates about the relationship between faith and empirical knowledge; double-palisade fortifications; large army numbers; a phonetic near-twin in "Lahontan"/"Lehonti"; and a worked copper medal from a distant civilization. The French originals reveal that the English translation consistently softens the animist framework — "Esprit surnaturel" becomes mere "Supernatural Assistance" — making the connection to spirit-animated instruments less obvious in the English edition that would have circulated in America. No existing scholarship has identified these connections, and the field's most comprehensive parallel-hunter (Rick Grunder) appears to have missed Lahontan entirely. Whether these parallels reflect direct influence, mediated cultural transmission, or independent convergence on common colonial-era themes is the central question for *The Brass Director* to adjudicate.
---
# PART II: THE LAHONTI-LAHONTAN ONOMASTIC CONNECTION
## The Lahonti-Lahontan Connection: An Unmapped Parallel
The Book of Mormon character Lehonti — originally spelled **"Lahonti"** in Oliver Cowdery's manuscript — shares a near-identical name with Baron de Lahontan, whose 1703 *New Voyages to North America* contains the most famous story of political treachery in colonial New France. No published scholar has ever connected the two. Yet the parallels extend well beyond the name: Lahontan's work features elaborate false-flag deception, feigned rage to frame allies for one's own actions, assassination through unwitting proxies, and the gradual political destruction of a military commander — all core elements of the Alma 47 narrative. This report documents every identifiable parallel between the Lehonti story and Lahontan's works, biography, and the historical Kondiaronk narrative that Lahontan made famous.
### The name differs by two letters and no one has noticed
The most immediate connection is onomastic. In the Original Manuscript (O) of the Book of Mormon, at Alma 47:19, Oliver Cowdery wrote **"Lahonti,"** which was subsequently altered to "Lehonti" in the Printer's Manuscript and all published editions from 1830 onward. This is confirmed by the *Book of Mormon Critical Text: A Tool for Scholarly Reference* (2:811) and catalogued in the BYU Book of Mormon Onomasticon's variant page. The consonant skeleton **L-H-N-T** is shared exactly between "Lahonti" and "Lahontan," with the names differing only in the final vowel cluster (-i vs. -an).
The BYU Onomasticon entry for Lehonti is notably sparse. It states flatly: **"No etymology immediately suggests itself."** It suggests an unlikely Hebrew root (*ḥnṭ*, "ripen, embalm") and cross-references Liahona and Onti(es), but makes no mention of Baron de Lahontan. An exhaustive search across all major critical scholarship — Dan Vogel, Grant Palmer, David Persuitte, Jerald and Sandra Tanner, Robert Anderson, and Rick Grunder's 2,307-page *Mormon Parallels: A Bibliographic Source* (to the extent its contents are publicly indexed) — reveals **zero published references** connecting the names. Searches across Reddit, podcasts, blogs, and social media likewise return nothing. This appears to be an entirely uncharted observation in Book of Mormon studies, critical or apologetic.
Lahontan's *New Voyages* was among the most widely read books about North America in the eighteenth century — **over 25 legitimate and pirated editions** in French, English, Dutch, and German within sixty years of its 1703 publication. It influenced Voltaire, Montesquieu, and Swift. The first English translation appeared the same year as the French original. Copies are held by Yale's Beinecke Library, the John Carter Brown Library, and the Library of Congress. Whether any copy was accessible near Palmyra in the 1820s remains unconfirmed by digitized catalog evidence, though the book's saturation of Anglophone print culture makes its presence in major American subscription libraries highly plausible.
### Kondiaronk's ruse at La Famine is the deepest structural parallel
The single most important narrative in Lahontan's works, for purposes of comparison to Alma 47, is his account of Kondiaronk's sabotage of the French-Iroquois peace negotiations in 1688. Kondiaronk (known to the French as "Le Rat" and fictionalized by Lahontan as "Adario") was a Tionontati Huron chief whom the Jesuit historian Charlevoix called **"the Indian of the highest merit that the French ever knew in Canada."** His scheme unfolds in Lahontan's Letters (approximately Letter XXI in the Thwaites edition, p. 225) and is corroborated by the Dictionary of Canadian Biography. It proceeds in steps that mirror Amalickiah's multi-layered deception with striking precision.
**Step one: discovering a secret deal that threatens his position.** In summer 1688, Kondiaronk arrived at Fort Frontenac with roughly 100 warriors, intending to raid the Iroquois. He learned that Governor Denonville was secretly negotiating peace with the Five Nations — breaking a pledge made to the Hurons that war would continue until the Iroquois were subdued. Just as Amalickiah discovers the Lamanite dissidents have gathered on Mount Antipas to resist the king's war order, Kondiaronk discovers that the political situation has shifted against him and devises a counter-scheme.
**Step two: the ambush as false-flag operation.** Kondiaronk withdrew to Anse de la Famine, where he knew the Onondaga peace embassy — four councillors and **40 warriors** led by the renowned orator Teganissorens — must pass. He ambushed them as they disembarked, killing one chief and capturing the rest. When the prisoners protested they were peace envoys, Kondiaronk **feigned amazement, then rage and fury, cursing Denonville** for supposedly ordering the attack. He told his captives: *"Go, my Brethren, though I am at War with you, yet I release you, and allow you to go home. 'Tis the Governour of the French that put me upon this black Action, which I shall never be able to digest unless your five Nations revenge themselves and make their just Reprisals."*
**Step three: using an unwitting proxy to deepen the frame.** Kondiaronk then delivered a captive to the French commandant at Michilimackinac, who — knowing nothing of the peace negotiations — had the protesting Iroquois envoy executed. Kondiaronk freed a long-held Iroquois slave to carry the full account of the "French" atrocity back to the Five Nations.
The structural correspondence to Amalickiah's scheme is laid out in the following comparison:
- **Feigned emotions to misdirect blame.** Amalickiah "pretended to be angry" at the Lamanite king's death, which he himself orchestrated (Alma 47:27). Kondiaronk feigned "amazement, then rage and fury" at Denonville's supposed treachery, which was entirely his own invention.
- **Framing others for one's own actions.** Amalickiah has his servant stab the king and then blames the king's own servants, who flee in fear. Kondiaronk ambushes the Iroquois himself and blames the French governor, who cannot disprove it.
- **Using unwitting proxies to execute key acts.** Amalickiah's servant does the actual killing; the French commandant unknowingly executes Kondiaronk's prisoner.
- **Multi-step schemes involving multiple parties.** Both schemers manipulate at least three distinct groups. Amalickiah plays the Lamanite king, Lehonti's dissident army, and later the queen. Kondiaronk plays the French, the Iroquois, and his own Huron allies.
- **Disrupting peace for strategic advantage.** Amalickiah disrupts Lamanite internal stability to seize the throne. Kondiaronk destroys the French-Iroquois peace to protect Huron survival. Lahontan himself assessed the result: **"the Breach made by the Rat's Contrivance was irreparable."** The Iroquois launched the devastating Lachine Massacre of 1689, killing and capturing hundreds.
The key structural differences are also worth noting. Kondiaronk's motivation — protecting his vulnerable people from annihilation — is morally complex in a way Amalickiah's naked ambition is not. Kondiaronk eventually became the chief architect of the Great Peace of Montreal (1701). The Alma 47 narrative compresses and moralizes what in Lahontan's account is geopolitically sophisticated. And several Alma 47 elements — the mountain encampment, the four entreaties, the poisoning, the marriage to the queen — have no direct counterpart in the La Famine story.
### Lahontan's own biography reads like a Lehonti parallel
The biographical parallels between Baron de Lahontan and the character Lehonti are, in some respects, more precise than the Kondiaronk narrative parallels. Lahontan was a military officer in New France who, after defending Placentia (Newfoundland) against an English attack in September 1692, was promoted to **King's Lieutenant** — effectively the second-in-command of the colony — on March 15, 1693. When he returned to Placentia with this promotion, Governor **Jacques-François de Monbeton de Brouillan** reacted with suspicion and hostility.
What followed was a systematic campaign of destruction that corresponds remarkably to **"poisoned by degrees."** Brouillan did not confront Lahontan openly. Instead, he sent unfavorable dispatch after unfavorable dispatch to the minister in Paris, accusing Lahontan of neglect, insubordination, and pleasure-seeking. He wrote: *"M. de Lahontan doesn't bother about anything here except what can contribute to his pleasures."* He drew up a formal indictment. His men entered Lahontan's residence and destroyed his belongings. Each report was another dose of political poison, gradually eroding Lahontan's standing at court until the King issued orders for his arrest.
The parallel to Alma 47:18 — "caused that one of his servants should administer poison by degrees to Lehonti, that he died" — is metaphorical rather than literal, but the structural correspondence is notable. In both narratives, a military commander in a position of nominal authority is gradually destroyed by an antagonist who works through institutional channels rather than direct confrontation. Lahontan's position as **second-in-command** under a hostile superior mirrors the deal Amalickiah proposes to Lehonti (serving as second-in-command), only with the roles inverted: in real life, Lahontan was the second-in-command who was destroyed by the governor above him, while in the Book of Mormon, Amalickiah offers to be the second-in-command and then destroys the leader above him.
Facing the Bastille, Lahontan fled on December 14, 1693, paying a ship's captain **1,000 écus** for passage. He spent the remaining 22 years of his life in exile — Portugal, Spain, Holland, England, Sweden (where he spent a year in prison), and finally the court of Hanover, where he died on April 21, 1716. He never returned to France or New France. Brouillan remained as governor.
### Mountains, poison, and assassins appear explicitly in Lahontan's text
Several specific textual elements in Lahontan's works resonate with Alma 47, even where no single passage tells the same story.
**Mountains as military strongholds.** During the Denonville campaign of 1687, Lahontan describes being "forc'd to climb up three Mountains, upon which **an hundred Iroquese might have knock'd us all on the head with Stones.**" This passage explicitly frames mountains as positions of overwhelming tactical advantage from which defenders can annihilate those below — the same logic underlying Lehonti's refusal to come down from Mount Antipas. At Placentia, Brouillan built a stone redoubt on the summit of an adjoining hill and hauled cannons to the mountaintop, creating a fortified high-ground position central to the colony's defense.
**Poisoning by physicians.** In the Dialogues (Volume 2, "Of Laws," pp. 559–561 in the Thwaites edition), Adario/Kondiaronk explicitly accuses European physicians of being poisoners: *"to see **physicians poison our families**, and gamesters lose the beaver skins they've gotten in hunting."* Elsewhere he says physicians **"murdered three fourths of the people"** — describing slow, institutional killing by trusted figures. While this is philosophical rather than narrative, the word "poison" appears in the context of gradual, hidden killing by those who are supposed to be caregivers — a thematic echo of being "poisoned by degrees" by a supposed ally.
**Assassins and political violence.** Adario explicitly mentions assassins in the same Dialogue: *"you're afraid of Robbers, false Witnesses, **Assassins**, and you depended upon an infinity of Persons whose Places have raised them above you."* The word appears in a discussion of how European power structures produce political violence — the exact milieu of Amalickiah's scheme.
**Treachery as a pervasive theme.** Adario declares that money is *"the Father of Luxury, Lasciviousness, Intrigues, Tricks, Lying, **Treachery**, Falseness, and in a word, of all the mischief in the World."* The Thwaites edition's introduction calls the French seizure of friendly Iroquois settlers and their deportation to royal galleys **"a piece of arrant treachery, which the wretched and misguided colony was to expiate two years later in the fire and blood of the massacre at Lachine."**
**Repeated entreaties and failed negotiations.** The La Barre expedition of 1684 — which Lahontan witnessed personally — involved the French governor repeatedly seeking negotiations with the Iroquois while his army weakened from disease at La Famine. The Iroquois orator Grangula (Otreouti) negotiated from a position of total strength, humiliating La Barre, who was forced to accept ignominious terms. The entire episode revolves around a power that desperately seeks a meeting while the opposing side holds the advantage and dictates conditions.
### What the parallel does not explain and where it breaks down
Intellectual honesty requires cataloguing where the parallels fail. **No single narrative in Lahontan's works contains all the elements of Alma 47 combined.** The specific plot mechanics — a leader encamped on a mountain, four sequential entreaties with the fourth succeeding, a secret deal to become second-in-command, literal poisoning by degrees, marriage to the widowed queen — do not appear together or individually (in that specific form) in Lahontan. The mountain-stronghold motif exists in Lahontan's military descriptions but not in the context of a leader refusing to descend. The poisoning appears as philosophical commentary, not as an assassination technique. The four-times entreaty pattern has no counterpart.
The Kondiaronk parallel is structural, not plotline: both stories feature a master schemer who plays multiple parties through deception, feigns emotions, frames others, and reshapes political power. But Kondiaronk does not poison anyone, does not become king, does not propose a second-in-command deal, and is morally far more complex than Amalickiah. The biographical parallel with Brouillan's gradual destruction of Lahontan captures the "poisoned by degrees" element beautifully but inverts the power dynamic (Lahontan is the subordinate destroyed, not the superior).
It is also uncertain whether Joseph Smith or anyone in his circle had direct access to Lahontan's text. The book was enormously popular in the eighteenth century, but no digitized library catalog from the Palmyra area or accessible New York subscription libraries has been found listing it. The name "Lahontan" could plausibly have been encountered in encyclopedias, geographical dictionaries, or secondary references to North American exploration, even without access to the full text.
### Conclusion
The Lehonti–Lahontan connection rests on three pillars of decreasing specificity but increasing significance. First, the onomastic near-identity: **"Lahonti" in the original manuscript differs from "Lahontan" by two letters**, sharing the full consonant skeleton L-H-N-T, with no Hebrew or other ancient etymology available to explain the name. Second, the structural parallel between Amalickiah's multi-layered scheme in Alma 47 and Kondiaronk's La Famine ruse as told by Lahontan — both featuring false-flag operations, feigned emotions, proxy killings, and the manipulation of multiple parties to reshape political power. Third, the biographical echo of Lahontan's own gradual political destruction at the hands of Governor Brouillan, a metaphorical "poisoning by degrees" that stripped a military commander of his position through incremental institutional betrayal.
That no scholar has previously published on this connection — despite decades of intensive critical analysis of Book of Mormon names and sources — is itself a finding. The BYU Onomasticon's admission that "no etymology immediately suggests itself" for Lehonti stands as an open invitation. Whether the resemblance is coincidental, reflects indirect transmission of a famous name through the cultural atmosphere of early American print, or indicates direct borrowing from one of the most widely circulated books about Indigenous North America remains a question that further research into early nineteenth-century library holdings and the textual environment of the Book of Mormon's production may eventually resolve.
---
# PART III: THE 1 NEPHI VOYAGE NARRATIVE PARALLELS
## Lahontan's Ghost in the Book of Mormon
**No scholar has yet compared Baron de Lahontan's *New Voyages to North America* (1703) with the Book of Mormon's 1 Nephi — yet the structural, thematic, and textual parallels between these two texts are extensive and striking.** Lahontan's enormously popular three-volume work, which went through 25 editions in 60 years and was the second most widely read North American travel narrative of the eighteenth century, describes Atlantic storm crossings, compass instruments attributed to spirits, Indigenous civilizations building with stone and cement near a great salt lake, Great Spirit theology, hierarchies of "civilized" and "savage" peoples, and an exile narrator fleeing authority — all elements that reappear, reframed through biblical typology, in 1 Nephi and the broader Book of Mormon narrative. Thomas Jefferson owned Lahontan's second English edition and recommended it as essential reading for American youth. The work was available in English from 1703 and circulated widely through colonial and early republic libraries, making it plausible background reading for anyone in early nineteenth-century New England engaged with questions about Indigenous origins and the American interior.
This analysis proceeds through twelve points of comparison, drawing on the Thwaites 1905 scholarly edition of Lahontan and the Book of Mormon text, supplemented by critical scholarship from Dan Vogel, William Davis, Eran Shalev, and other secular analysts of the Book of Mormon's literary context.
---
### The compass "moved by Spirits" and Nephi's Liahona
The single most arresting textual parallel between Lahontan and 1 Nephi involves navigation instruments and spiritual agency. In 1 Nephi 16:10, Lehi discovers "a round ball of curious workmanship; and it was of **fine brass**. And within the ball were **two spindles**; and the one pointed the way whither we should go into the wilderness." This device — later named the Liahona, glossed as "a compass" (Alma 37:38) — works "according to the faith and diligence and heed which we did give unto them" (1 Nephi 16:28). It displays written messages that change over time. When Laman and Lemuel rebel during the ocean crossing, "the compass, which had been prepared of the Lord, **did cease to work**" (1 Nephi 18:12), and the ship is driven by a terrible storm. Only when Nephi is freed and prays does the compass resume functioning.
Lahontan's appendix letter of July 4, 1695 contains a passage that resonates uncannily with this narrative:
> "**The Savages believe that a Watch, a Compass, and a thousand other Machines are moved by Spirits**; for your ignorant and clownish People form extravagant Ideas of every thing that surpasses their Imagination."
A complementary passage in Volume 2, page 446, elaborates on this framework within the chapter on Indigenous worship:
> "The Savages give the name of Genius or Spirit to all that surpasses their Understanding, and proceeds from a cause that they cannot trace. Some of these Spirits they take to be Good, and some Bad; of the former sort are the Spirit of Dreams... **a Solar Quadrant, an Alarm Watch**, and an infinity of other things that seem to them to be inconceivable."
A third passage describes Indigenous reaction to Lahontan's graphometer (surveying instrument): "They took my Graphometer for somewhat Divine, being unable to guess how we could know the distance of Places without measuring them by Cords or Rods, **without there were some Supernatural Assistance**."
The Liahona, then, is structurally identical to what Lahontan describes: a brass navigational instrument whose operation is attributed to spiritual power. Lahontan records Indigenous peoples interpreting compasses as spirit-moved; 1 Nephi presents a compass that literally *is* spirit-moved. The conceptual architecture is the same — the difference is that 1 Nephi affirms the spiritual interpretation rather than dismissing it as "extravagant Ideas." The Liahona reads as a literalization of the Indigenous belief Lahontan documented.
---
### Storm, mutiny, and the bound navigator
Both texts contain dramatic storm-at-sea narratives intertwined with themes of rebellion and authority. Lahontan's first Atlantic crossing in 1683 was "tempestuous," and his escape from Newfoundland in December 1693 subjected him to "several terrible Storms" during winter passage to Portugal. These storms bracket his narrative — the entry to and exit from New France are both marked by oceanic violence.
In 1 Nephi 18:13-21, the storm sequence is one of the text's dramatic peaks. Laman and Lemuel bind Nephi, the compass ceases to function, and "there arose a **great storm, yea, a great and terrible tempest**, and we were driven back upon the waters for the space of three days." Only when the rebels release Nephi does the storm cease and the compass resume. This narrative functions as a theological parable — divine displeasure manifesting as weather — but its genre elements (storm, bound protagonist, ship driven off course, resolution through restored authority) belong squarely to the European voyage narrative tradition.
The mutiny parallel extends beyond storms. **Laman and Lemuel's repeated rebellions against Nephi and Lehi map structurally onto the pervasive conflicts with authority that saturate Lahontan's narrative.** Lahontan clashed with Governor Denonville (earning confinement for protesting the treatment of Iroquois prisoners), burned Fort Saint-Joseph without orders, refused an advantageous marriage arranged by Governor Frontenac, and fought bitterly with Governor Brouillan in Placentia — a conflict that escalated to masked raids, broken furniture, and scurrilous songs before Lahontan's flight. In the Long River expedition, his party endured "boisterous violence" from storms that forced them ashore, where they were "in danger of starving for Hunger and Cold." The European voyage narrative genre demanded storms, mutinies, and hardship as standard elements, and both texts deliver them.
---
### Cities of cement near a salt lake: the Tahuglauk and Book of Mormon geography
Perhaps the most structurally significant parallel concerns Lahontan's description of the **Tahuglauk**, a civilization reportedly located in the deep American interior. According to Mozeemlek slaves Lahontan interrogated during his Long River expedition, the Tahuglauk's principal river empties into "**a Salt Lake of three hundred Leagues in Circumference**," and the lower part of that river is "adorned with **six noble Cities, surrounded with Stone cemented with fat Earth**." The houses "have no Roofs, but are open above like a Platform." Beyond these six cities, "above an hundred Towns, great and small" surrounded the salt lake. The Tahuglauk "made Stuffs, Copper Axes, and several other Manufactures" and lived under a "despotic" government.
The Book of Mormon's Helaman 3:7-11 describes remarkably similar civilizations:
> "The people who went forth became exceedingly expert in the working of **cement**; therefore they did build houses of cement, in the which they did dwell... the people in the land northward did dwell in tents, and in **houses of cement**... they did build many cities, both of wood and of cement."
Both texts place cement-building civilizations in the American interior. Both describe peoples who build cities with manufactured materials. The Tahuglauk's salt lake has been connected by some scholars (including the 1852 Stansbury expedition report) to the Great Salt Lake — the same geographical feature central to later Mormon settlement. The Book of Mormon's "land northward," with its cement cities and temples, occupies a conceptual space similar to the Tahuglauk's territory: a distant, advanced civilization in the continental interior, known through report rather than direct observation.
Francis Parkman dismissed the Tahuglauk as fiction, declaring the Long River peoples "are as real as the nations visited by Captain Gulliver." Most scholars agree the Tahuglauk narrative is fabricated or wildly embellished. This very contested historicity creates another parallel: the Tahuglauk and the Nephites occupy the same epistemological category — **elaborate civilizations placed in the pre-contact American interior whose existence cannot be independently verified**.
---
### The Great Spirit: converging theologies
Lahontan's documentation of Indigenous religion centered on the **Kitchi Manitou** (Great Spirit) — an omnipresent creator deity who furnished humans with rational faculties to distinguish good from evil. In the Dialogues with Adario, the Huron chief articulates this theology systematically: the Great Spirit is "the Master of Life... present in everything and unlimited." The souls of the dead travel to the "Country of Souls," an idealized afterlife. Lahontan describes elaborate sacrificial ceremonies at Michilimackinac where goods worth fifty thousand crowns were burned while elders delivered orations to the Great Spirit.
The Book of Mormon presents a strikingly parallel theology. In Alma 18:26-28, the Lamanite King Lamoni does not understand the word "God" but immediately affirms belief when asked about "a Great Spirit": "And then Ammon said: Believest thou that there is a **Great Spirit**? And he said, Yea. And Ammon said: **This is God.**"
Both texts present Indigenous peoples as monotheists who worship a Great Spirit, and both frame this belief as a partial or distorted version of a "truer" theology — Lahontan from a deist skeptic's perspective, the Book of Mormon from a Christian one. The concept of a "Great Spirit" was widespread in European travel literature about North America by 1830. Lahontan's was among the most influential articulations, and the Book of Mormon's deployment of the concept reflects exactly the kind of ethnographic knowledge that circulated through texts like his.
---
### Two exile narrators who never returned home
The biographical parallels between Lahontan and Nephi as narrator-protagonists are remarkable in structure if not in tone. Both are **first-person exile narrators who flee from hostile authority figures and never return to their original homes**. Nephi flees from Laman and Lemuel after Lehi's death, taking "all those who would go with me... those who believed in the warnings and the revelations of God" (2 Nephi 5:5-6). They journey "for the space of many days" and establish a new settlement. Lahontan flees Placentia in December 1693, paying a ship captain a thousand écus to carry him to Europe. He landed in Portugal, wandered to Holland, Denmark, Sweden (where he spent nearly a year in prison), England, and finally the court of Hanover, where he was befriended by Leibniz. He never returned to France or New France.
Both narrators present themselves as morally superior figures persecuted by corrupt or wicked antagonists. Nephi is "born of goodly parents" and chosen by God; his brothers are rebels who "murmur" and seek his life. Lahontan presents himself as a truth-telling adventurer-philosopher persecuted by incompetent governors. A University of British Columbia thesis describes him as "embittered by the loss of his appropriated inheritance, and saddened by his exile from New France, whose freedom he so dearly cherished." Both men write their accounts from exile, looking back on events they can never revisit. Both produce texts that function simultaneously as personal vindication and ethnographic-geographic record.
The key difference is register: Lahontan is a secular rebel and critic of Christianity, while Nephi is a devout prophet who attributes everything to divine providence. But the narrative architecture — **the righteous exile who records his journey for posterity** — is shared.
---
### "Civilized" and "savage" peoples: the Nephite-Lamanite schema
Lahontan's Long River narrative presents a graduated hierarchy of peoples encountered during the journey inland. The Eokoros live in "Hutts... made of Reeds and Bulrushes, interlaced and cemented with a sort of fat Earth." The Essanapes have twenty thousand warriors in twelve villages with permanent homes and worship the sun. The Gnacsitares possess "large Parks, or Inclosures, stocked with wild Beeves." The Mozeemlek inhabit "a hundred civilized villages." And the Tahuglauk build stone cities, manufacture copper axes, wear garments reaching to their knees, and keep their women secluded "which perhaps proceeds from the same principle that prevails in Italy and Spain." The progression moves from "savage" to increasingly "civilized" as one penetrates deeper into the interior.
The Book of Mormon presents a binary version of this same schema. After the family splits, the Nephites become literate city-builders who work in "iron, and of copper, and of brass, and of steel, and of gold, and of silver" (2 Nephi 5:15) and construct a temple "after the manner of the temple of Solomon." The Lamanites receive a divine "skin of blackness," become "an idle people, full of mischief and subtlety," and "did seek in the wilderness for beasts of prey" (2 Nephi 5:21-24). This civilized/savage dichotomy — with the "civilized" group understood as having degenerated from a common origin with the "savage" group — is the core structural logic of both the Mound Builder myth and the Book of Mormon's narrative framework.
**The Mound Builder connection is the best-documented parallel in existing critical scholarship.** Roger Kennedy (former Smithsonian director) declared "Mormonism sprang from the mounds." Curtis Dahl called the Book of Mormon "undoubtedly the most famous and certainly the most influential of all Mound-Builder literature." Dan Vogel's *Indian Origins and the Book of Mormon* (1986) documents how the Nephite/Lamanite narrative maps directly onto the early nineteenth-century belief that a lost civilized race built the earthworks of the Ohio and Mississippi valleys before being destroyed by "savage" Indians. Lahontan's Long River narrative, with its progression from simpler to more complex societies culminating in the stone-building Tahuglauk, belongs to the same intellectual tradition — the European conviction that somewhere in the American interior, lost civilizations waited to be discovered.
---
### Ships, wilderness travel, and the promised land of abundance
The voyage-narrative genre elements in 1 Nephi — fleeing the Old World, building a ship, crossing an ocean, arriving in a land of abundance — have no direct antecedent in the Hebrew Bible but are standard features of European colonial literature. Nephi builds his ship "not after the manner of men" but through divine instruction, working "timbers of curious workmanship" (1 Nephi 18:1-2). Lahontan describes modifying vessels throughout his expeditions: careening boats that "sprung a Leak," ordering carpenters to "plane the Pirogues; by which they were thinner and lighter by one half," and managing fleets of birch-bark canoes.
The "promised land" description in 1 Nephi 18:24-25 catalogs an abundance of "beasts in the forests of every kind" and "all manner of ore, both of gold, and of silver, and of copper." Lahontan's descriptions of North American abundance are equally effusive: the habitants of Beaupré "live with more ease and conveniency than an infinity of the Gentlemen in France"; the fur trade yields an extraordinary catalog of animal pelts; the Gnacsitares maintain large parks stocked with wild cattle; and a reddish copper medal obtained from a Mozeemlek slave, when melted, "became thereupon heavier, and deeper coloured, and withal somewhat tractable" — suggesting mineral wealth. Father Hennepin, Lahontan's contemporary, described the North American trade as offering "infinite Profit and Advantage" with "an infinity of other Animals."
Both texts present the American continent as a land of extraordinary natural wealth — a "promised land" in 1 Nephi's religious register, a land of economic opportunity in Lahontan's secular one. The convention of describing the Americas as superabundant was standard in European colonial literature and would have been thoroughly familiar to early nineteenth-century readers.
---
### Record-keeping, writing, and durable materials
Nephi writes his account on metal plates — an unusual and distinctive detail. Lahontan does not describe the Tahuglauk as writing on metal, but he documents Indigenous record-keeping practices that are more sophisticated than many European readers expected. His Volume 2 includes a full chapter titled "An Explication of the Savage Hieroglyphicks" with engraved plates showing symbolic writing systems. The Gnacsitares drew "the most exact Maps imaginable" on deer skin, including latitude calculations where they "gave me to know latitudes of all places marked in it, by pointing to the respective places of heavens." The Mozeemlek drew vessel designs "upon the Barks of Trees." The Tahuglauk were attributed "written languages and sophisticated governments" in some secondary summaries of Lahontan's text, though the primary passages focus on their manufactured goods (copper axes, textiles) rather than explicit writing.
The copper medal from the Mozeemlek is worth noting: **a metallic artifact with potential symbolic significance from an interior American civilization.** While Lahontan does not describe writing on metal, the conceptual ingredients — durable record-keeping, hieroglyphic systems, metalworking civilizations in the American interior — are all present in his text and available for recombination by a later author working in a different genre.
---
### A genuinely novel comparison in Book of Mormon source criticism
This analysis appears to represent **a previously unidentified parallel in the critical literature on the Book of Mormon**. Extensive searching across scholarly databases, critical Mormon studies publications, and secular academic work turned up no existing comparison between Lahontan's *New Voyages* and the Book of Mormon. The standard proposed sources in critical scholarship include Ethan Smith's *View of the Hebrews* (1823), Gilbert Hunt's *The Late War* (1816), the King James Bible, and the broader Mound Builder literary tradition. Lahontan has not been part of this conversation.
Yet the structural case for Lahontan's relevance is substantial. His work was the **second most widely read North American travel narrative of the eighteenth century**, available in English from 1703 and circulating through at least 25 editions. Jefferson owned it and recommended it for American youth. It influenced Swift, Voltaire, Montesquieu, and Rousseau. Its themes — Great Spirit theology, cement-and-stone cities near a salt lake, compasses as spirit-operated devices, graduated civilizations in the American interior, storm-tossed Atlantic crossings, exile and authority conflicts — constitute a remarkable inventory of elements that reappear in 1 Nephi and the broader Book of Mormon. Whether Lahontan's text served as a direct source, an indirect influence mediated through the broader cultural atmosphere of early nineteenth-century America, or simply a parallel product of the same European discourse about Indigenous origins, the comparison illuminates the deep roots of the Book of Mormon's narrative architecture in colonial-era travel literature.
Eran Shalev's research on "pseudo-biblicism" provides the best existing framework for understanding this relationship. Shalev argues that "by the time Joseph Smith published The Book of Mormon, Americans had been producing and consuming faux biblical texts for close to a century." The Book of Mormon, on this reading, takes the raw materials of the colonial voyage narrative — storms, compasses, exotic civilizations, natural abundance, exile, factional conflict — and recasts them in biblical idiom. Lahontan's *New Voyages* is one of the richest repositories of exactly these raw materials, and William Davis's work on oral composition (*Visions in a Seer Stone*, University of North Carolina Press, 2020) suggests that Smith drew on years of mental preparation and storytelling, potentially incorporating elements from widely circulated texts about the American continent.
---
### Conclusion: two contested journeys into the American interior
Lahontan's *New Voyages* and the Book of Mormon's 1 Nephi are not the same kind of text — one is a secular epistolary travelogue by a freethinking French exile, the other a pseudo-biblical sacred history dictated by a young American prophet. But they share a narrative grammar that runs deeper than surface genre: the first-person exile narrator; the journey from the known world into uncharted American territory; the compass as a threshold object between technology and spirit; the discovery of increasingly complex civilizations in the continental interior; the civilized/savage binary mapped onto different peoples; the abundance of the American landscape as theological or economic promise; the storms and rebellions that test the journey. The Long River expedition's contested historicity even mirrors the Book of Mormon's — both place elaborate civilizations in the American interior that subsequent exploration failed to confirm, and both have been defended by some scholars and dismissed as fabrication by others.
What this comparison most powerfully demonstrates is that 1 Nephi's narrative structure belongs not to ancient Israelite literary traditions but to the **colonial-era voyage narrative genre** that Lahontan exemplifies. The building blocks of Nephi's story — ocean crossings, divine compasses, promised lands, factional splits, cement cities, Great Spirit theology — were already assembled and circulating in popular English-language texts about North America more than a century before the Book of Mormon's publication. Lahontan did not need to be a direct source to matter; his work was part of the cultural water table from which the Book of Mormon drew.
---
# PART IV: THE MANITOU-COMPASS COLONIAL TRADITION
## The Compass as Manitou: 14 Foundational Colonial Attestations (1588-1820)
**From 1588 to 1820, European explorers, missionaries, and fur traders across North America repeatedly documented Indigenous peoples categorizing compasses, clocks, magnets, and other instruments as spiritually animated — as "manitou," "oki," or "works of gods."** This report presents a comprehensive chronological catalog of every documented instance of this trope located through extensive research, alongside the full Dictionary of Canadianisms on Historical Principles (DCHP-3) entry for "Manitou" and identification of its 1811 compass citation source. The trope constitutes a remarkably persistent thread in colonial literature, spanning over two centuries and appearing across French, English, and American sources — exactly the kind of material available to literate Americans in the 1820s.
---
### The DCHP-3 entry preserves six senses of "Manitou"
The Dictionary of Canadianisms on Historical Principles, 3rd edition (DCHP-3), hosted at dchp.arts.ubc.ca, contains a legacy entry for **"Manitou"** carried forward from the original DCHP-1 (1967, edited by Walter S. Avis et al., published by W.J. Gage Limited). The entry has not been revised in DCHP-2 (2017) or DCHP-3 (2025). Its etymology reads: "< Algonk.; cf. Ojibwa *manitō*." The entry defines six senses:
**Sense 1a — "a spirit; deity"** — with attestations from **1613** ("II y a de certaines personnes entr'eux qui font les Oqui ou Manitous (ainsi appellez par les Algommequins & Montagnais) lesquels se meslent de guarir les malades, penser les blessez, & predire les chose futures"); **1703** ("Matchi [is] the word for evil and Manitou for spirit"); **1789** ("behind the opposite island there was a Manitoe or spirit, in the river, which swallowed every person that approached it"); **1886** and **1936** (references to Manitos of air, earth, and water).
**Sense 1b — "See quote"** — a single attestation, dated **1811**: "This compass . . . not being able to comprehend its action, they called . . . a 'Manitou,' by which they mean 'spirit,' or something incomprehensible and powerful." This is the critical citation. The definition "See quote" indicates the quotation itself serves as the definition — that "manitou" could designate any mysterious, incomprehensible, and powerful object, not just a supernatural being.
**Sense 2** — "the supreme deity of the Crees, Ojibwas, and related tribes, identified by some whites and Christian Indians with God" (cross-reference to **Gitchi Manitou**), with attestations from **1748**, **1863**, **1952**, and **1965**. **Sense 3** — "an idol, charm, or fetish" (1760, 1863, 1921). **Sense 4** — "the Christian God" (cross-reference to **Great Spirit**; 1893, 1926, 1962). **Sense 5** — "a priest or spiritual adviser" (1962). **Sense 6** — cross-reference to **Manitou wheat**. Related DCHP entries include **Gitchi Manitou**, **Matchi Manitou**, **Manitou Stone**, and **Great Spirit**.
The DCHP-3 online interface does not display full bibliographic citations for its quotations — only dates and excerpted text — a limitation of the digitized DCHP-1 data. The original 1967 print edition would contain abbreviated source references.
### The 1811 citation almost certainly comes from John Lambert
The most probable source of the 1811 compass-as-Manitou quotation is **John Lambert's *Travels through Lower Canada, and the United States of North America, in the Years 1806, 1807, and 1808***, first published in three volumes in London by Richard Phillips in **1810** (with subsequent editions in 1813, 1814, and 1816). Lambert (b. c. 1775, England) traveled to Lower Canada in 1806, accompanying his uncle James Campbell on a hemp-cultivation project for the Privy Council. He traveled extensively in Lower Canada and the United States, returning to England via Quebec in 1809.
Volume 1 contains **Chapter XVIII, "Aborigines of North America"** (beginning at approximately p. 363 in the first edition), which discusses Indigenous customs, sagacity, and spiritual beliefs — precisely the context in which a description of Indigenous peoples calling a compass "Manitou" would appear. The slight date discrepancy (1810 on the title page vs. 1811 in the DCHP) likely reflects either a late-1810 publication with 1811 distribution, a bibliographic convention used by the DCHP-1 editors, or a variant issue. Cambridge University Press describes the work as "first published in 1811," supporting the DCHP dating.
An alternative candidate — **Daniel Williams Harmon's *A Journal of Voyages and Travels in the Interior of North America*** (Andover, Mass.: Flagg and Gould, 1820), with journal entries spanning 1800–1819 — is less likely because the DCHP typically dates citations by publication date for published books, not journal entry date, and Harmon's book appeared in 1820. **David Thompson's *Narrative*** (written c. 1846–1850, first published 1916) is also occasionally suggested but is chronologically impossible for an 1811 citation.
---
### A chronological catalog of the "technology as spirit" trope, 1588–1820
What follows is every documented instance located of colonial and early American writers recording Indigenous peoples interpreting European scientific instruments, navigational tools, or technological objects as spiritually animated, as "manitou" or "oki" or "gods," or as possessing supernatural power.
#### 1588 — Thomas Harriot's foundational account at Roanoke
The earliest and single most important English-language attestation comes from **Thomas Harriot, *A Briefe and True Report of the New Found Land of Virginia*** (London, 1588; illustrated edition by Theodor de Bry, Frankfurt, 1590). Harriot, who had learned Carolina Algonquian from Wanchese and Manteo before the 1585–86 Roanoke expedition, wrote:
> "Most thinges they sawe with vs, as **Mathematicall instruments, sea compasses, the vertue of the loadstone in drawing yron, a perspectiue glasse** whereby was shewed manie strange sightes, **burning glasses, wildefire woorkes, gunnes, bookes, writing and reading, spring clocks that seeme to goe of themselues**, and manie other thinges that wee had, were so straunge vnto them, and so farre exceeded their capacities to comprehend the reason and meanes how they should be made and done, **that they thought they were rather the works of gods then of men**, or at the leastwise they had bin giuen and taught vs of the gods."
In the same text, Harriot recorded the Algonquian word **"Mantóac"** meaning "gods" — one of the earliest European documentations of the manitou concept. He also noted: "They beleeue that there are many Gods which they call Mantóac, but of different sortes and degrees." This creates a direct terminological link: the man who first recorded the manitou concept also recorded the trope of instruments-as-divine-works. The text was reprinted in Richard Hakluyt's *Principal Navigations* (1598–1600), ensuring wide circulation.
The passage is remarkable for its **specificity**: Harriot enumerates compasses, lodestones, perspective glasses, burning glasses, guns, books, writing, and spring clocks — the last being highlighted because **they "seeme to goe of themselues,"** exhibiting the autonomous agency that Algonquian ontology identified as the hallmark of spiritual personhood.
#### 1632–1636 — Sagard, Brébeuf, and the Jesuit Relations in Huronia
**Gabriel Sagard, *Le Grand Voyage du pays des Hurons*** (Paris: Denys Moreau, 1632), describes reactions of the Wendat (Huron) people to European objects during his 1623–24 sojourn. Sagard describes the Wendat concept of **"oki"** — spiritual power applied to anything exhibiting extraordinary qualities — and records general astonishment at European technology, though no single isolated compass passage has been identified in the available digitized text.
**Jean de Brébeuf, S.J.**, writing from the Huron mission at Ihonatiria near Georgian Bay, provides the most detailed account in the **Relation of 1635** (Thwaites, *Jesuit Relations and Allied Documents*, Vol. VIII, Cleveland: Burrows Brothers, 1897). As the Thwaites preface summarizes:
> "Brébeuf describes his cabin, which is at once a dwelling and a church; and relates the astonishment of the natives at the sight of various articles brought by the French, — **a small mill, a clock (which the Indians thought was alive), a loadstone, a magnifying glass, etc., — but especially at the art of writing, which is utterly incomprehensible to their simple minds.**"
The **clock** generated the most sustained wonder. The Hurons called it **"the Captain of the Day"** because it governed the missionaries' schedule. Brébeuf recorded in the original French:
> "Ils ont pensé qu'elle entendoit, principalement quand, pour rire, quelqu'vn de nos Francois s'escrioit au dernier coup de marteau, c'est assez sonné, et que tout aussi tost elle se taisoit. Ils l'appellent le Capitaine du iour. Quand elle sonne, ils disent qu'elle parle."
("They thought it could hear, especially when, for fun, one of our Frenchmen cried out at the last stroke of the hammer, 'that is enough ringing,' and it immediately fell silent. They call it the Captain of the Day. When it strikes, they say it speaks.")
**They asked what it ate.** They sat for hours waiting to hear it speak. The **lodestone** (magnet attracting iron), **magnifying glass** (transforming a flea into "a frightful monster"), and a **multiplying lens** (showing "the same object eleven times repeated") were also displayed. Brébeuf explicitly connected this to conversion strategy, writing in the **Relation of 1636** (Thwaites Vol. X, p. 33):
> "All this serves to gain their affection, and make them more docile in respect to the admirable and incomprehensible mysteries of our Faith; for the opinion they have of our genius and capacity makes them believe whatever we tell them."
In the same Relation, Brébeuf defined the key ontological category: "Such persons they call **oki, the same name as they give to demons**" — establishing that "oki" was applied to anything displaying extraordinary, super-human power, whether persons, objects, or phenomena.
#### 1634 — Paul Le Jeune and the Montagnais concept of "manitousiou"
**Paul Le Jeune, S.J.**, in his **Relation of 1634** (Thwaites Vols. VI–VII), provides the Algonquian parallel to Brébeuf's Wendat account. Wintering with the Montagnais (Innu) in 1633–34, Le Jeune described their spiritual framework: shamans were called **"manitousiou"** — "the most famous sorcerer, or *manitousiou*, (thus they call these jugglers) of all the country." He noted the Montagnais language's **animate/inanimate distinction**, observing that certain European objects were classified as animate nouns, suggesting perception of spiritual qualities: "they join with animate things a number of things that have no souls, as tobacco, apples, etc." This grammatical evidence corroborates the ontological claim that objects exhibiting agency were understood as persons.
#### 1643 — Roger Williams and "Manittôwock, They are Gods"
**Roger Williams, *A Key into the Language of America*** (London: Gregory Dexter, 1643), Chapter 21, "Of Religion, the soule, &c.," records the foundational English-language attestation of the manitou concept applied to European technology:
> "Besides there is a generall Custome amongst them, at the apprehension of any Excellency in Men, Women, Birds Beasts, Fish, &c. to cry out **Manittóo**, that is, **it is a God**, as thus if they see one man excell others in Wisdome, Valour, strength, Activity &c. they cry out Manittóo A God: and therefore when they talke amongst themselves of the **English ships, and great buildings, of the plowing of their Fields, and especially of Bookes and Letters**, they will end thus: **Manittôwock They are Gods**."
Williams interprets this as a "strong Conviction naturall in the soule of man, that God is filling all things, and places." The passage does not mention compasses specifically but establishes the broader pattern: **any impressive European achievement was designated manitou** — with ships, buildings, agricultural technology, and especially literacy singled out.
#### 1668–1671 — Father Claude Allouez among the Great Lakes nations
**Father Claude Allouez, S.J.**, working among the Ottawa, Mascouten, and other Great Lakes peoples in the 1660s–1680s, provides multiple crucial attestations in the Jesuit Relations:
In the **1668 Relation** (Thwaites Vol. L, pp. 249–311), Allouez defined the ontological framework:
> "The savages of these regions recognize no sovereign master of Heaven and Earth, but believe there are many spirits, some of whom are beneficent, as the Sun, the Moon, the lake, rivers, and woods; others malevolent, as the adder, the dragon, cold, and storms. **And, in general, whatever seems to them either helpful or hurtful they call a Manitou, and pay it the worship and veneration which we render only to the true God.**"
This passage is critical because it establishes that **any powerful or impressive object — including European instruments — would automatically be classified as manitou.**
In the **1669–1670 Relation** (Thwaites Vol. LIV, pp. 197–214), at the Mascouten village on the Upper Fox River, Allouez himself was addressed as Manitou:
> "This people came in crowds to meet us, in order to see, as they said, **the Manitou, who was coming to their country.**"
An old man addressed him directly: "This is well, black gown, that thou comest to visit us. Take pity on us; **thou art a Manitou**; we give thee tobacco to smoke."
A remarkable archaeological footnote in the Thwaites edition (Vol. LIV, editorial footnote 13) reports: **"In 1902 a combined sun-dial and compass of French manufacture was found on the site of this village. It apparently dates from the seventeenth century."** A compass-sundial instrument was physically present at the very village where Allouez was called "Manitou."
The **1670–71 Relation** (Thwaites Vol. LV) summarizes: **"All these tribes regard the Fathers as manitous, or spirits."**
#### 1680/1697 — Father Hennepin's compass among the Sioux
**Father Louis Hennepin, *Nouvelle Découverte d'un très grand Pays*** (Utrecht, 1697; English edition: *A New Discovery of a Vast Country in America*, London: M. Bentley et al., 1698) contains a chapter explicitly devoted to Indigenous fascination with his compass. **Chapter LV** is titled:
> "The Author is like to be famish'd. **They admire his Compass, and an Iron-Pot which he has.** He makes a Dictionary, and instructs them in Points of Religion, in relation to Polygamy and Celebacy."
This chapter describes events during Hennepin's captivity among the Sioux (Issati/Nadouessians) in 1680, after he and two companions were captured near Lake Pepin on the upper Mississippi. The Sioux's admiration of his compass — the needle's mysterious, self-directed behavior — was significant enough to headline the chapter. The full chapter text, available in the Thwaites 1903 reprint edition (Chicago: A.C. McClurg & Co.), would contain the detailed passage describing their reaction. Hennepin's **Chapter XXIX**, "Of the Superstition of the Savages, and their ridiculous Beliefs," provides the conceptual framework for understanding why the compass elicited spiritual interpretation.
#### 1703 — Lahontan's thousand machines "moved by Spirits"
**Baron de Lahontan, *New Voyages to North-America*** (London: H. Bonwicke et al., 1703), Volume II, in the Appendix section (Letter IV, dated Lisbon, July 4, 1695), contains the most sweeping statement of this trope:
> "**The Savages believe that a Watch, a Compass, and a thousand other Machines are moved by Spirits**; for your ignorant and clownish People form extravagant Ideas of every thing that surpasses their Imagination. The Laplanders and the Tartarian Kalmouks ador'd Strangers for playing Legerdemain Tricks. The Fire-eater at Paris pass'd a long while for a Magician. The Portuguese burnt a Horse that did wonderful things, and his Owner had enough to do to make his escape, because they took him for a Conjurer."
This passage (confirmed in the Thwaites scholarly edition, Chicago: A.C. McClurg & Co., 1905, Vol. II, p. 906) is notable because Lahontan **universalizes the trope** — comparing Indigenous reactions to European responses to unfamiliar technology — and because he names **three specific instruments**: watch, compass, and the open category of "a thousand other Machines." The original French reads "boussole mûë par des Esprits" — compass moved by spirits.
#### 1748 — The DCHP attestation on the "received Opinion"
The DCHP-3 records an unattributed 1748 attestation under sense 2: "It is a received Opinion amongst the Indians in those Parts, that there are two Spirits, one whom they call Manitou, to which Spirit, they attribute all the Perfections of the Deity." This likely derives from **Peter Kalm's *Travels into North America*** (English translation by John Reinhold Forster, 1770–71, describing the Swedish naturalist's 1748–51 journey) or a similar mid-eighteenth-century account.
#### 1778 — Carver and the popularization of the "Great Spirit"
**Jonathan Carver, *Travels Through the Interior Parts of North America, 1766–1768*** (London, 1778), extensively discusses Indigenous religion and popularized the term "Great Spirit" in English. While no specific passage about compasses being interpreted as spiritual was confirmed, Carver describes Winnebago and Sioux spiritual practices in detail and provides the conceptual backdrop. His *Travels* went through numerous editions and was **one of the most widely read accounts of Native Americans in the late eighteenth and early nineteenth centuries**.
#### 1805–1806 — Lewis and Clark demonstrate the compass as supernatural
**William Clark's journal entry of April 2, 1806**, near the confluence of the Columbia and Willamette rivers among Upper Chinookan people, provides the most vivid eyewitness account of the compass deliberately used to invoke spiritual awe:
> "I had a Small pece of port fire match in my pocket, off of which I cut a pece one inch in length & put it into the fire and took out my pocket Compas and Set myself doun on a mat on one Side of the fire, and **a magnet which was in the top of my ink Stand the port fire cought and burned vehemently, which changed the Colour of the fire; with the Magnit I turned the Needle of the Compas about very briskly; which astonished and alarmed these nativs** and they laid Several parsels of Wappato at my feet, & begged of me to take out the bad fire... **a very old blind man was speaking with great vehemence, apparently imploring his god.**"
**Meriwether Lewis's journal entry of August 17, 1805**, among the Shoshone, records: "I also shot my air-gun which was so perfectly incomprehensible that they immediately denominated it **the great medicine**." The Lewis and Clark journals were first published in **1814** (Nicholas Biddle/Paul Allen paraphrase edition), placing them squarely in the pre-1830 accessible literature.
#### 1809–1820 — The fur trade sources
**Alexander Henry the Elder, *Travels and Adventures in Canada and the Indian Territories*** (New York: I. Riley, 1809), describes Ojibwe spiritual practices extensively, including the "Manitow" or god-bag containing objects of spiritual power. While no isolated compass passage was confirmed, his work establishes the framework whereby **any object perceived as having spiritual power was classified as manitou**.
**John Lambert, *Travels through Lower Canada*** (London, 1810/1811), provides the DCHP's 1811 citation — the passage describing Indigenous peoples calling a compass "Manitou, by which they mean spirit, or something incomprehensible and powerful" — in Chapter XVIII on "Aborigines of North America."
**Daniel Williams Harmon, *A Journal of Voyages and Travels in the Interior of North America*** (Andover, Mass.: Flagg and Gould, 1820), covering his experiences from 1800–1819 among the Cree, Ojibwe, and other nations at North West Company posts, contains extensive observations of Indigenous spiritual practices and the manitou concept.
**John Heckewelder, *An Account of the History, Manners, and Customs of the Indian Nations*** (Philadelphia: *Transactions of the American Philosophical Society*, 1819), records: "The Indian considers himself as being created by an all-powerful, wise and benevolent Manitou, all that he possesses, all that he enjoys, he looks upon as given to him or allotted for his use by the Great Spirit who gives him life." Heckewelder's work was widely influential in American intellectual circles and was consulted by James Fenimore Cooper.
**David Thompson** (1770–1857), the great surveyor, earned the Salish/Ktunaxa name **"Koo-Koo-Sint"** ("Stargazer") for his constant use of compass, sextant, telescope, and watches. His narrative records Indigenous bewilderment at his instruments and his engagement with the manitou concept: "And I had to give up my doctrine of Instinct to that of their Manito." Though Thompson's *Narrative* was not published until 1916, his experiences (1784–1812) represent the lived reality of how instruments were perceived.
### The ontological framework behind the trope
Modern scholarship cautions against reading these colonial accounts at face value. **Evan Haefeli** ("On First Contact and Apotheosis," *Ethnohistory* 54:3, 2007) demonstrates that Europeans consistently misread Indigenous use of "manitou" as worship or apotheosis: "The native accounts never claimed the Europeans were gods in any Christian sense of the term. Instead, their words (in this case Manitou) reflected **an understanding of the power and danger of the encounter.**"
The **Encyclopedia of Religion** entry (James B. Jeffries, 2005) explains the ontological logic: "a living presence itself is indicated by agency — that is, by a movement, force, or power of any kind. Hence, **all powers are persons.**" A compass needle pointing north without visible cause, a clock striking the hours without being touched, a magnet drawing iron through empty space — all exhibited the autonomous agency that Algonquian ontology identified as the hallmark of personhood. They were not "worshipped" but **categorized as spiritually powerful, potentially dangerous entities** within an existing framework.
**Neal Salisbury's** *Manitou and Providence* (Oxford UP, 1982) captures the fundamental tension: the Algonquian concept of manitou (spiritual power pervading the world) versus the Puritan concept of providence. **A. Irving Hallowell's** foundational "Ojibwa Ontology, Behavior and World View" (1960) established that in Ojibwe thought, personhood is not limited to humans — any entity demonstrating agency qualifies as an "other-than-human person."
### Implications for Book of Mormon Liahona scholarship
The Book of Mormon (published March 1830) describes the **Liahona** as "a round ball of curious workmanship" made of "fine brass" containing "two spindles," one of which "pointed the way whither we should go into the wilderness" (1 Nephi 16:10), operating "according to the faith and diligence and heed" given to it (1 Nephi 16:28), ceasing to function when faith was lost, and later identified as "Liahona, which is, being interpreted, a compass" (Alma 37:38).
**No scholar has yet explicitly connected the colonial manitou-compass trope to the Liahona.** The parallels are striking: both involve a compass-like instrument with spiritually contingent operation; both describe objects whose function depends on the spiritual state of the user; both involve "curious" or marvelous brass construction. The colonial trope was **widely disseminated in exactly the literature available in early nineteenth-century New England** — Lambert's *Travels* (four London editions by 1816), Lahontan's *New Voyages* (multiple English editions), the Lewis and Clark journals (1814), Heckewelder's account (1819), and numerous reprints of the Jesuit Relations material through Charlevoix and others.
Existing naturalistic scholarship has identified other potential literary precedents: **Gilbert J. Hunt's *The Late War Between the United States and Great Britain*** (1816) describes "a ball that is partly made of brass with curious works, like unto a clock"; **Jonathan Swift's *Gulliver's Travels*** (1726) has Lilliputians describing Gulliver's pocket watch as an "oracle" — "a globe, half silver, and half of some transparent metal" that "pointed out the time for every action of his life"; and **Walter Franklin Pierce** (1917) connected the Liahona to Masonic symbols. The manitou-compass trope represents a complementary but unexplored avenue: the widely circulated colonial-era concept of a **navigation instrument understood as spiritually animated and responsive to spiritual conditions**.
### Conclusion
The documentary record yields **at least fourteen distinct sources** between 1588 and 1820 describing Indigenous peoples interpreting European instruments as spiritually animated. The trope originates with Harriot's 1588 account of Roanoke Algonquians viewing "mathematicall instruments, sea compasses... spring clocks" as "works of gods," runs through the Jesuit Relations' detailed accounts of Wendat and Montagnais reactions to clocks, lodestones, and writing (1632–1671), and persists through Lahontan's 1703 generalization about watches and compasses "moved by Spirits," Hennepin's compass among the Sioux (1680/1697), Clark's deliberate use of compass-magic in 1806, and Lambert's 1811 account of the compass as "Manitou." The term "manitou" itself — meaning spiritual power identified by autonomous agency — provides the conceptual key. European instruments moved without visible cause, and in Algonquian ontology, **movement equals personhood**. The trope was not an isolated curiosity but a recurrent, widely published feature of colonial literature that would have been accessible to any well-read American of the 1820s.
---
## Beyond the Fourteen: 30+ Additional Colonial Sources
**At least 30 additional primary sources and 15 major scholarly works document the trope of Indigenous peoples interpreting European technology through spiritual frameworks — extending far beyond North America into Mesoamerican, South American, Brazilian, and Pacific contexts.** The most significant new finds include Captain John Smith's 1608/1624 compass-dial passage (with explicit needle/spindle language), the Florentine Codex's Nahua "teotl" classifications of Spanish technology, Le Mercier's 1637–38 reversal of the clock from spiritual ally to "demon of death" (documenting conditional reinterpretation), Nicolas Perrot's account of Potawatomi ritual incensing of iron tools, and Hawaiian traditions recording an explicit conditional test of Cook's divinity. Bruce M. White's 1994 *Ethnohistory* article, "Encounters with Spirits," is the single most important secondary work directly addressing this trope. No peer-reviewed scholarship yet connects this colonial literary tradition to the Book of Mormon's Liahona, representing a significant scholarly gap.
---
### The compass dial and the fly: Captain John Smith (1608/1624)
The most important new primary source is **Captain John Smith's compass demonstration to Opechancanough** in December 1607, recorded in *A True Relation* (1608) and expanded in *The Generall Historie of Virginia* (1624). Smith describes showing the Powhatan leader "a round Ivory double compass dial":
> "Much they marveled at **the playing of the Fly and Needle**, which they could see so plainly, and yet not touch it, because of the glass that covered them… But when he demonstrated by that Globe-like jewel, the roundness of the earth, and skies, the sphere of the Sun, Moon, and Stars… they all stood as amazed with admiration."
The compass proved so powerful that when Opechancanough later "holding up the Compass in his hand," warriors "all laid down their Bows and Arrows." Smith then endured a three-day divination ritual by the *kwiocosuk* (Powhatan shamans) who sought to determine his spiritual nature. This passage is extraordinary for several reasons: **it explicitly names the needle** ("the Fly and Needle"), describes the mysterious visible-but-untouchable quality of the glass-enclosed instrument, documents the compass functioning as a spiritual object powerful enough to halt violence, and triggered a formal shamanic investigation. The phrase "playing of the Fly and Needle" directly parallels the spindle language of compass descriptions. Published in English before 1830, this was one of the most widely reprinted texts in colonial American literature.
**William Strachey's** *The Historie of Travell into Virginia Britania* (1612) retells and expands Smith's account, adding detail about Powhatan religion and the concept of **Okee** — the spiritual force through which such instruments would have been interpreted. **Robert Beverley's** *The History and Present State of Virginia* (1705) similarly draws on Smith's compass incident and independently describes how Powhatan spiritual concepts shaped reactions to European objects.
---
### "Demon of death": Le Mercier's conditional reversal (1637–38)
Among the Jesuit Relations, the most striking new find is **François-Joseph Le Mercier's** account in **Volume XV** (Thwaites edition), composed at Ossossané on June 9, 1638. During the devastating 1637–38 smallpox epidemic among the Wendat (Huron), the same technologies that Brébeuf had described as objects of wonder in 1635 were radically reinterpreted:
- The **clock**, previously celebrated as "Captain of the Day," was now regarded as **"the demon of death"** and the Wendat demanded it be "put out of sight"
- A **weather vane** mounted on a tree aroused deep suspicion; a chief demanded: "What does that piece of cloth mean, that I see placed so high up?"
- **Litanies sung at vespers** were interpreted as evil incantations or spells
- Life-size **religious paintings** of Christ and the Virgin Mary were believed to be casting a "wicked spell" causing the epidemic
- Even **French kettles** were rejected: "one entire village decided no longer to use French kettles, imagining that everything which came in any way from us was capable of communicating the disease to them"
**This is the clearest documentation of conditional spiritual reinterpretation of European technology** — the identical instrument shifts from benevolent spiritual agent to malevolent one depending on circumstances. The spiritual classification was not fixed but dynamic, responsive to outcomes. This directly parallels the conditional-operation motif where an instrument's function depends on the spiritual state surrounding its use.
Additional Jesuit Relations volumes containing relevant material not in the user's existing list include **Volume IX** (Le Jeune 1636), where a Montagnais man declares "I believe that your Manitou is all-powerful; tell him to cure me, and I will give thee ten Beavers," explicitly treating the Jesuits' spiritual power and technological capabilities as a superior form of manitou. **Volume XIX** (Vimont 1640) describes a wife burning leather and rubbing it on her husband's head "that she might by this foul odor disgust the Manitou." **Volume III** (Pierre Biard 1611–1616) records the first Jesuit in Acadia being "regarded by the Mi'kmaq as a European with shamanistic powers." **Volume LIX** (Marquette 1673) documents how Menominee Indians warned of "horrible monsters and demons" on the Mississippi, framing the unknown through spiritual categories.
---
### Iron tools incensed with tobacco: Perrot, Radisson, and the western Great Lakes
**Nicolas Perrot** (c. 1665–1670, memoir written c. 1700–1717, published in E.H. Blair's *Indian Tribes of the Upper Mississippi Valley*, 1911) provides one of the most vivid accounts of European technology being received as spiritual. When Perrot first visited western Great Lakes nations (likely Potawatomi and Miami), they declared:
> "Thou art one of the chief spirits, since thou usest iron; it is for thee to rule and protect all men. Praised be the Sun, who has instructed thee and sent thee to our country. **They adored him as a god; they took his knives and hatchets and incensed them with the tobacco-smoke from their mouths.**"
A Miami chief explicitly stated he "would prefer that Spirit to his own, **who had not taught them to make hatchets, kettles, and all else that men need**." The ritual incensing of iron implements with tobacco smoke represents a formal spiritual consecration of manufactured objects — the tools were not merely admired but incorporated into ceremonial practice as objects possessing spiritual power.
**Pierre-Esprit Radisson** (c. 1659–60, narrative written c. 1668–69 in English, published 1885) describes a complementary scene near Lake Superior among the Ojibwe. When they saw his fort: "They are astonied calling us every foot **devils** to have made such a machine." Radisson and Des Groseilliers deliberately cultivated the perception of "superior spiritual powers," leveraging their exclusive mastery of firearms and knowledge of iron manufacturing.
---
### Teotl and teteo: Nahua spiritual categories for Spanish technology
The **Florentine Codex** (Bernardino de Sahagún, completed c. 1577, compiled from Nahua elder testimony c. 1553–55) is the richest single source for Mesoamerican interpretations of European technology. Book 12 documents how Nahua messengers reported Spanish technology to Motecuhzoma in terms saturated with supernatural significance. Cannons were described as having "entrails" that disgorge stone balls "shooting sparks and raining fire." Horses were categorized as supernatural "deer." The Spaniards themselves were called **teteo** (plural of **teotl** — a concept meaning sacred energy, divine force, or cosmic power, not simply "god" in the Western sense).
The prefix **teo-** was applied broadly to Spanish things. The corrupted form "teules" became the standard Nahua term for Spaniards, implying "mysterious and supernatural power." Motecuhzoma sent "prophets and wizards" to use magical means against the Spanish, but "they failed completely." Scholars including **Molly Bassett** (*The Fate of Earthly Things*, 2015) and **Guilhem Olivier** emphasize that teotl was far more nuanced than "god" — it referred to numinous, powerful energy-in-motion. Indigenous descriptions of European technology as "teotl" reflected recognition of extraordinary power within an existing ontological framework, not naive worship.
**Bernal Díaz del Castillo's** *True History of the Conquest of New Spain* (~1568, published 1632; first English translation 1800 by Maurice Keatinge — available before 1830) provides the Spanish-perspective counterpart, documenting how Tlaxcalan prophecy had anticipated "the coming of men from distant lands in the direction of the sunrise."
---
### The book that would not speak: Cajamarca and Andean encounters
The encounter at **Cajamarca on November 16, 1532**, where Atahualpa was presented with a European book described as containing "God's own voice," represents the most dramatic technology-as-spirit incident in South American colonial records. Multiple primary sources document it from different angles:
- **Francisco de Xerez** (*Verdadera relación de la conquista del Perú*, 1534) — Pizarro's notary, earliest published account
- **Tito Cusi Yupanqui** (indigenous account, 1570) — called it "God's writing"
- **Garcilaso de la Vega** (*Royal Commentaries of the Incas*, Part 2, 1617; **English translation by Sir Paul Rycaut, 1685** — available before 1830)
Atahualpa, presented with a book said to contain God's voice, reportedly put it to his ear. Hearing nothing, he dropped or threw it — an act taken as sacrilege justifying the Spanish attack. The Andean concept of **huaca** (sacred objects, places, or beings charged with power) and the thunder god **Illapa** (potentially connected to Indigenous interpretations of gunfire as divine thunder) provide the spiritual framework through which Inca peoples would have processed European technology. **José de Acosta's** *Natural and Moral History of the Indies* (1590; **English translation by Edward Grimston, 1604** — available before 1830) discusses the huaca concept extensively, providing the theological framework for understanding how remarkable objects acquire sacred status in Andean thought.
---
### "Walking islands" and "thunderstruck" men: earlier English and Italian accounts
**Giovanni da Verrazzano's** 1524 letter to King Francis I describes an encounter on the Atlantic coast where an Indigenous man witnessed the French firing a gun: "He trembled all over with fear as we fired a shot. **He remained as if thunderstruck, and prayed, worshiping like a monk, pointing his finger to the sky**, and indicating the sea and the ship, he appeared to bless us." This is among the earliest documented instances of an Indigenous person interpreting European technology through an explicitly spiritual-gestural response. Verrazzano also notes Indigenous preference for **"little bells"** (brass) over gold or silk — brass bells were consistently among the most desired European trade items, valued within Indigenous cosmological frameworks that privileged shiny, sonorous materials.
