**William Wood's** *New England's Prospect* (1634, published in English) records that Massachusetts Bay Algonquians "took the first ship they saw for a **walking island**, the mast to be a tree, the sail white clouds, and the discharging of ordnance for lightning and thunder." They called out: "What much hoggery, so big walk, and so big speak, and by and by kill." The ship is conceptualized not as a manufactured object but as an animate feature of the natural-spiritual landscape.
**Samuel de Champlain** (Voyages, multiple volumes 1603–1632) provides the crucial definitional passage connecting the oqui/manitou concept to technological wonder: "whenever they see a man doing something extraordinary, or who is more capable than usual… they call him **oqui**" (Huron) or **manitou** (Algonquian). This explicitly establishes that anyone performing extraordinary feats — which would include operating mysterious instruments — was classified within the spirit/manitou category.
---
### Koo-Koo-Sint and the sextant: fur trade-era accounts
**David Thompson** (narrative written c. 1848, published 1916) provides a late but compelling account. The Piegan called Thompson **"Koo-Koo-Sint"** ("You Who Look at the Stars" or "The Stargazer") because of his constant use of his sextant for astronomical observations. **Some believed he could see the future in the stars**, interpreting his surveying instruments as divination tools. The Piegan also "strongly believed that bears were Thompson's supernatural protectors" — when three grizzly bears appeared during an attack on his party, this was taken as proof of his spiritual power. Thompson's brass sextant, compass, and telescope were his most prized possessions; in old age he pawned his sextant to buy food.
**John Heckewelder's** *An Account of the History, Manners, and Customs of the Indian Nations* (1818/1819 — **available before 1830**) preserves a Delaware/Lenape oral tradition about first seeing Henry Hudson's *Half Moon* in 1609. The ship was concluded to be "a remarkably large house in which **the Mannitto** (the Great or Supreme Being) himself was." A man "dressed entirely in red, who must be the Mannitto himself" offered liquor in a glass; a warrior drank it, staggered, and fell — the assembly believed he had died. The glass vessel and its contents were treated as sacred gifts from the Mannitto "whose refusal could provoke his wrath and bring destruction on them" — a clear conditional-operation concept.
**John R. Jewitt's** *Narrative of the Adventures and Sufferings of John R. Jewitt* (1815, **available before 1830**) provides a rare captivity account from the Pacific Northwest among the Nuu-chah-nulth (Nootka), documenting their material culture, spiritual practices, and attitudes toward European goods from an insider perspective.
---
### Mana, akua, and the conditional test: Pacific encounters
Captain Cook's arrival at Kealakekua Bay, Hawaiʻi, in January 1779 produced what **Marshall Sahlins** (*Islands of History*, 1985; *How "Natives" Think*, 1995) argues was a genuine identification of Cook with the god **Lono**, based on his arrival during the Makahiki festival, his ship's rigging resembling Lono ritual artifacts, and his clockwise circumnavigation mirroring Lono processions. The Hawaiian concept of **mana** — spiritual power that can be gained or lost through actions — structured all interpretations of European technology. Ships, guns, and material culture were assessed through the **mana/tapu** framework.
Most significant for the conditional-operation theme is **Samuel Kamakau's** (19th-century Hawaiian historian) recorded test of divinity: "Here is the test of a god: **if we tempt them and they do not open their gourd container which holds their ancestral gods then they are themselves gods, but if they open the sacred gourds, then they are not gods** — they are foreigners." Cook's crew failed this test. This is an **explicit conditional assessment** — divinity determined by moral/spiritual behavior, directly paralleling the conditional-instrument motif.
In Melanesian traditions, objects possessing **mana** could lose effectiveness: "if it loses its power, **magical rituals will be enacted to strengthen it**." This conditional framework — instruments working or failing based on spiritual conditions — is a widespread Pacific concept that directly parallels the Algonquian and Iroquoian frameworks.
**Anne Salmond's** *Two Worlds: First Meetings between Maori and Europeans 1642–1772* (1991) documents that when HMS *Endeavour* appeared in 1769, Māori understood the ship as coming from **te pō** — the dark realm of ancestors and gods. The horizon was a **tapu** zone "where almost anything might happen." The Tahitian priest Tupaia, recognized as a **tohunga** with extraordinary mana, "claimed credit for the power of Cook's muskets and cannon."
---
### Brazilian and Tupinambá encounters
**Hans Staden's** *Warhaftige Historia* (1557; Latin and multilingual editions through de Bry's *Grand Voyages*, 1593; modern English by Neil Whitehead, Duke UP, 2008) documents how the Tupinambá accepted Staden's claims that prayer and Christianity gave him supernatural power — he "cured the tribal king and his household from illness through the power of prayer," and the Tupinambá embraced him. **Jean de Léry's** *Histoire d'un voyage fait dans la terre du Brésil* (1578; partial English in Samuel Purchas's *Hakluytus Posthumus*, 1625 — **available before 1830**) describes Tupinambá rituals centered on **maracas** (rattles believed to contain spirits), establishing the spiritual framework through which manufactured objects could be understood as spirit-vessels.
**Ramón Pané's** *Relación acerca de las antigüedades de los indios* (c. 1494–1498) — the first European ethnography of the Americas — documents the Taíno concept of **zemis**: material objects (stones, bones, carved figures) inhabited by supernatural powers. This zemi framework, where physical objects serve as vessels for spiritual agency, provides the Caribbean conceptual basis through which European instruments would naturally have been interpreted.
---
### The scholarly architecture: fifteen key secondary works
The secondary literature on this trope clusters around several complementary frameworks:
- **Bruce M. White, "Encounters with Spirits: Ojibwa and Dakota Theories about the French and Their Merchandise,"** *Ethnohistory* 41:3 (1994): 369–405. The single most directly relevant scholarly article. White demonstrates that both Ojibwa ("Manidoo") and Dakota ("wašicun") applied spiritual terms to the French specifically because of their technology, which was deemed "beyond the power of ordinary human beings." Cites **Pierre-Antoine Tabeau** (late 18th-century Missouri River fur trader): "a new nation is always disposed in favor of the whites, it worships them, sometimes, up to the point of superstition."
- **Christopher L. Miller and George R. Hamell, "A New Perspective on Indian-White Contact,"** *Journal of American History* 73:2 (1986): 311–328. Argues European trade goods (beads, metals, mirrors) were incorporated into pre-existing Indigenous cosmological categories associating shiny, translucent, and sonorous materials with spiritual power. **George Hamell's** earlier "Trading in Metaphors" (1983) establishes that brass, copper, glass, and crystal occupied the same symbolic space as Indigenous sacred materials (quartz, native copper, shell). This directly explains why **brass** instruments would carry special spiritual significance.
- **A. Irving Hallowell, "Ojibwa Ontology, Behavior, and World View"** (1960). The foundational text establishing that the Ojibwe animate/inanimate distinction does not correspond to Western living/non-living categories. The famous elder's response — "not all stones are alive, but some are" — establishes that objects exist on a spectrum of potential personhood. **A compass needle that moves autonomously would naturally be classified as animate and potentially a "person" within this ontology.**
- **Neal Salisbury, *Manitou and Providence*** (1982) reverses the traditional colonial perspective, showing how the manitou concept — spiritual power permeating the natural world — structured all Indigenous responses to European arrival. **Michael Adas, *Machines as the Measure of Men*** (1989) traces how Europeans weaponized technological demonstrations as metrics of civilizational superiority. **Stephen Greenblatt, *Marvelous Possessions*** (1991) theorizes "wonder" as the central mechanism of colonial encounter and appropriation. **Cornelius Jaenen, *Friend and Foe*** (1976) documents specific French-Indigenous instances of technology classified within oki/manitou frameworks. **Kenneth Morrison, *The Solidarity of Kin*** (2002) reconstructs Algonkian "indigenous modes of rationality" that shaped encounters with missionary technology. **Daniel Richter, *Facing East from Indian Country*** (2001) demonstrates how Indigenous peoples "discovered Europeans and struggled to make sense of a new world" on their own terms.
- **Gananath Obeyesekere, *The Apotheosis of Captain Cook*** (1992) provides the essential counter-argument: that narratives of Indigenous peoples deifying Europeans and their technology may be European projections rather than accurate reports of Indigenous belief. **Tzvetan Todorov, *The Conquest of America*** (1984) analyzes the semiotic asymmetries that structured the Cortés-Aztec encounter.
---
### Brass, spindles, and conditional operation: a synthesis
Three themes the user specifically flagged appear across the newly identified sources in ways worth consolidating:
**Brass** features prominently in the material culture of colonial encounters. Hamell's framework demonstrates that brass and copper occupied a pre-existing Indigenous symbolic category associated with spiritual power — shiny, sonorous metals were cosmologically linked to underwater spirit beings and the power of light. Verrazzano's 1524 account notes Indigenous preference for **brass bells** over gold. Perrot's account specifically documents tobacco-incensing of metal implements. Colonial-era compasses, astrolabes, and sundials were predominantly manufactured in brass, making brass the default material of the "spirit instrument."
**Needles and spindles** are most explicitly documented in Smith's 1608/1624 passage — **"the playing of the Fly and Needle"** — where the compass needle's autonomous movement, visible through glass but untouchable, provoked both wonder and spiritual investigation. Harriot's 1588 account of "sea compasses" and "spring clocks that seem to go of themselves" similarly foregrounds autonomous mechanical motion as the trigger for spiritual classification.
**Conditional operation** — instruments working or failing based on spiritual circumstances — is documented most clearly in three sources: Le Mercier's 1637–38 account showing the Wendat clock shifting from benevolent to malevolent spiritual agent during an epidemic; Kamakau's Hawaiian conditional test of Cook's divinity; and the broader Melanesian framework where objects possessing mana require ritual renewal when their power wanes. The Algonquian manitou concept is inherently conditional — manitou can be "increased or decreased, lost or found, and transferred" based on moral and spiritual conduct.
---
### Conclusion: a literary tradition deeper and wider than expected
This research reveals that the "instruments as spirits" trope is not a scattered curiosity but a **pervasive structural feature of colonial encounter literature across five continents and three centuries**. The trope operates through remarkably parallel Indigenous frameworks — manitou, teotl, mana, zemi, huaca, oki, wakan, orenda — all sharing the core ontological principle that objects exhibiting autonomous agency, extraordinary power, or unusual material properties participate in the spiritual order. The Algonquian linguistic system, which grammatically classifies objects as animate or inanimate based on their perceived agency rather than biological life, provides the deepest explanatory framework. A compass needle that moves on its own, under glass, responding to invisible forces, would be a paradigmatic case of an object warranting animate classification — literally, a thing with a spirit inside it.
The absence of any peer-reviewed scholarship connecting this colonial literary tradition to the Book of Mormon's Liahona represents a significant gap. Every defining feature of the Liahona — brass material, interior spindles, directional function, conditional operation based on faith, "curious workmanship" — maps precisely onto the colonial "manitou-compass" tradition documented in sources that were widely available in English by the 1820s. Smith's compass passage, Harriot's account, Lahontan's "Watch, Compass, and thousand other Machines moved by Spirits," Heckewelder's Delaware Mannitto narrative, and numerous other pre-1830 English-language texts constitute a rich cultural reservoir from which these specific narrative elements could have been drawn.
---
# PART V: THE GENEALOGY OF "CURIOUS WORKMANSHIP"
## The Literary Genealogy of "Curious Workmanship" Before 1830
**The exact phrase "curious workmanship" does not appear anywhere in the King James Bible**, yet it surfaces five times in the Book of Mormon and its prefatory material. Far from being a biblical quotation, the phrase was a standard English collocation that circulated primarily through translations of classical antiquity—especially Whiston's enormously popular *Josephus*—and through biblical commentary traditions that expanded on KJV craft vocabulary. The most striking pre-1830 parallel appears in Gilbert Hunt's *The Late War* (1816), a pseudo-biblical school textbook published in New York that uses the phrase four times and contains a passage combining "brass," "ball," "curious works," and "clock" in a single description—matching four key attributes of the Book of Mormon's Liahona. No prior scholar has produced a systematic philological genealogy of this phrase; this represents a genuine gap in the source-critical literature.
---
### The KJV provides building blocks but never the exact phrase
The King James Bible contains the semantic raw materials for "curious workmanship" but never assembles them. The word **"curious"** appears in the archaic sense of "wrought with care and art" in seven instances of **"curious girdle"** (Exodus 28:8, 28:27–28, 29:5, 39:5, 39:20–21), once as **"curious works"** (Exodus 35:32), and once as **"curiously wrought"** (Psalm 139:15). Separately, **"workmanship"** appears seven times (Exodus 31:3, 31:5, 35:31; 2 Kings 16:10; 1 Chronicles 28:21; Ezekiel 28:13; Ephesians 2:10). But "curious" never modifies "workmanship" in any verse. The closest proximity occurs across a verse boundary in Exodus 35:31–32, where "workmanship" ends one verse and "curious works" begins the next.
The single most important KJV verse for understanding the Book of Mormon's usage is **Exodus 35:32**: "And to devise curious works, to work in gold, and in silver, and in brass." This verse links "curious," "works," and "brass" in a single clause about sacred craftsmanship—precisely the semantic constellation the Book of Mormon deploys. The KJV also employs the near-synonym "cunning work" and "cunning workman" more than ten times in Exodus for the same Hebrew roots (חֹשֵׁב / *chosheb* and מַחֲשֶׁבֶת / *machashebeth*, both deriving from חָשַׁב / *chashab*, "to think, devise, plan"). The translators used "curious" and "cunning" essentially as interchangeable English synonyms for divinely inspired artisanship.
No pre-KJV English Bible translation uses the phrase either. The **Tyndale** and **Coverdale** Bibles render the relevant Exodus passages as "broderd worke" (embroidered work). The **Geneva Bible** uses "embroydred garde" where the KJV has "curious girdle." The **Bishops' Bible** uses "the same workmanship" and the **Douay-Rheims** "the very workmanship also"—both employing "workmanship" without "curious." The combination is an extra-biblical English invention.
---
### A chronological catalog of confirmed pre-1830 instances
The phrase first appears in English around the turn of the seventeenth century and clusters overwhelmingly in one genre: **translations of classical texts describing ancient craftsmanship**. The following catalog represents all instances confirmed through digitized corpora, organized chronologically.
**John Stow, *A Survey of London* (1598; revised editions 1603, 1618).** Stow described the fan-vaulted cloisters of St. Stephen's College within the Palace of Westminster as being "of curious workmanship." This is among the earliest confirmed attestations—an antiquarian describing medieval English architecture.
**Meric Casaubon, translation of Marcus Aurelius, *Meditations*, Book IV (1634).** "Those things, that are commonly called fair and good, as those which are commended either for the matter itself, or for curious workmanships." The plural form is notable. Casaubon uses it in a philosophical reflection on the nature of value versus craftsmanship.
**Robert Boyle, *A Discourse of Things Above Reason* (1681).** "When with bold telescopes I survey the old and newly discovered stars and planets; when with excellent microscopes I discern the unimitable subtility of **nature's curious workmanship**; and when, in a word, by the help of anatomical knives, and the light of chymical furnaces, I study the book of nature." This is the sole confirmed instance connecting the phrase to scientific instruments—telescopes and microscopes—placing it within the Royal Society's natural-philosophical vocabulary.
**Matthew Henry, *Commentary on the Whole Bible*, on Exodus 31:1–11 (1706–1714).** "The Israelites, who had been masons and bricklayers in Egypt, were not qualified for curious workmanship." Henry's commentary extends KJV language, combining "curious" and "workmanship" in a way the Bible text itself never does. Given Henry's extraordinary influence on English-language Protestant devotion, this may be one of the most widely encountered instances of the phrase in American homes.
**Isaac Littlebury, translation of Herodotus, *The History*, Book III (1709).** "He questioned them concerning the necklace and bracelets, and when they had explained to him **the curious workmanship of those things**, the King laughed, and told them that chains of a far greater strength were to be found in Ethiopia." Here the phrase describes ornamental metalwork encountered across cultural boundaries—a classic trope of encounter literature.
**William Whiston, translation of Josephus, *Antiquities of the Jews*, Book VIII, 5:2 (1737).** "Now the contexture of **the curious workmanship** of these stones was in three rows, but the fourth row would make one admire its sculptures." This instance is arguably the most significant for Book of Mormon source criticism. Whiston's *Josephus* was, after the Bible, **the most widely owned book in eighteenth- and nineteenth-century American households**. The passage describes Solomon's Temple—the same architectural complex that pervades both Masonic ritual and Book of Mormon temple-building narratives.
**John and William Langhorne, translation of Plutarch's *Lives*, "Paulus Aemilius" (1770).** "The young men that led these victims were girded with **belts of curious workmanship**; and after them came the boys who carried the gold and silver vessels for the sacrifice." The phrase describes ceremonial objects in a Roman triumph.
**Robert Potter, translation of Euripides, *Hippolytus* (1781).** "On the roof, for shelter form'd, too curious workmanship were lost." A philosophical observation about the limits of refinement.
**Gilbert Hunt, *The Late War Between the United States and Great Britain* (1816).** Four confirmed instances, discussed in detail below.
Google Ngram data shows the phrase in continuous use from approximately **1600 to 1850**, with a notable uptick during the **1778–1821 period**—precisely overlapping with Joseph Smith's youth.
---
### Where the phrase does not appear
The absences are as telling as the presences. The exact phrase "curious workmanship" has **not been found** in Shakespeare, Spenser, Milton, Bunyan, Defoe, Goldsmith, Swift, Addison, or any canonical English literary author. It does not appear in the major travel and exploration compilations—Hakluyt's *Principal Navigations*, Purchas's *Pilgrimes*, Carver's *Travels*, Smith's *Generall Historie*—despite extensive searching. It was not confirmed in Ethan Smith's *View of the Hebrews* (1823/1825) or Josiah Priest's *Wonders of Nature and Providence* (1825).
This distribution reveals a clear genre signature. "Curious workmanship" belonged to the register of **learned English prose about ancient artifacts**—translations of classical historians and antiquarian description—rather than to fiction, poetry, or travel narrative. The phrase functioned as a translator's stock rendering for Greek and Latin descriptions of skilled craftwork. Its absence from original English literary composition suggests it was borrowed into English primarily through the act of translation itself.
---
### *The Late War* presents the most striking parallel
Gilbert Hunt's *The Late War Between the United States and Great Britain* (1816), a pseudo-biblical history of the War of 1812 written in KJV-style English for use in New York schools, contains the exact phrase **four times**—more than any other confirmed pre-1830 text:
- **Chapter 6, verse 12** (a ceremonial sword): "the people of Columbia were pleased with the noble conduct of Jones, and for his valiant acts they gave him **a sword of curious workmanship**."
- **Chapter 8, verse 13** (a surrendered sword): "Carden came on board the ship of Columbia, he bowed his head, and offered to put **his sword, of curious workmanship**, into the hands of Decatur."
- **Chapter 19, verse 13** (American weapons): "And **their weapons of war were of curious workmanship**, and they sent forth balls of lead."
- **Regarding steamboats**: "These steam boats were cunningly contrived, and **had abundance of curious workmanship**."
The weapons-of-war passage is directly parallel to **Ether 10:27**: "And they did make all manner of **weapons of war**. And they did work all manner of work of exceedingly **curious workmanship**." Both texts pair "weapons of war" with "curious workmanship" in pseudo-biblical register.
But the most remarkable parallel involves a different passage. *The Late War* Chapter 50 describes a torpedo as "made partly of **brass** and partly of iron, and were cunningly contrived with **curious works**, like unto a **clock**; and as it were a large **ball**." Compare 1 Nephi 16:10: "a round **ball** of **curious workmanship**; and it was of fine **brass**. And within the ball were two spindles." Both descriptions share four attributes: an object that is **(1) brass, (2) ball-shaped, (3) associated with "curious" craft language, and (4) mechanical** (clock-like with pointers/spindles). This cluster of co-occurring descriptors is unique among all identified pre-1830 texts.
*The Late War* was published in New York and marketed as a school textbook. Samuel Mitchill, who endorsed the book, is the same scholar Martin Harris reportedly visited regarding the Book of Mormon characters. Chris and Duane Johnson's 2013 computational analysis, using four-word phrase matching against over 100,000 pre-1830 texts, identified *The Late War* as having more rare n-gram matches with the Book of Mormon than **99.999%** of the comparison corpus. Ryan Thomas, an independent scholar, assessed the evidence as indicating "indirect rather than direct" literary dependence—elements that "percolated" in memory rather than were copied from an open book.
---
### The Masonic claim is overstated but structurally suggestive
The claim advanced by some critics that "curious workmanship" is "a uniquely Masonic phrase" with "no meaning outside of" Freemasonry is **demonstrably false**. The phrase's extensive attestation in non-Masonic contexts—classical translations, antiquarian prose, biblical commentary—predates any documented Masonic usage. Despite extensive searching, the exact phrase could not be confirmed in William Preston's *Illustrations of Masonry*, Thomas Smith Webb's *Freemason's Monitor*, or William Morgan's 1826 Masonic exposé.
However, the **structural parallel** between Masonic temple imagery and the Liahona merits attention. Walter Franklin Pierce noted in a 1917 article in the *American Journal of Psychology* that Morgan's exposé describes Solomon's Temple pillars topped with "a large **globe or ball**" of "**brass**," which is "**hollow**" and contains two sets of maps inside—one celestial, one terrestrial. The Liahona is described as a round **ball** of fine **brass** containing **two spindles**. The Masonic connection may operate through shared imagery (brass + ball + hollow + paired internal elements) rather than through the specific phrase "curious workmanship."
---
### Five instances in the Book of Mormon reveal a consistent pattern
The Book of Mormon uses "curious workmanship" in five passages (including prefatory material), always describing objects of extraordinary or divinely guided craft:
- **1 Nephi 16:10**: The Liahona—"a round ball of curious workmanship; and it was of fine brass"
- **1 Nephi 18:1**: Nephi's ship—"we did work timbers of curious workmanship"
- **Alma 37:39**: The Liahona retrospectively—"there cannot any man work after the manner of so curious a workmanship"
- **Ether 10:27**: Jaredite civilization—"they did work all manner of work of exceedingly curious workmanship"
- **Testimony of the Eight Witnesses**: The gold plates—"all of which has the appearance of ancient work, and of curious workmanship"
Related terms extend the pattern: **"curious workmen"** (Helaman 6:11, describing ore refiners), **"exceedingly curious man"** (Alma 63:5, describing shipbuilder Hagoth), and **"workmanship thereof was exceedingly fine"** (1 Nephi 18:4 and 2 Nephi 5:16). The phrase is reserved exclusively for objects of advanced, often divinely inspired, craftsmanship—navigational instruments, ships, temples, weapons, and sacred records. This usage is consistent with both the KJV register of Exodus (sacred artisanship) and the classical-translation tradition (ancient craftsmanship admired across cultural distance).
---
### No scholar has systematically traced this phrase's genealogy
The scholarly literature on this specific phrase is fragmentary. The **Johnsons' computational study** (2013–2014) identified *The Late War* parallels but did not trace the phrase's longer history. **Ryan Thomas** (2013) treated the parallels as evidence of indirect literary dependence without conducting phrase-level philology. **Rick Grunder's** *Mormon Parallels* (2008) catalogued *Late War* parallels bibliographically, noting "curious workmanship" among shared vocabulary. **Edgar C. Snow Jr.** published a brief literary essay titled "Of Curious Workmanship" in *Sunstone* (December 1994) discussing the multiple meanings of "curious," but this was a devotional reflection, not a philological study. **Dan Vogel, Grant Palmer, Mark Thomas, David Wright, Brent Metcalfe**, and other prominent critical scholars have not published dedicated analyses of this specific phrase, though their broader theses about nineteenth-century environmental influence encompass it.
On the apologetic side (flagged per instructions), an LDS blog catalogued the phrase's appearances in classical translations to argue it was not anachronistic—ironically producing what may be the most comprehensive pre-existing inventory of the phrase's occurrences. Stanford Carmack's Early Modern English studies, published exclusively in apologetic venues, did not single out "curious workmanship" as a specifically archaic feature. **This phrase-level genealogy represents a genuine gap in both critical and apologetic scholarship.**
---
### Conclusion: a common phrase with an uncommon concentration
"Curious workmanship" was **not rare** in pre-1830 English. It was a standard collocation in educated prose, attested from at least 1598, peaking in frequency during the late eighteenth and early nineteenth centuries. But it was genre-specific: it belonged to the world of **classical translation, biblical commentary, and antiquarian description**—not to fiction, poetry, or everyday speech. Noah Webster's 1828 *American Dictionary* defined "curious" as "wrought with care and art; elegant; neat; finished" and cited KJV Exodus as illustration, confirming the sense was still current.
The most plausible transmission pathway runs through **three overlapping channels**. First, the **KJV itself** supplied the component vocabulary—"curious works," "curious girdle," "workmanship"—without ever combining them into "curious workmanship." Second, **Whiston's translation of Josephus** (1737), describing Solomon's Temple with the exact phrase, was ubiquitous in American households and directly relevant to the Book of Mormon's temple-building narratives. Third, **Matthew Henry's Commentary** (1706–1714), using the exact phrase in its gloss on Exodus 31, was among the most widely consulted devotional resources in English-speaking Protestantism. These three sources—the KJV as generative matrix, Josephus as prestige exemplar, and Henry as devotional bridge—would have saturated the linguistic environment of any biblically literate early-nineteenth-century American.
*The Late War* occupies a different position. Its four instances of "curious workmanship" and especially its torpedo passage (brass + ball + curious works + clock) represent not a general linguistic influence but a **specific textual parallel** with uncommon density of co-occurring features. Whether this reflects direct borrowing, shared pseudo-biblical idiom, or convergent drawing on KJV vocabulary remains the central interpretive question—one that the absence of a systematic philological study has left unresolved. What this genealogy establishes is that the phrase was available from multiple directions simultaneously: from the Bible's semantic neighborhood, from the most popular books in American homes, from a New York school textbook written in scriptural English, and from the general educated vocabulary of the period. The Book of Mormon's distinctive contribution was not the phrase itself but the **objects it was applied to**—a brass navigational ball, a divinely designed ship, sacred metal plates—concentrating the language of ancient craftsmanship onto artifacts that served the text's narrative theology.
---
## Three Pre-1830 Source Texts Examined
The most significant finding from this research is a passage in Josephus that constitutes the strongest pre-1830 parallel to the Book of Mormon's Liahona yet identified in the literature. **In Antiquities 3.218, Josephus reports that the high priest's breastplate and sardonyx stone "left off shining two hundred years before I composed this book, God having been displeased at the transgressions of his laws."** This describes a sacred instrument of divine guidance — explicitly called "the Oracle" — made of precious materials, that provided revelatory direction and ceased to function due to moral unworthiness. That three-part pattern mirrors the Liahona's core mechanics precisely. The other two "curious workmanship" texts (Josephus Book VIII and Plutarch's Paulus Aemilius) prove far less significant as parallels, though the wider literary context of all three illuminates the phrase's circulation in exactly the period relevant to Book of Mormon composition.
---
### Josephus Book VIII, Chapter 5 describes palace stonework, not sacred objects
The "curious workmanship" passage in Antiquities VIII.5.2 describes **carved building stones in the walls of Solomon's royal palace complex**, not the temple, not the breastplate, and not the Urim and Thummim. The full passage reads: "Now the contexture of the curious workmanship of these stones was in three rows, but the fourth row would make one admire its sculptures, whereby were represented trees, and all sorts of plants; with the shades that arose from their branches, and leaves that hung down from them." Josephus specifies these were large architectural stones ("stones of ten cubits"), sawed from quarries, arranged as wall decoration. The fourth row featured bas-relief plant sculptures so finely carved "you would think they were in motion." This corresponds to **1 Kings 7:9-12**, Solomon's palace courtyard described as "three rows of hewed stones and a row of cedar beams."
While the Book VIII passage itself yields no Liahona parallel, the surrounding chapters contain a remarkable density of brass objects in Solomon's Temple. Josephus describes the **brass pillars Jachin and Boaz** (hollow, four-fingers-thick brass, eighteen cubits high); the **Brazen Sea**, a brass hemisphere resting on "a short pillar that had ten spirals round it" supported by twelve oxen that "looked to the four winds of heaven, three to each wind"; **ten brazen wheeled bases** with lavers — "large round brass vessels" on wheeled stands with spiral pillars, engravings, and naves "a foot and a half in diameter"; and a **brazen altar** twenty cubits long. The cumulative picture is of a sacred precinct filled with round brass objects oriented toward cardinal directions, mounted on wheels and spirals — imagery that resonates with the Liahona's description as a round brass ball with spindles, though no single object combines all features into a portable divine compass.
The phrase "curious workmanship" in Whiston's translation here is standard **18th-century English for skilled craftsmanship**, equivalent to the KJV's use of "curious" in Exodus 28:8 (the "curious girdle" of the ephod). It does not carry any specialized theological weight in this architectural context.
---
### Plutarch's triumph passage lacks the phrase in standard translations
The attribution of "belts of curious workmanship" to the Langhorne translation of Plutarch's Life of Paulus Aemilius requires correction. The phrase appears in **Rosalie Kaufman's *Our Young Folks' Plutarch*** (c. 1900), a children's adaptation, not in any standard scholarly translation. Kaufman paraphrased the description of young sacrificial attendants wearing ornamental tunics into "a belt of curious workmanship." The Dryden/Clough translation renders the same passage as "young men wearing frocks with ornamented borders," while the Perrin/Loeb describes "tunics with ornamental borders." The actual Langhorne translation (1770) is not freely available in searchable digital form — only as scanned page images on the Internet Archive — but the underlying Greek describes ceremonial garments with decorative purple borders, standard sacrificial vestments for the triumph over Perseus of Macedon in 167 BC.
That said, the Life of Paulus Aemilius is saturated with **divine guidance and augural themes** that intersect broadly with the Liahona concept. Aemilius served as an **augur** — one of Rome's priestly interpreters of divine signs — and Plutarch emphasizes he "so carefully studied the ancient customs of his country" that he elevated the augural priesthood from honorary title to high art. Three specific episodes stand out. First, the **Perseus-the-dog omen**: Aemilius found his daughter weeping over a pet dog named Perseus that had died, and instantly declared "Good fortune, my daughter; I embrace the omen" — interpreting a spontaneous coincidence as divine prophecy of his victory over King Perseus. Second, the **sacrificial divination before Pydna**: Aemilius sacrificed twenty oxen to Hercules without favorable signs, but "at the one-and-twentieth, the signs promised victory to defenders" — a vivid scene of conditional divine communication through physical objects (entrails). Third, **lightning completed his sacrifice** at Amphipolis, interpreted as the gods themselves finishing what the priest had begun.
The conditional-operation parallel, while indirect, appears in Plutarch's remark about Perseus: Hercules "is not wont to regard the faint offerings of cowards, nor to fulfil unsanctioned vows." The gods answer the worthy (Aemilius) and ignore the unworthy (Perseus). **No single sacred object in this Life functions as a Liahona-type device**, but the theology of conditional divine response permeates the narrative.
---
### Matthew Henry's commentary links divine craftsmanship to revelatory function
Henry uses "curious workmanship" in his Concise Commentary on Exodus 31:1-11, noting that "the Israelites, who had been masons and bricklayers in Egypt, were not qualified for curious workmanship; but the Spirit who gave the apostles utterance in divers tongues, miraculously gave Bezaleel and Aholiab the skill that was wanting." His Complete Commentary uses the related phrase "curious arts." The key theological move Henry makes is connecting **divinely bestowed craft skill** (God filling Bezaleel with "the Spirit of God") to the sacred function of the objects created — the tabernacle, the ark, and the priestly garments. This is a direct link between craftsmanship and spiritual power.
Henry's commentary on the **Urim and Thummim** (Exodus 28:30) is more directly relevant to Liahona parallels. He defines them as instruments through which the high priest was "endued with a power of knowing and making known the mind of God in all difficult doubtful cases, relating either to the civil or ecclesiastical state of the nation." He calls the Urim and Thummim God's **"cabinet-council"** for the high priest under Israel's theocracy. On Numbers 27:21, Henry describes their directional function in language strikingly reminiscent of the Liahona: "At the word of the priest, according to the judgment of urim, Joshua and all Israel must **go out and come in**" — physical movement governed by a sacred revelatory object. He also notes their conditional aspect: even Joshua, though "full of the Spirit," was required to consult the oracle rather than lean on his own understanding.
Critically, Henry records that the Urim and Thummim "was lost in the captivity, and never regained after" — a cessation of function parallel to the Liahona, though Henry attributes it to historical loss rather than divine displeasure. His commentary was **the most widely used Protestant biblical commentary in the English-speaking world** during the early 19th century. While the first American edition appeared in 1828 (Towar & Hogan, Philadelphia), British editions circulated through ministerial libraries and imported booksellers for decades prior, amplified by George Whitefield's and John Wesley's extensive use of Henry in their preaching across the American colonies.
---
### The Josephus breastplate passage is the strongest Liahona parallel
The critical passage is Antiquities III.8.9 (Ant. 3.214-218) in Whiston's translation. Josephus explains that Moses designed the priestly garments so that God could signal his presence or absence to "not the Hebrews only, but to those foreigners also who were there." Three specific phenomena are described:
**The sardonyx shoulder-stone signaling God's presence:** The stone on the high priest's right shoulder shone with "bright rays darting out thence, and being seen even by those that were most remote." Josephus emphasizes this "splendor yet was not before natural to the stone" — the shining was supernatural, not an inherent property of the gem.
**The twelve breastplate stones foretelling military victory:** God "declared beforehand, by those twelve stones which the high priest bare on his breast... when they should be victorious in battle," producing "so great a splendor" that "all the people were sensible of God's being present for their assistance." Josephus notes this is why the Greeks called the breastplate **"the Oracle"** (translating the Hebrew *Essen/Hoshen*).
**The cessation passage (Ant. 3.218):** "Now this breastplate, and this sardonyx, **left off shining two hundred years before I composed this book, God having been displeased at the transgressions of his laws.**" Since Josephus wrote the Antiquities around AD 93-94, this places the cessation circa 107-106 BC, during the Hasmonean period.
The parallel structure with the Liahona is systematic across all three defining features:
| Feature | Josephus's breastplate/sardonyx | Book of Mormon's Liahona |
|---|---|---|
| **Materials and craft** | Gold, precious stones of "immense value," chains "finely wrought," embroidered ephod | "Fine brass," "curious workmanship" that "no man could work after the manner of so curious a workmanship" |
| **Revelatory function** | Explicitly called "the Oracle"; declared victory beforehand; signaled God's presence via supernatural radiance visible at great distance | One spindle "pointed the way whither we should go"; writing appeared "giving understanding concerning the ways of the Lord" |
| **Conditional operation** | "Left off shining... God having been displeased at the transgressions of his laws" | "Those marvelous works ceased" when people "were slothful, and forgot to exercise their faith and diligence" (Alma 37:41); "the compass... did cease to work" during rebellion (1 Nephi 18:12) |
Both instruments share a **theological architecture of conditional divine communication through physical objects**. The mechanism differs (supernatural luminescence vs. mechanical pointing), but the deeper logic is identical: God provides a tangible, visible instrument; the instrument's function is contingent on the people's righteousness; and when the people transgress, the instrument ceases operating — explicitly because of God's displeasure. One scholarly source at the intersection of LDS studies has noted that "the Urim and Thummim, in fact, bears more resemblance to the Liahona than the interpreters," highlighting this conceptual link. Josephus's description also resonates with the broader Book of Mormon theme of sacred objects that work "according to the faith of the children of men" (Alma 37:40).
---
### Whiston's Josephus was ubiquitous in the early American world
The availability question is settled decisively. Whiston's 1737 translation of Josephus was, by scholarly consensus, **"second in popularity only to the Bible itself"** in the English-speaking world through the 18th and 19th centuries. Heinz Schreckenberg described it as having become "a kind of household book alongside the Bible" in the Anglo-American world. Among Puritans, **Josephus was the only book besides the Bible permitted for Sunday reading**. Thomas Jefferson owned Whiston's first edition. American editions appeared as early as 1773-1775 (L'Estrange translation, Philadelphia/New York), with William Durell's 1792 folio edition boasting a subscriber list "that reads like a Who's Who." By the 1820s, the **Manchester Rental Library** — within five miles of the Smith family farm — held Whiston's six-volume 1806 edition, and copies were regularly advertised in Palmyra bookshops.
Was it at **Dartmouth** during Spalding's attendance (1782-1785)? No surviving catalog transcription confirms the specific title, but the circumstantial case is overwhelming. Josephus was among the initial forty volumes donated to found Yale's Collegiate School in 1701; Dartmouth's founder Eleazar Wheelock was a Yale graduate; Dartmouth's library was described as "strong in theology and classics"; and Josephus was considered essential reading for exactly those subjects. **Spalding's familiarity with Josephus can be considered virtually certain** given his classical education, ministerial career, and role running Cherry Valley Academy, "the first classical school west of Albany."
For **Sidney Rigdon** in Pittsburgh (1822-1826), Patterson & Lambdin operated a bookstore, printing house, and book bindery — the kind of business that would have stocked Josephus as a perennial bestseller and likely Plutarch's Lives as a standard classical text. Multiple witnesses claimed Rigdon was "frequently in" Lambdin's shop, though Rigdon denied this and Mrs. Lambdin also denied knowledge of the association. Regardless of the disputed personal connection, any literate person in early 19th-century Pittsburgh had easy access to Josephus through bookshops, lending libraries, and ministerial collections.
The Google Ngram data confirms that **"curious workmanship" peaked between approximately 1778 and 1821**, overlapping precisely with Spalding's active period (1782-1816) and extending to the Book of Mormon's composition era. The phrase's prevalence in this window reflects its presence in Whiston's Josephus, the KJV Bible's "curious girdle" language, Masonic literature about Temple construction, and the broader 18th-century English convention of using "curious" to mean "skillfully wrought."
---
### Conclusion
The three "curious workmanship" texts examined here occupy different positions on a spectrum of relevance to the Liahona. The Josephus Book VIII passage describes palace stonework and yields no direct parallel. The Plutarch passage — likely a later paraphrase rather than Langhorne's actual translation — describes ceremonial garments within a narrative rich in augural theology but lacking any specific divine-guidance instrument. Matthew Henry's commentary connects divine craftsmanship to revelatory function and describes the Urim and Thummim as a directional oracle, but treats its cessation as historical loss rather than divine punishment.
The breakthrough finding is **Josephus's breastplate account in Antiquities III.8.9**. This passage, widely available through the most popular non-biblical book in early America, describes a sacred object that matches the Liahona's theological architecture on three independent dimensions: precious craftsmanship, revelatory guidance, and conditional operation that ceases upon transgression. The cessation language — "left off shining... God having been displeased" — is functionally identical to the Liahona's ceasing to work "according to their faith." Whether this parallel reflects direct literary influence, shared ancient theological patterns, or coincidence is a question these findings cannot resolve, but the parallel itself is more precise and comprehensive than has been generally recognized in the scholarly literature on Book of Mormon source criticism.
---
# PART VI: CARVER'S TRAVELS AND THE TRANSMISSION CHAIN
## Carver's Travels and the Liahona: A Missing Link
Jonathan Carver's *Travels Through the Interior Parts of North America* (1778) served as the single most important English-language conduit through which French colonial ethnography about Indigenous spirituality reached early American readers — yet the book **does not contain the specific compass-as-manitou passage** from Lahontan that is most relevant to the Liahona. This finding reshapes the proposed transmission chain. Carver's enormous popularity (over 30 editions before 1830, including 8 American printings) guaranteed that his descriptions of the Great Spirit, ancient fortifications, hieroglyphics, and spirit-animated objects saturated the intellectual environment of Joseph Smith's era. But the specific conceptual bridge between Lahontan's "machines moved by Spirits" and the Book of Mormon's faith-conditional brass compass appears to require a different pathway than the neat Lahontan → Carver → View of the Hebrews → Book of Mormon chain.
### Carver never reproduced Lahontan's compass passage
The most critical finding of this research is negative. **Lahontan's famous passage** — from a letter dated July 4, 1695, in Volume II of *New Voyages to North America* (1703) — states:
> "The Savages believe that a Watch, a Compass, and a thousand other Machines are moved by Spirits; for your ignorant and clownish People form extravagant Ideas of every thing that surpasses their Imagination."
Lahontan reinforced this with a second passage about his graphometer, where Indigenous peoples "took my Graphometer for somewhat Divine, being unable to guess how we could know the distance of Places without measuring them by Cords or Rods, without there were some Supernatural Assistance." These two passages — one naming the compass explicitly as spirit-animated, the other describing a European measuring instrument as "Divine" — are the closest textual antecedents in pre-1830 travel literature to the Liahona concept.
Carver borrowed extensively from Lahontan in other domains: funeral orations (Carver pp. 399–400, from Lahontan II, pp. 51–52), war songs, the Chippeway vocabulary, and ethnographic descriptions of Indigenous customs. E.G. Bourne's landmark 1906 article in the *American Historical Review* demonstrated that Carver's second part — "Of the Origin, Manners, Customs, Religion, and Language of the Indians" (pp. 181–441) — was "a mere patchwork of selections from the journals of Hennepin, Lahontan, Charlevoix and Adair." Yet despite this systematic borrowing, **Carver did not reproduce, paraphrase, or echo Lahontan's compass/watch/spirits passage**. No mention of compasses or magnetic needles as objects perceived by Indigenous peoples through a spiritual lens appears anywhere in Carver's text.
What Carver does contain is a conceptually adjacent but distinct passage about European technology as supernatural. Describing peoples near the Shining Mountains who fled the Spanish conquest, he writes that their ancestors had been "infested ever since with monsters vomiting fire, and hurling about thunder and lightning; from whose bowels issued men, who, with **unseen instruments, or by the power of magick**, killed the harmless Indians at an astonishing distance" (p. 118–120). This frames firearms and ships — not navigational instruments — through an Indigenous spiritual lens.
### Manitous as faith-conditional directional objects
While Carver lacks the compass passage, he offers a different parallel to the Liahona that has not been noted in scholarship. In Chapter IX on warfare, Carver describes warriors placing "great confidence in their **Manitous, or household gods**, which they always carry with them; and being persuaded that they take upon them the office of centinels, they sleep very securely under their protection" (pp. 308–309). Bourne traced this to Charlevoix's *Journal* (I, 358), where the original specifies that warriors placed these manitous on a lattice-work **facing the direction of their route** and invoked them for an hour before sleeping.
This passage combines several Liahona-relevant elements simultaneously: portable sacred objects carried during travel, directional orientation toward the route of a journey, spiritual agency attributed to physical instruments, and conditional protection dependent on proper invocation. The manitous function as direction-indicating, spiritually animated objects that guide and protect travelers — a closer structural parallel to the Liahona than is typically recognized, even though they are not described as compasses or as being made of brass.
The **conditional element** — spirits helping the worthy and abandoning the unworthy — appears elsewhere in Carver. After a violent thunderstorm, the Indians informed Carver "that it was a proof of the anger of the **evil spirits**, whom they were apprehensive that they had highly offended" (p. 85). Carver's religion chapter (pp. 380–388) describes beliefs in a supreme Great Spirit alongside subordinate good and evil spirits, with ideas of a "future State" where the worthy are rewarded and the unworthy punished. This theological framework — where divine favor operates conditionally on righteousness — pervades Carver's ethnographic descriptions.
### Ancient fortifications and hieroglyphics fed the mound-builder myth
Carver's most consequential contribution to the Book of Mormon's intellectual environment lies not in the compass question but in his pioneering descriptions of ancient earthworks and mysterious inscriptions. Near Lake Pepin, he described discovering ruins of an ancient fortification:
> "I could plainly discern that it had once been **a breastwork of about four feet in height, extending the best part of a mile, and sufficiently capacious to cover five thousand men. Its form was somewhat circular, and its flanks reached to the River. Though much defaced by time, every angle was distinguishable and appeared as regular, and fashioned with as much military skill, as if planned by Vauban himself.**" (pp. 57–58)
He then posed the question that would define American antiquarian speculation for the next half century: "How a work of this kind could exist in a country that has hitherto been the seat of war to untutored Indians alone, whose whole stock of military knowledge has only, till within two centuries, amounted to drawing the bow… I know not." This is recognized as **the first published account calling attention to ancient earthwork monuments in the Mississippi Valley**, establishing the template for the mound-builder myth — the idea that a sophisticated civilization predating contemporary Indigenous peoples had been destroyed.
At the cave called **Wakon-teebe** ("Dwelling of the Great Spirit") near the Falls of St. Anthony, Carver found "many Indian hieroglyphics, which appeared very ancient, for time had nearly covered them with moss, so that it was with difficulty I could trace them. They were cut in a rude manner upon the inside of the walls" (pp. 63–65). His Chapter XVII, "Of their Language, Hieroglyphicks, &c." (pp. 414–419), includes a "Descriptive Specimen of their Hieroglyphicks." The combination of ancient fortifications built by a vanished civilization, mysterious ancient writing systems, and a supreme "Great Spirit" theology closely mirrors Book of Mormon themes.
Dan Vogel's *Indian Origins and the Book of Mormon* (1986, Signature Books) explicitly lists Carver in its bibliography of pre-1830 sources, noting the book "contains a lengthy discussion of the various theories on Indian origins (115–253) and a description of ancient fortifications (35–36)." Vogel's Chapter 4, "Indians and Mound Builders," traces how pre-1830 descriptions of fortifications like those described by Carver parallel the fortifications in the Book of Mormon.
### Carver's Great Spirit theology and the Lost Tribes argument
Carver devoted **35 pages** (Chapter I, pp. 181–216) to theories of Indian origins, presenting them through a review of prior authorities. His most extensive treatment is of James Adair's Lost Tribes of Israel theory (pp. 202–208), which he summarizes in detail: Adair "without hesitation pronounces that the American Aborigines are descended from the Israelites, either whilst they were a maritime power, or soon after their general captivity." Carver presents Adair's arguments about tribal divisions, worship of "**Yohewah**" (Jehovah), theocratic government, prophets and high priests, festivals, and laws of uncleanliness.
Carver himself favored a Tartarian/Chinese migration theory over the Israelite hypothesis, noting skeptically that "if the Indians are really derived from the Hebrews… the principal [ceremony], that of circumcision, would never have been laid aside." But his detailed summary of Adair's arguments ensured the Lost Tribes theory reached a far wider audience than Adair's own 1775 book. Carver's Great Spirit theology is presented sympathetically throughout. The Winnebago prince's prayer at the Falls of St. Anthony concludes with "fervent petitions that the **Great Spirit** would constantly afford us his protection on our travels, giving us a bright sun, a blue sky, and clear untroubled waters" (pp. 66–69), prompting Carver to equate the Great Spirit with "the universal Parent of mankind."
The Killistinoe priest's prophecy (pp. 123–131) is the most vivid passage of prophetic guidance in the book. After being bound in elk skin and placed in an enclosure, the priest announced that "the **Great Spirit** has deigned to hold a Talk with his servant at my earnest request… **to-morrow, soon after the sun has reached his highest point in the heavens, a canoe will arrive**, and the people in that will inform us when the traders will come." The prediction was fulfilled exactly the next day at noon. Carver attests to witnessing this personally as "a cool and dispassionate observer."
### Eight American editions made Carver vastly more accessible than Lahontan
The publication data decisively establishes Carver's importance as a transmission vector. **Eight American editions** appeared between 1784 and 1813, printed in Philadelphia (4 editions: 1784, 1789, 1792, 1796), Portsmouth, NH (1794), Boston (1797, 1802), and Walpole, NH (1813). By contrast, **Lahontan had zero American editions** before 1830 — the first readily available American reprint was Thwaites' 1905 edition. The preface to Carver's third edition (1781) boasts that "few works have had a more rapid sale than the following; two large editions having been disposed of in two years." Bourne called it the most instantly and widely famous book of American authorship in the eighteenth century.
George Washington owned a copy. The New York Society Library held the work in its eighteenth-century collection. The 1813 Walpole, NH edition was printed by **Isaiah Thomas & Co.** — the same Isaiah Thomas who donated 470 books to Dartmouth College's library in 1819, making it highly probable Solomon Spalding (Dartmouth Class of 1785) would have encountered the text. Editions were printed in the same New England and mid-Atlantic regions where Joseph Smith's family lived before and during the period of the Book of Mormon's composition. Parts of Carver's Travels were "regularly reprinted in magazines and newspapers" throughout the early Republic period.
The comparison with Lahontan is stark. Americans reading Carver were indirectly absorbing Lahontan's content — funeral orations, war customs, religious descriptions — through Carver's unattributed borrowings. Carver functioned as what scholars have called a "borrowed and generalized collage" (Fulford) of earlier French accounts repackaged for English-speaking audiences. For any idea in Lahontan to reach Joseph Smith's western New York environment, Carver was the most plausible English-language vehicle — with the critical exception of the compass/spirits passage, which Carver omitted.
### The transmission chain needs revision
The proposed chain of **Lahontan (1703) → Carver (1778) → View of the Hebrews (1823) → Book of Mormon (1830)** is not established in existing scholarship as a recognized pathway. The Carver → View of the Hebrews link is particularly weak: Ethan Smith's documented sources are James Adair's *History of the American Indians* (1775) and Elias Boudinot's *Star in the West* (1816), not Carver. No scholar has documented specific textual borrowings from Carver in View of the Hebrews.
More importantly, the Liahona-Lahontan connection itself — the parallel between "machines moved by Spirits" and a faith-conditional brass compass — **has never been argued in published Book of Mormon criticism**. Searches across Dan Vogel's work, B.H. Roberts' *Studies of the Book of Mormon*, the CES Letter, MormonThink, and LDS apologetic literature (FAIR, Interpreter Foundation, BYU Religious Studies Center) returned no results connecting Lahontan's compass passage to the Liahona. This appears to be a genuinely novel observation.
The more productive research direction may be to recognize that the Liahona concept draws from a diffuse cultural matrix rather than a single textual chain. Carver's contributions to that matrix include portable manitous with directional-sentinel functions, conditional divine favor theology, the Great Spirit as a supreme being communicating through prophetic instruments, and the general framework of Indigenous spirituality that made faith-conditional sacred objects conceptually available to early nineteenth-century writers. The specific compass-as-spirit-animated idea, however, appears to have entered popular culture through channels other than Carver — possibly through oral tradition, other French accounts, or the general lore of European-Indigenous encounter that circulated in frontier communities.
### Conclusion
Carver's *Travels* is indispensable to understanding the intellectual environment of the Book of Mormon, but not for the reasons the proposed transmission chain suggests. Its contributions are threefold. First, it was the **primary English-language vehicle** through which French colonial ethnography about Indigenous spirituality reached mass American readership, even though it selectively omitted Lahontan's most Liahona-relevant passage. Second, it **pioneered the mound-builder narrative** — ancient fortifications, mysterious hieroglyphics, a vanished civilization — that became the dominant framework for American antiquarian speculation by the 1820s. Third, its presentation of Great Spirit theology, prophetic divination, and conditionally-operative spiritual guidance created a widely familiar template for faith-dependent sacred instruments. The phrase "curious workmanship" does not appear in Carver, nor does any brass compass or navigational instrument described as spiritually animated. The strongest Liahona parallel in Carver is the manitou passage — portable sacred objects oriented toward the direction of travel that actively protect their faithful carriers — a parallel that, like the broader Lahontan connection, appears to be entirely unexplored in the existing scholarly literature.
---
# PART VII: THE AHONE HYPOTHESIS (RIGOROUSLY DEBUNKED)
## Pronouncing Ahone: Linguistic Reconstruction
**The best available reconstruction of the Powhatan deity name "Ahone" yields a three-syllable pronunciation with stress on the middle syllable — approximately uh-HOH-neh** (reconstructed IPA: /ʌˈhoːnɛ/ or /ʌˈhʊnɛ/). This estimate rests on Frank Siebert's 1975 phonological reconstruction of Powhatan and the language's documented stress rules, but no definitive IPA transcription has ever been published for this specific word. The name is attested in exactly one colonial source — William Strachey's *Historie of Travaile into Virginia Britannia* (c. 1612) — and **no variant spellings like "Ahonee" or "Ahoni" appear in any primary colonial document**. The popular claim that multiple early spellings exist is unfounded. For Book of Mormon source criticism, a critical barrier exists: Strachey's text was not published until 1849, nineteen years after the Book of Mormon, and no published scholar has ever proposed a connection between "Ahone" and "Liahona."
### What reconstructed Powhatan phonology tells us about the vowels and stress
The foundation for any pronunciation estimate is Frank T. Siebert Jr.'s landmark 1975 study, "Resurrecting Virginia Algonquian from the Dead: The Reconstituted and Historical Phonology of Powhatan," published in *Studies in Southeastern Indian Languages* (University of Georgia Press, pp. 285–453). This **168-page reconstruction** used Strachey's and Smith's word lists alongside comparative evidence from other Algonquian languages. Siebert reconstructed Powhatan's full consonant inventory — p, t, č [tʃ], k, m, n, s, h, w, y [j], r [ɾ] — and its vowel system: short a [ʌ], e [ɛ], i [i], o [ʊ]; long ā [ʌː], ē [eː], ī [iː], ō [oː].
Applying this system to Strachey's spelling ⟨Ahone⟩ yields a segmental analysis: initial ⟨A⟩ = /a/ [ʌ], ⟨h⟩ = /h/, ⟨o⟩ = either /o/ [ʊ] or /oː/ [oː], ⟨n⟩ = /n/, and final ⟨e⟩ = /e/ [ɛ]. Powhatan stress operates through **syncope of "weak" vowels** (/a/ and /e/). When the first syllable contains a weak vowel, even-numbered syllables carry the stress. In the syllabification /a.ho.ne/, the first syllable is weak (/a/), so **syllable two (/ho/) receives primary stress** and syllable three (/ne/) is light. This produces the pattern a-**HO**-ne. The only available pronunciation guide, from the Native Languages of the Americas website (native-languages.org), concurs: "considering the spellings Strachey used in recording other Indian words, **ah-hone** is a good guess." Whether Siebert himself specifically analyzed "Ahone" in his 263-item etymological vocabulary cannot be confirmed from accessible sources, as the full paper is not digitized in searchable form.
Blair Rudes, who reconstructed Powhatan dialogue for Terrence Malick's film *The New World* (2005), used Siebert's work as a starting point and supplemented it with comparative data from Passamaquoddy, Munsee Delaware, and the Massachusett Bible translation. Rudes noted that Strachey "did not always hear correctly what was said" and that "some sounds of Virginia Algonquian could not be spelled accurately" in English orthography. This caution applies directly to "Ahone" — the final ⟨e⟩ could represent a reduced vowel, a full /e/, or even a spelling convention rather than a distinct syllable, though the three-syllable reading is most consistent with Algonquian word structure.
### Strachey stands alone as the source for this name
A finding that cannot be overstated: **William Strachey is the sole colonial writer to record the name "Ahone."** His *Historie of Travaile into Virginia Britannia* (composed c. 1611–1612, existing only in manuscript until the Hakluyt Society published it in 1849) contains two references. The appended "Dictionarie of the Indian Language" lists "Ahone, God" as its very first entry under the letter A. In the narrative text of Chapter VII ("Of the religion amongst the inhabitants"), Strachey writes that the Powhatan priests describe a supreme deity "who governes all the world, and makes the sun to shine, creatyng the moone and starrs his companyons," whom "they calling Ahone." This deity is "the good and peaceable god" who "requires no such dutyes, nor needes be sacrificed unto, for he intendeth all good unto them." The contrasting figure, **Okeus**, functions as a punitive magistrate "looking into all men's accions, and examining the same according to the severe scale of justice."
Captain John Smith's *A Map of Virginia* (1612) and *The Generall Historie of Virginia* (1624) describe Powhatan religion but mention only **"Oke"** and **"Okee"** — never Ahone. Andrew Lang emphasized this point in *The Making of Religion* (1898) and *Myth, Ritual, and Religion* (rev. 1901): E.B. Tylor and Daniel Brinton both missed Ahone entirely because they relied on Smith rather than Strachey. Samuel Purchas, who personally interviewed the Powhatan priest Tomocomo (Uttamatomakkin) in London around 1616, focused on Okeus and **does not clearly name Ahone**. Robert Beverley's *The History and Present State of Virginia* (1705) discusses the idol names "Okee, Quioccos, Kiwasa" but **makes no reference to a benevolent supreme creator deity by name**. Alexander Whitaker's *Good Newes from Virginia* (1613) also omits Ahone.
### The historical spelling record is far thinner than often claimed
The range of supposed historical spellings — "Ahone, Ahonee, Ahoni" — widely repeated online is **largely a modern fabrication**. Here is what the actual documentary record shows:
The only spelling attested in a primary colonial source is **"Ahone"** from Strachey. The alternative name **"Rawottonemd"** appears in later scholarly literature as a ceremonial or alternative designation, though its exact provenance is less clear. Some modern secondary sources circulate spellings like "Ahoone," "Ahon," and "Ahome," but these cannot be traced to any 17th- or 18th-century manuscript. The baby-name site names.org claims spellings such as "Ahaone" and "Okee-Ahone" attributed to Captain John Smith, but this is demonstrably false — Smith never mentioned Ahone at all. The spellings "Ahonee" and "Ahoni" appear to be modern inventions, possibly arising from attempts to indicate pronunciation or from conflation with other Algonquian terms.
For the fearsome counterpart deity, the spelling record is richer: **Oke** (Smith, 1612), **Okee** (Smith, 1624; Beverley, 1705), **Okeus** (Strachey, c. 1612; Purchas, 1617/1625), **Quioccos** and **Kiwasa** (Beverley, 1705), and **Oki** in modern linguistic usage. The root *oki* appears across multiple Algonquian languages as a generic term for spirit or sacred power, which is why it has extensive attestation; "Ahone" has none of this cross-linguistic support.
### Goddard, Rountree, and the problem of Christian projection
Ives Goddard (1937–2025), the leading authority on Algonquian historical linguistics at the Smithsonian Institution, contributed extensively to the classification of Eastern Algonquian languages. His key publications include "Eastern Algonquian Languages" in the *Handbook of North American Indians*, Vol. 15 (1978) and "Comparative Algonquian" in *The Languages of Native America* (1979). Goddard and ethnohistorian Helen C. Rountree (Professor Emeritus, Old Dominion University) both argued that the colonial descriptions of Ahone versus Okee as "good god" versus "devil" were **gross oversimplifications that projected Christian dualism** onto Powhatan spirituality. Rountree's *The Powhatan Indians of Virginia: Their Traditional Culture* (University of Oklahoma Press, 1989) remains the standard scholarly treatment; its Chapter 8, "Medicine and Religion," examines the evidence critically. Some contemporary Virginia Algonquian people maintain that Ahone and Okeus may have been aspects of a single spiritual reality rather than opposed deities.
Andrew Lang remains the most sustained advocate for Ahone's authenticity as a genuinely indigenous concept. In Chapter 14 of *The Making of Religion*, he conceded he had "no explicit corroborative evidence for Ahone" beyond Strachey but argued strenuously that parallel creator-deity concepts across Eastern Algonquian peoples — particularly Kiehtan among the Wampanoag (recorded by Edward Winslow in 1622) and Cautantowwit among the Narragansett — demonstrated that "such a being as Ahone was within the capacity of the Indians in these latitudes."
### "Ahone" is linguistically isolated, with no Algonquian cognates
Despite conceptual parallels to creator figures across the Algonquian world, **the word "Ahone" itself has no known cognates in any other Algonquian language**. The pan-Algonquian term for sacred power is *manitou* (from Proto-Algonquian *\*maneto:wa*), appearing as Gitche Manitou in Ojibwe, Kise-manitow in Cree, and Aasha Monetoo in Shawnee. New England creator names — Kiehtan (Wampanoag), Cautantowwit (Narragansett), Ketanëtuwit (Lenape) — derive from Eastern Algonquian roots meaning "great" combined with "spirit/lord," but are phonetically unrelated to "Ahone." The Abenaki creator Tabaldak is equally distant. No Proto-Algonquian or Proto-Eastern Algonquian etymology for "Ahone" has been established in the published literature. The word's opacity may reflect the fragmentary nature of the Powhatan corpus — only **263 verified Algonquian words** out of Strachey's approximately 500 entries — or it may be a Powhatan-specific formation whose morphological structure is now irrecoverable.
### The Liahona question: no scholarly basis for a connection
No published scholar — in LDS apologetics, secular Book of Mormon source criticism, or Algonquian linguistics — has ever proposed a connection between "Ahone" and "Liahona." Every scholarly etymology of "Liahona" (Alma 37:38) analyzes it within a **Semitic linguistic framework**: Reynolds and Sjodahl (1959) parsed it as L ("to") + Yah ("Yahweh") + ʾOn (Egyptian Heliopolis); Jonathan Curci's influential 2007 analysis in the *Journal of Book of Mormon Studies* reads L ("to") + Yah ("the Lord") + ʿona (Hebrew "direction/whither"), yielding "The Direction of the Lord." The phonetic overlap with "Ahone" — the shared segment *-ahon-* — spans a morpheme boundary in the Hebrew analysis, where it results naturally from well-attested Semitic roots.
The **chronological barrier is decisive**. Strachey's *Historie*, the only text that names Ahone, existed only in manuscript until 1849. The Book of Mormon was published in 1830. Purchas's *Pilgrimes* (1625) drew on Strachey and Smith but does not clearly transmit the name "Ahone." Smith's widely available works never mention it. The standard source-criticism literature — Dan Vogel's *Indian Origins and the Book of Mormon* (1986), B.H. Roberts's posthumous *Studies of the Book of Mormon*, Fawn Brodie's *No Man Knows My History* (1945) — focuses on *View of the Hebrews* (Ethan Smith, 1823) and Mound Builder mythology as potential influences on the Book of Mormon, not on specific Algonquian deity names. No mechanism has been demonstrated by which Joseph Smith could have encountered the word "Ahone" before 1830.
### Conclusion
The pronunciation of "Ahone" is best approximated as **uh-HOH-neh** (/ʌˈhoːnɛ/), with the stress on the second syllable, based on Siebert's reconstructed Powhatan phonology and the language's syncope-based stress system. This reconstruction carries inherent uncertainty — the language has been extinct since approximately 1785, and the entire corpus rests on Strachey's imperfect transcriptions. The word is attested in a single source, has no known Algonquian cognates, and its Proto-Algonquian etymology remains unestablished. For the Book of Mormon source-criticism question specifically, three facts are dispositive: the sole primary source was unpublished until 1849; no other colonial text transmits the name; and the phonetic resemblance to "Liahona" is superficial, coinciding with a morpheme boundary in well-established Hebrew analysis. The resemblance between unrelated words across unrelated languages is common — it does not, by itself, constitute evidence of borrowing or contact.
---
## The li + Ahone = Liahona Hypothesis: Source Accessibility Analysis
**Neither component of the proposed "Liahona" etymology was available in published form before 1830.** The Lenape particle "li" (to, thither) first appeared in print in 1888, and the Powhatan deity name "Ahone" was confined to William Strachey's unpublished manuscript until 1849. The claim that these terms circulated in "the most basic colonial literature" collapses under documentary scrutiny. Robert Beverley's 1705 *History and Present State of Virginia* does not contain the word "Ahone" anywhere in its text, and no comprehensive Lenape vocabulary was published before the 1880s. For the Spalding-Rigdon framework, this means the accessibility chain is broken twice over: a literate American before 1830 could not have encountered either term in any published source.
---
### Zeisberger's dictionary existed only in manuscript until 1887
David Zeisberger's Lenape linguistic work produced two distinct published editions, **both decades after 1830**. The multilingual *Zeisberger's Indian Dictionary* (English-German-Onondaga-Delaware) was edited by Eben Norton Horsford and published in 1887 from a manuscript held at Harvard College Library. The alphabetical *Lenâpé-English Dictionary*, edited by Daniel G. Brinton and Albert Seqaqkind Anthony, was published by the Historical Society of Pennsylvania in **1888** from an anonymous manuscript in the Moravian Church archives at Bethlehem, Pennsylvania.
The claim that Zeisberger's dictionary "circulated widely in manuscript" overstates the evidence considerably. Before 1830, access was limited to a narrow circle of institutional scholars. The Harvard manuscript came from a donation by Judge Lane and sat in the college library. The Bethlehem manuscript remained in Moravian Church archives. Peter Stephen Du Ponceau, secretary of the American Philosophical Society's Historical and Literary Committee, borrowed Zeisberger's German-language grammar manuscript from John Heckewelder around 1816 and translated it into English. This grammar — focused on verb morphology, not vocabulary — was published separately in **1827** by J. Kay, Jr. in Philadelphia, making it the only Zeisberger linguistic work available in print before 1830.
Du Ponceau himself noted he "had no idea at the time that this grammar would ever be published." The grammar manuscript was returned to the Bethlehem library after translation. The dictionary manuscripts were never made available to the general public. Access required physical visits to church or university archives and personal connections to the small circle of APS-affiliated scholars in Philadelphia.
### The Lenape particle "li" was first published in 1888
The entry **"Li, to; thither"** appears in the Brinton 1888 *Lenâpé-English Dictionary* as a standalone headword, falling between "Leu" (true, it is so) and "Liechenan" (to leave out). Critically, this entry carries **no source attribution letter** — it lacks the "Z." (Zeisberger), "H." (Heckewelder), or "E." (Ettwein) markings that Brinton used for entries drawn from specific named sources. This means "li" appeared in the anonymous base manuscript, likely compiled by Rev. C.F. Dencke (died 1839) and physically copied by Rev. Kampman in the early 1840s.
Grammatically, "li" appears to function as a **directional particle** with both free and bound uses. Internal dictionary evidence shows it operating as a suffix in compound forms: "ika" (there, yonder) plus "-li" yields "ikali" (thither), with extended forms "ikalissi" (further, still further) and "ikalitti" (a little further). Anthony's Minsi dialect equivalent "nellakli'" also ends in "-li." No modern academic grammar of Lenape/Unami has been found that specifically analyzes this morpheme, and all online Lenape dictionaries listing "li" derive from the Brinton 1888 text.
**No published source before 1830 documents "li" as a standalone entry.** The pre-1830 published Lenape sources are remarkably sparse:
- Zeisberger's *Delaware-Indian and English Spelling Book* (1776, 1806) — elementary school texts for mission children
- Heckewelder's "Words, Phrases, and Short Dialogues" (1819) — **only 12 pages** of practical conversational phrases, not individual particle entries
- The Du Ponceau-Heckewelder "Correspondence respecting the languages of the American Indians" (1819) — linguistic theory with scattered vocabulary examples, not systematic word lists
- Zeisberger's *Grammar* (1827) — verb morphology and conjugation paradigms, not a vocabulary of individual particles
None of these publications systematically catalogs directional particles. The comprehensive vocabulary listings that would include "li" remained locked in manuscript archives until the 1880s.
### Beverley's 1705 text does not contain the word "Ahone"
This is the most consequential finding. A full-text search of Robert Beverley's *The History and Present State of Virginia* (1705/1722), accessed via the Project Gutenberg digitization (ebook #32721), confirms that **the word "Ahone" appears nowhere in the book**. In Book III, Chapter VIII ("Religion, Worship and Superstitious Customs of the Indians"), Beverley discusses Powhatan religion extensively, using three deity names: **"Okee"** (his primary term, used repeatedly), **"Quioccos,"** and **"Kiwasa."** A plate caption reads: "Idol called, OKEE, QUIOCCOS, or KIWASA."
Beverley does describe a concept of a benevolent deity abstractly, noting the Indians believed in a "good God" who required no worship and an "evil" one they tried to appease. But he never supplies the Powhatan name for this benevolent figure. His religious vocabulary is limited to: Okee, Quioccos, Kiwasa, quioccassan (temple/shrine), pawcorances (altar stones), pawwawing (conjuration), and huskanawing. The word "Ahone" is simply absent.
This matters enormously because the claim that "Ahone was popularized through Beverley's 1705 text" is demonstrably false.
