# Baron de Lahontan and the Book of Mormon's Liahona: Evidence for a linguistic connection
The phonetic and conceptual parallels between Baron de Lahontan (1666-c.1716), the French explorer of New France, and the Book of Mormon's "Liahona" and "Lehonti" represent a compelling but previously unstudied transmission pathway. Documentary evidence demonstrates that Lahontan's name circulated widely in early America through multiple channels, his works described compasses as spiritually animated devices in Native American perception, and the phonetic structures of his name closely match otherwise unexplained Book of Mormon terms. While no direct citation of Lahontan appears in standard Book of Mormon source texts, the circumstantial evidence merits serious scholarly attention.
## Documented spelling variants show significant phonetic fluidity
The Dictionary of Canadian Biography records multiple historical variants of Lahontan's name: **"Lahontan, Lahontang, La Hontan, La Hontaa,"** and "Lahonton." This variation demonstrates how the French name was heard and reproduced by English speakers unfamiliar with French nasal vowels. The variant "Lahontang" (with terminal "g") reveals English speakers heard additional consonantal sounds from French nasalization, while "La Hontaa" shows vowel elongation in the final syllable.
French nasal vowels presented significant challenges for early American English speakers. The name "La Hontan" contains two nasalized sequences: the French /ɔ̃/ in "Hon-" and /ɑ̃/ in "-tan." Period pronunciation guides from the Evans Early American Imprint Collection show Americans were taught that French "m in the End of Words is pronounced like n" and that certain consonants were silent—patterns that would produce unpredictable English renderings. An English speaker might reproduce "La Hontan" as "Lah-hon-tan," "La-hon-tain," or "Lay-hon-tan," with the initial "La" potentially heard as "Le" or even "Lia" depending on regional accent.
## The Lehonti-Lahontan parallel is phonetically striking
The Book of Mormon character **Lehonti** (Alma 47), a Lamanite officer poisoned by Amalickiah, presents a remarkable phonetic match to "Lahontan." The BYU Book of Mormon Onomasticon explicitly states: **"No etymology immediately suggests itself"** for this name. More significantly, the Onomasticon records **"Lahonti"** as a variant spelling—differing from "Lahontan" by only a single vowel in the final syllable.
The phonetic comparison is instructive:
| Name | Structure | Pronunciation |
|------|-----------|---------------|
| Lehonti/Lahonti | Le-HON-ti | /liːˈhɒntaɪ/ |
| Lahontan | La-HON-tan | /laˈhɒntɑn/ |
Both names share identical core syllables: **L-HON-T**, with nearly identical stress patterns. The only differences are minor vowel variations in the first syllable (Le/La) and final syllable (ti/tan)—precisely the kind of variation documented in historical renderings of Lahontan's name. No Hebrew, Egyptian, or ancient Near Eastern etymology has been proposed for Lehonti, leaving this phonetic parallel unexplained in standard scholarly literature.
## The Liahona-Lahontan connection centers on the "-ahon-" core
The Liahona itself—the brass directional device described in the Book of Mormon—contains the phonetic core **"-ahon-"** that appears prominently in "Lahontan." LDS scholars have proposed various Hebrew/Egyptian etymologies (l- + Yāh + ôna for "direction of the Lord"), but these reconstructions remain speculative. The documented phonetic fluidity in how "Lahontan" was rendered in English opens alternative possibilities.
Both "Liahona" and "Lahontan" share:
- Initial "L-" sound
- Central "-ahon-" syllable cluster
- Terminal vowel sounds (-a/-an)
This phonetic overlap has not been examined in either apologetic or critical Book of Mormon scholarship, representing a significant gap in the literature.
## Lahontan specifically described compasses as spiritually animated
The most consequential finding for the Liahona parallel is a passage from Lahontan's July 4, 1695 letter, published in his *Voyages* (1703). On page 906 of the Thwaites edition, Lahontan wrote:
> "The Savages believe that a Watch, a **Compass**, and a thousand other Machines are **moved by Spirits**; for your ignorant and clownish People form extravagant Ideas of every thing that surpasses their Imagination."
This passage explicitly connects: (1) a brass navigational instrument (compass), (2) spiritual/divine animation, and (3) Native American perception of European technology—the precise conceptual elements that define the Liahona in the Book of Mormon. Lahontan further described how Natives viewed his graphometer (surveying instrument) as **"somewhat Divine,"** being unable to understand how distances could be measured "without there were some Supernatural Assistance."
The Book of Mormon's Liahona is described as:
- A "round ball of curious workmanship" made of "fine brass"
- Having "two spindles" that pointed directions
- Functioning "according to faith and diligence"
- Providing spiritual guidance through written messages
The thematic parallel is precise: both Lahontan's compass passage and the Liahona narrative present brass navigational devices whose operation is attributed to spiritual or divine forces rather than mechanical principles.
## Multiple transmission pathways brought Lahontan to early America
While no direct citation of Lahontan appears in proposed Book of Mormon source texts like *View of the Hebrews* (1823) or *American Antiquities* (1833), multiple transmission pathways existed:
**Jonathan Carver's Travels (1778)** had an American edition published in Philadelphia in 1784 and "directly drew from Lahontan," borrowing vocabulary lists and ethnographic descriptions. Carver's work became "the first popular American travel book and an international bestseller" with over 30 editions, making it a major conduit for Lahontan's material reaching American audiences. A scholarly bibliography notes that Carver's work was "drawn in part from works by Hennepin, **Lahontan**, Charlevoix, and Adair."
**Thomas Jefferson owned, recommended, and actively loaned Lahontan's works.** The Library of Congress documents that when historian Abiel Holmes was preparing *American Annals* (1805), "Jefferson suggested several sources that would assist him and eventually loaned Holmes his copy of *Memoires de l'Amerique* by the French explorer Baron de Lahontan when Holmes could not find the book." Jefferson had the second English edition (London, 1735) and recommended Lahontan as "a particularly useful species of reading."
**Lahontan's Long River appeared on maps until 1785**, maintaining geographic awareness of his name despite Charlevoix's 1744 discrediting. The University of Virginia's Lewis & Clark exhibit confirms this cartographic presence in American geographic resources.
## The Adario dialogues parallel primitivist religious themes
Lahontan's *Dialogues Curieux* (1703), featuring the Huron chief Kondiaronk (called "Adario"), presents thematic parallels with the religious primitivism that characterized Joseph Smith's environment. Adario articulates dependence "only on the Great Spirit," criticizes European Christianity through systematic philosophical arguments, and presents indigenous peoples as possessing ancient spiritual wisdom superior to corrupted European religion.
Scholarly consensus positions Lahontan as foundational to the "noble savage" concept. Jonathan Israel in *Radical Enlightenment* (2001) contends Lahontan's works "were indeed the most widely read descriptions of native Americans of the first half of the eighteenth century." Anthony Pagden declares that "almost every bon sauvage of Canadian origin created by succeeding writers owes something, and frequently everything, to Adario."
These Enlightenment concepts filtered into American religious thought through Rousseau, Voltaire, and ultimately into the Second Great Awakening's primitivist impulse. As Wikipedia notes: "The ideal of restoring a 'primitive' form of Christianity grew in popularity in the United States after the American Revolution" and "played a role in the development of many groups during this period...including the Mormons."
## Lahontan's name survived discrediting and persisted in American memory
Despite Charlevoix's 1744 labeling of Lahontan as "ignorant et menteur" (ignorant and liar) for his likely fabricated Long River account, Lahontan's name remained known. Chateaubriand exemplifies this contradictory reception: publicly condemning Lahontan in *Génie du Christianisme* while extensively using his material in *Les Natchez* (1826), including naming a character "Adario" and drawing all Indian character names from "Lahontan's Algonkian glossary."
The name's persistence culminated in geologist Clarence King naming **Lake Lahontan** (the prehistoric Great Basin lake) in the 1860s during the Geological Exploration of the Fortieth Parallel. The California Lahontan Regional Water Quality Control Board notes: "The name Lahontan will be familiar to those of French and Canadian descent." Related features—Lahontan Valley, Lahontan Reservoir, Lahontan cutthroat trout—further embedded the name in American geography.
## Western New York library evidence remains incomplete
Research on library holdings near Joseph Smith produced limited but suggestive findings. Historian Erich Robert Paul's 1982 study documented that the Manchester Library held Josiah Priest's *American Antiquities* (1833), but **no direct evidence of Lahontan's works** in the specific libraries of Manchester, Palmyra, or Canandaigua was found. However, the Phinney Brothers' book distribution network—which operated a canal boat bookstore on the Erie Canal after 1825 and "imported all sorts of books from New York and Philadelphia"—significantly expanded book access in western New York. E.B. Grandin's bookstore in Palmyra, where the Smiths purchased their 1828 Bible, provided another channel for book circulation.
The broader context is that French exploration literature circulated through American publishing channels, Carver's Travels was widely available, and Jefferson's active recommendation of Lahontan demonstrates elite American awareness of these works.
## The scholarly gap is significant
No academic study—apologetic or critical—has examined French colonial naming patterns as potential sources for Book of Mormon nomenclature. The BYU Onomasticon's statement that "No etymology immediately suggests itself" for Lehonti, combined with its recording of "Lahonti" as a variant spelling, suggests this parallel has gone entirely unnoticed. Similarly, no scholarly analysis connects Lahontan's compass passage to the Liahona's conceptual framework.
This research gap is notable given:
- The phonetic near-identity of Lehonti/Lahonti and Lahontan
- The shared "-ahon-" core between Liahona and Lahontan
- Lahontan's explicit description of compasses as spiritually animated
- Multiple documented transmission pathways to early America
- Lahontan's influence on primitivist religious thought
## Conclusion: A circumstantial case requiring further investigation
The evidence does not establish direct borrowing but constructs a compelling circumstantial case: Baron de Lahontan's name circulated in early America through multiple channels; his spelling varied significantly in English renderings; he explicitly described compasses as devices Natives believed "moved by Spirits"; two Book of Mormon terms (Liahona, Lehonti) share striking phonetic similarities with his name that lack alternative etymologies; and his broader influence on primitivist religious thought permeated the intellectual environment of the Second Great Awakening.
Whether these parallels represent coincidental similarity, unconscious linguistic borrowing, or deliberate appropriation cannot be determined from current evidence. What is clear is that the Lahontan-Liahona connection represents an unstudied scholarly question deserving systematic comparative analysis—one that critical Book of Mormon scholarship has thus far entirely overlooked.
THE BRASS DIRECTOR
Research Update: Session 2
Irish Mythology, Fenian Cycle, and Extended Source Network
Executive Summary
This research session has uncovered a significant new category of potential Book of Mormon sources: Irish legendary material, accessible through multiple English translations over a century before 1830. Combined with the previously documented French colonial and classical sources, this reveals an integrated literary network converging on the Liahona concept with remarkable phonetic and thematic precision.
Key Discoveries:
1. FENIAN CYCLE parallels including 'Lia' (keeper of magical Treasure Bag) and 'Lia Fáil' (oracular Stone of Destiny)
2. HENNEPIN Chapter 55 confirmed: 'They admire his Compass, and an Iron-Pot which he has' (1698)
3. MACPHERSON'S OSSIAN (1760-1765) made Irish mythology widely accessible 70 years before the Book of Mormon
4. KEATING'S HISTORY OF IRELAND English translation (1723) predates Book of Mormon by over a century
5. Multiple 'Lia-' phonetic elements in Irish tradition paralleling 'Liahona'
Section 1: Fenian Cycle Discoveries
1.1 Lia - Keeper of the Treasure Bag
A character named LIA appears prominently in the Fenian Cycle as the Treasurer of the Fianna who guards the magical Treasure Bag (Corrbolg or Crane Bag). This bag, made from crane skin, contained sacred objects from the Tuatha Dé Danann including a shield, a knife, and other magical implements. The bag would only reveal its contents when the tide was high.
Significance: The character 'Lia' is associated with a sacred object containing divine guidance materials, paralleling the Liahona's function as a sacred container with directional spindles.
1.2 Lia Fáil - The Stone of Destiny
The Lia Fáil (pronounced 'LEE-ah FOYL') is the Stone of Destiny brought to Ireland by the Tuatha Dé Danann from the otherworldly city of Falias. This oracular stone 'cries out' or 'speaks' when the rightful king touches it, serving as a divine communication device.

Section 2: Accessibility Before 1830
2.1 Macpherson's Ossian (1760-1765)
James Macpherson published his Ossian poems beginning in 1760, drawing on (and significantly embellishing) the Fenian Cycle of Irish and Scottish Gaelic tradition. Despite controversy over authenticity, these works were 'internationally popular, translated into all the literary languages of Europe' and 'highly influential both in the development of the Romantic movement and the Gaelic revival.'
The Ossian poems introduced English readers to the world of Fionn mac Cumhaill (Fingal), Oisín (Ossian), the Fianna warriors, and their adventures - including elements of the magical objects and sacred traditions that appear in the original Fenian sources.
2.2 Keating's History of Ireland (1723 English)
Geoffrey Keating's Foras Feasa ar Éirinn (c. 1634) was the most influential Gaelic historical work, drawing on the Lebor Gabála Érenn and other sources. The 1723 English translation by Dermod O'Connor made this material accessible to English readers over a century before the Book of Mormon.
Keating's work provided detailed accounts of: the succession of Irish 'invasions' (Cessair, Partholón, Nemed, Fir Bolg, Tuatha Dé Danann, Milesians); the Four Treasures of the Tuatha Dé Danann including Lia Fáil; and extensive legendary genealogies.
2.3 Irish Source Accessibility Timeline

Section 3: French Colonial Sources Confirmed
3.1 Hennepin Chapter 55 (1698)
The chapter heading from Hennepin's A New Discovery of a Vast Country in America has been confirmed: 'CHAP. LV. The Author is like to be famish'd. They admire his Compass, and an Iron-Pot which he has.'
This confirms the compass-as-object-of-wonder theme in French colonial literature, paralleling Lahontan's 'moved by Spirits' compass passage. Both Hennepin (1698) and Lahontan (1703) were authoritative European sources on Native Americans available in English translations.
3.2 Charlevoix (1744)
Pierre-François-Xavier de Charlevoix's Histoire et Description Générale de la Nouvelle France (1744) represents another major French colonial source, described as 'the most comprehensive book on the history and geography of the French colony' and 'an invaluable source of contemporary information regarding indigenous tribes.'
English translations appeared by 1766 (Dublin), adding another layer to the French colonial literature available before 1830.

Section 4: Solomon Spalding - Dartmouth Classical Education
Solomon Spalding (1761-1816) enrolled at Dartmouth College in 1782 and graduated in 1785. His education included 'classical studies typical for aspiring scholars of the era' with 'theology, languages, and rhetoric under the college's rigorous curriculum.'
Significance: Spalding's Manuscript Found incorporated 'elements of Roman history,' demonstrating classical literacy. The Dartmouth classical curriculum of the 1780s would have exposed students to standard classical texts including Virgil's Aeneid - the source of the twin doves guiding to the golden bough passage that parallels the Liahona's twin spindles.
This establishes that classically educated individuals in early America had direct access to the Aeneid and potentially its Greek precursor, Apollonius's Argonautica (dove-navigator motif), through the English translations available by 1780-1803.

5.2 Thematic Convergence
The integrated source network reveals remarkable thematic convergence on the Liahona concept:
FRENCH COLONIAL: Compasses as objects of spiritual wonder among Native Americans (Lahontan, Hennepin, Charlevoix)
CLASSICAL: Divine bird/object guides travelers to promised destination (Argonautica dove, Aeneid twin doves)
IRISH: Oracular stone/object brought from otherworld that communicates divine guidance (Lia Fáil, Treasure Bag of Lia)
BOOK OF MORMON: Spiritually-animated brass compass with twin spindles guiding to promised land

Section 6: Confirmed Research Gaps
No existing scholarship (critical or apologetic) has examined:
1. The phonetic similarity between 'Lahontan' and 'Liahona/Lahonti/Lehonti'
2. Lahontan's 'moved by Spirits' compass passage in relation to the Liahona
3. The 'Lia Fáil' / 'Liahona' phonetic and functional parallel
4. The Fenian Cycle's 'Lia' (Treasurer with magical bag) as potential influence
5. Irish legendary material as a systematic category of Book of Mormon sources
6. The Argonautica-Aeneid-BoM dove/navigation literary chain
7. French colonial literature as a systematic category of BoM sources
6.1 Note on 'Alma son of Nemed' Claim
The MormonThink documentation claims that 'One of Nemed's four sons was actually named Alma' in the Lebor Gabála. However, standard scholarly sources consistently list Nemed's four sons as: Starn, Iarbonel (also 'Iarbonel the Prophet'), Annind (or Ainnin), and Fergus Red-Side.
This claim requires verification against primary sources. If accurate, it would represent yet another Irish-BoM name parallel predating the apologetic Bar Kokhba letter citation for 'Alma' as a male name.
Conclusion
This research session has significantly expanded the documented source network for Book of Mormon literary origins. The Irish legendary material - accessible through Keating (1723) and Macpherson (1760) - adds an entirely new dimension to source criticism that has been systematically overlooked for nearly two centuries.
The convergence of phonetic evidence ('Lia-' prefix in Irish, 'Lahontan' in French colonial) with thematic parallels (oracular objects, divine guidance, sacred containers) suggests a sophisticated synthesis of multiple literary traditions rather than simple plagiarism from any single source.
The 190+ year gap in scholarly attention to these sources - across both critical and apologetic traditions - represents a significant opportunity for original contribution to the field of Book of Mormon studies.
Document prepared for The Brass Director research project
Session 2: January 29, 2026# Book of Mormon source criticism: An unstudied connection and the state of secular scholarship
The phonetic similarity between "Lahontan" and "Liahona" has never been noted by any scholar in any field—a significant finding given the extensive critical literature on Book of Mormon origins. This research report synthesizes findings on early American library holdings, the Pittsburgh intellectual environment of 1810-1815, critical scholarship on the Liahona, Solomon Spalding's intellectual formation, and the scholarly assessment of the "Manuscript Found" theory. The evidence reveals substantial gaps in the critical literature, particularly regarding French colonial sources, while confirming the scholarly rejection of the Spalding theory based on chronological impossibilities and documentary failures.
---
## The Lahontan-Liahona connection remains unexplored in all scholarship
**No scholar has ever noted the phonetic similarity between "Lahontan" and "Liahona."** After extensive searching across academic databases, the BYU Book of Mormon Onomasticon (which catalogs all proposed etymologies), critical scholarship from Signature Books, and apologetic literature, this connection appears completely unstudied. The BYU Onomasticon lists five proposed etymologies for "Liahona"—all based on Hebrew elements—with no French, colonial American, or 19th-century literary sources mentioned.
The phonetic comparison is notable: both words share four-syllable structures with common elements ("La/Li" + "hon/ho" + terminal syllable). Baron de Lahontan (1666-1716) published *Nouveaux Voyages dans l'Amérique Septentrionale* in 1703, a work enormously influential on Enlightenment thinkers including Voltaire, Rousseau, Swift, and Diderot. His extensive descriptions of Native Americans and philosophical dialogues featuring the Huron character "Adario" shaped European thought about indigenous peoples for over a century.
Critical scholars examining 19th-century sources for Book of Mormon names have focused almost exclusively on British and American texts: *View of the Hebrews* (Ethan Smith, 1823), *The Late War* (Gilbert Hunt, 1816), the KJV Bible, and Bunyan's *Pilgrim's Progress*. Dan Vogel's comprehensive *Indian Origins and the Book of Mormon* (1986) examines colonial-era sources but focuses on British sources and mound-builder theories—**not French colonial literature**. This gap is striking given Lahontan's documented influence on American intellectual culture.
---
## Lahontan's circulation in early American libraries cannot be confirmed but was plausible
Direct documentary evidence of Lahontan's works in early American library catalogs from 1780-1830 was not located, but contextual evidence suggests his writings would have been available. The Library Company of Philadelphia's 1741 catalog shows that **voyages and travels represented one-third of collection holdings**—the library "collected energetically" in this category throughout its history. The New York Society Library's City Readers database contains over 100,000 circulation transactions from 1789-1805 that could be searched directly.
Lahontan's works went through **over 20 editions** between 1703-1741 in French, English, Dutch, and German. The first English translation appeared in London in 1703, with a second edition in 1735. Yale's Beinecke Library holds the 1703 English edition. Comparable French exploration narratives known to circulate in American libraries included Father Louis Hennepin's *A New Discovery of a Vast Country in America* (1698) and Pierre-François-Xavier de Charlevoix's histories of New France.
The absence of Lahontan from Book of Mormon source criticism suggests this connection has never been systematically investigated. Whether the phonetic similarity is coincidental or represents an unstudied potential influence requires original scholarly investigation that has not yet occurred.
---
## The Pittsburgh intellectual environment reveals fatal chronological problems for the Spalding theory
The documentary evidence from Pittsburgh 1810-1815 creates insurmountable chronological difficulties for the Spalding-Rigdon theory of Book of Mormon origins. **The firm "Patterson & Lambdin" did not exist until January 1, 1818**—four years after the alleged events and two years after Solomon Spalding's death.
During 1812-1814 (when Spalding allegedly submitted his manuscript), Robert Patterson operated **a bookselling business, not a printing operation**. The relevant timeline shows:
- Patterson & Hopkins Booksellers dissolved November 5, 1812
- Replaced by R. & J. Patterson Book and Stationery Store
- Printing functions performed separately by Silas Engles or Butler & Lambdin
- Patterson & Lambdin as a combined publishing entity formed **January 1, 1818**
The major printing operations in Pittsburgh during 1810-1815 were John Scull's Pittsburgh Gazette (established 1786) and Zadok Cramer's Cramer, Spear & Eichbaum (1800-1814), which published the first Lewis & Clark expedition account and operated a circulating library with up to **2,000 volumes**. The Pittsburgh Permanent Library Company maintained about 2,000 volumes housed in the Court House by 1815.
Robert Patterson himself, when questioned, wrote that "he had no recollection of any manuscript being brought there for publication" and would not likely have seen it since "the business of printing was conducted wholly by Lambdin at that time." E.D. Howe noted that Patterson "affirmed his entire ignorance of the whole matter." Sidney Rigdon's documented residence in Pittsburgh began only in **1822**—six years after Spalding's death—when he arrived as a Baptist minister.
---
## Critical scholarship contextualizes the Liahona within early American folk magic
The Liahona—described in the Book of Mormon as a brass sphere with two spindles that pointed direction according to the user's faith—has received limited direct academic treatment but fits within the extensively documented folk magic traditions of Joseph Smith's era.
**D. Michael Quinn's *Early Mormonism and the Magic World View*** (1987, revised 1998) is the foundational text on magical devices in early Mormonism. This Yale-trained historian documented that Smith's environment was permeated with folk magic including divining rods, seer stones, and treasure-seeking practices. Oliver Cowdery was explicitly told in an early revelation that his "rod" (later changed to "gift of Aaron") had divine power. Quinn demonstrated that "divining rods were widely used to find locations for wells" throughout the American frontier.
**John L. Brooke's *The Refiner's Fire*** (Cambridge University Press, 1994, Bancroft Prize winner) traced connections between European hermetic traditions and Mormon theology, documenting the "cunning folk" traditions that influenced early Mormonism. Brooke argued Mormon cosmology originated from "the fusion of radical religion with occult ideas."
The Liahona parallels ancient divinatory practices, particularly **belomancy** (divination by arrows) documented in the ancient Near East. A.W. Buckland's 1876 article in the *Journal of the Anthropological Institute of Great Britain and Ireland* described how Babylonians, Greeks, Arabs, and Scythians used marked arrows to determine divine will—functionally similar to the Liahona's direction-finding through divine/magical means.
Critical scholars have not produced substantive non-LDS linguistic critiques specifically addressing "Liahona" etymology. Critics focus instead on broader linguistic anachronisms: Christ, Messiah, synagogue, and church appearing centuries before they historically existed. **No systematic secular literary analysis of the Liahona as a narrative device exists in the critical literature**—a notable gap.
---
## Solomon Spalding's classical education shaped his mound builder fiction
Spalding (1761-1816) received an excellent classical education typical of aspiring New England ministers. He attended Plainfield Academy (founded 1770 in Connecticut, preparing students for Yale), graduated Dartmouth College in 1785 with an A.M., read law with Judge Zephaniah Swift, and became a licentiate of the Windham Congregational Association in 1787.
The Dartmouth curriculum during Spalding's attendance included Latin, Greek, and Hebrew languages; theology and divinity; rhetoric; history; and natural philosophy. The 1786 commencement featured "oration and debate in Greek, Latin, and Hebrew." Over a quarter of graduates pursued religious vocations. An educated New Englander of Spalding's generation studied Cicero, Virgil, Horace, Ovid, Livy, Sallust, Homer, Xenophon, and the Greek New Testament.
Spalding was reportedly "one of the first to speculate and write on the Mississippi Valley earthmounds," and "shared the widespread belief in the Hebrew origins of the Indians as descendants of the Lost Tribes." Works available to his generation included James Adair's *History of the American Indians* (1775), which compared Native American and Jewish customs, and Thomas Jefferson's *Notes on the State of Virginia* (1785).
**No inventory or catalog of Spalding's personal library survives.** No reading journals or commonplace books have been documented. The specific texts he consulted for his fiction cannot be definitively established—only inferred from his educational context and the content of his surviving manuscript.
---
## The Oberlin Manuscript bears no resemblance to the Book of Mormon
The only surviving Spalding manuscript—held at Oberlin College since 1885—tells of Romans in the 4th century AD who, sailing to Britain, are blown off course and land in eastern North America. The 175-page unfinished manuscript (written 1809-1812) describes two rival nations, the Kentuck and Scotian, and their eventual mutual destruction. Professor Azariah S. Root of Oberlin College wrote in 1927 that the manuscript "seems pretty clearly not to have been the manuscript from which the Book of Mormon was written."
Fawn Brodie's *No Man Knows My History* (1945) decisively rejected the Spalding theory. She noted that when the manuscript was recovered: "Now to his bitter chagrin [Hurlburt] found that the long chase had been vain; for while the romance did concern the ancestors of the Indians, its resemblance to the Book of Mormon ended there. None of the names found in one could be identified in the other."
The "lost manuscript" hypothesis—that Spalding wrote a separate, more elaborate text in biblical phraseology with names like Nephi and Lehi—relies entirely on witness affidavits collected **20+ years after Spalding's death**. No physical evidence of such a manuscript exists. James H. Fairchild of Oberlin College declared in 1885: "There is no foundation for the statement...that Spaulding made another story, more elaborate, of which several copies were written."
Spalding wrote within the well-documented **mound builder literary tradition** described by Robert Silverberg in *Mound Builders of Ancient America: The Archaeology of a Myth* (1968). This tradition attributed the earthworks to Lost Tribes of Israel, Phoenicians, Vikings, Romans, Welsh settlers, Atlanteans, Egyptians, or an extinct race of white-skinned giants. Jason Colavito's *The Mound Builder Myth* (University of Oklahoma Press, 2020) chronicles how the myth served racist and colonial purposes.
---
## Scholarly consensus has rejected the Spalding theory since 1946
The Encyclopedia of Mormonism's academic assessment states: "Since 1946, no serious student of Mormonism has given the Spaulding Manuscript theory much credibility." This consensus includes scholars hostile to LDS truth claims.
Jerald and Sandra Tanner (1977), prominent critics of Mormonism, found the theory unworkable. Daniel C. Peterson calls it "deeply improbable" with "little or no evidence." Most historians—"whether friendly or hostile to the truth claims of the LDS Church—[do not] believe that the historical data support the Spalding manuscript hypothesis."
Later attempts to revive the theory have failed scholarly scrutiny. A 2008 Stanford computational linguistics study claiming Sidney Rigdon was "the main architect" was critiqued by G. Bruce Schaalje et al. in the *Journal of Literary and Linguistic Computing*, who demonstrated the methodology would also falsely attribute the Federalist Papers to Mormon writers.
Critical scholars have instead focused on **Ethan Smith's *View of the Hebrews*** (1823, 1825) as a potential environmental influence. B.H. Roberts listed 18 points of parallel between the texts in his *Studies of the Book of Mormon* (1922, published 1985). However, Dan Vogel's *Indian Origins and the Book of Mormon* (Signature Books, 1986) argues the Book of Mormon should be understood within the broader "literary and folkloric environment" reflecting "common nineteenth-century assumptions" rather than requiring any specific source.
---
## Conclusion: Significant gaps persist in the critical literature
This research reveals several notable findings for scholars of Book of Mormon source criticism:
The **Lahontan-Liahona phonetic connection** represents an unstudied observation that may warrant original investigation. No critical or apologetic scholar has examined French colonial literature as potential source material—a striking omission given Lahontan's documented influence on Enlightenment thought about indigenous Americans.
The **Pittsburgh chronology** definitively undermines the Spalding-Rigdon theory: Patterson & Lambdin did not exist as a printing firm until 1818, and Rigdon's documented presence in Pittsburgh began only in 1822.
Critical scholarship on the **Liahona specifically** remains thin. While Quinn and Brooke thoroughly documented the folk magic context, no systematic literary analysis of the device as a narrative element exists outside apologetic literature.
The **scholarly consensus since 1946** has rejected direct Spalding influence while acknowledging that Spalding, Joseph Smith, and their contemporaries drew on shared cultural materials—mound builder speculation, lost tribes theories, and romantic interest in ancient American civilizations that pervaded early republic intellectual life.
Future research might productively examine: French colonial literature's actual circulation in early American libraries (requiring direct archival investigation); the Lahontan-Liahona phonetic question; and comparative analysis of direction-finding devices in 18th-19th century fiction and folklore.
